# Spiritual Ethos

*Justice and Remembrance — Reza Shah-Kazemi. Chapters one to three, with both primary texts from the appendices.*

## Introducing Ali (ع) and his Spiritual Ethos

I am the city of knowledge and Ali (ع) is its gate; so whoever desires knowledge, let him enter the gate. (Ana madinatu'l-ilm wa 'Ali babuha; Fa-man arada'l-ilm fal-ya'ti'l-bab).

<!-- page 1 -->

To speak of Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع) — cousin and son-in-law of the Prophet Muhammad, fourth caliph of Islam and first in the line of Shi'i Imams — is to speak of the quintessential spirituality of the Islamic tradition. In this seminal figure of nascent Islam one finds an integral expression of the two fundamental sources of Islamic spirituality: the Qur'anic revelation and the inspired Sunna of the Prophet. By Sunna we do not mean simply the outward imitation of the Prophet's actions — a reductionism all too prevalent in our times — but rather the spiritual substance of prophetic perfection to which the Qur'an itself points: وَإِنَّكَ لَعَلَىٰ خُلُقٍ عَظِيمٍۢ — 'Verily, thou art of a tremendous nature' (68: 4). The Prophet's words and deeds express that spiritual substance but do not exhaust it. To inwardly assimilate this substance, rather than merely imitate its outward forms, is the goal of every spiritually inclined Muslim. There is a profound affinity between the believer's soul and the prophetic nature — a mysterious proximity that transcends time and space — as expressed in the verse: ٱلنَّبِىُّ أَوْلَىٰ بِٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ مِنْ أَنفُسِهِمْ ۖ وَأَزْوَٰجُهُۥٓ أُمَّهَٰتُهُمْ ۗ وَأُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْأَرْحَامِ بَعْضُهُمْ أَوْلَىٰ بِبَعْضٍۢ فِى كِتَٰبِ ٱللَّهِ مِنَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُهَٰجِرِينَ إِلَّآ أَن تَفْعَلُوٓا۟ إِلَىٰٓ أَوْلِيَآئِكُم مَّعْرُوفًۭا ۚ كَانَ ذَٰلِكَ فِى ٱلْكِتَٰبِ مَسْطُورًۭا: 'The Prophet is closer to the believers than they are to themselves, and his wives are their mothers; and by the ordinance of God, blood relatives are more entitled to one another than other believers and emigrants, unless you would show kindness to your close associates — this is decreed in the Record' (33: 6). The Prophet thus outwardly manifests the perfection that the spiritually sensitive believer intuits, to some degree or another, as defining the very essence of his or her own soul.

It is not surprising, therefore, to find the Prophet saying that Ali (ع) 'is as my own soul (ka-nafsi)' — عليٌّ مِنِّي بِمَنْزِلَةِ نَفْسِي — affirming not only the extraordinarily close bond between Ali (ع) and himself, but also the affinity — and ultimately the identity — between every sanctified soul and the prophetic nature. Even more mysteriously, he said to Ali (ع), 'You are from me, and I am from you (anta minnī wa ana minka)' — أنْتَ مِنِّی وَ أَنَا مِنْکَ. As for Ali (ع)'s assimilation of the substance (⟪ar:مواد⟫) of the Qur'an, we have this prophetic saying: إِنَّ عَلِيًّا مَعَ الْقُرْآنِ وَالْقُرْآنُ مَعَ عَلِيٍّ، لَنْ يَفْتَرِقَا حَتَّى يَرِدَا عَلَيَّ الْحَوْضَ — 'Ali is with the Qur'an and the Qur'an is with Ali (ع). They will not separate from each other until they return to me at the [paradisal] pool (al-hawd).' The spirit of the Qur'an and the soul of the Prophet were thus fully interiorized by Ali (ع), and it is precisely this interiorization of the twin sources of the Islamic revelation that constitutes the spiritual path of Islam.

<!-- page 2 -->

Our principal way of investigating and meditating upon the spirituality of Ali (ع) is to engage with the corpus of teachings attributed to him. Even if not every one of the thousands of sayings bearing his name can be confidently ascribed to him, the sheer magnitude of the corpus testifies that he must indeed have articulated a very large number of profound teachings. No other companion of the Prophet possesses anything approaching the body of work attributed to Ali (ع). Similarly, even if his foundational role in the development of a whole range of sciences remains debatable — sciences such as jurisprudence (fiqh), theology (kalam), Qur'anic exegesis (tafsir), rhetoric (balagha), grammar (nahw), and calligraphy (khatt), the mystical knowledge associated with Sufism, and such arcane sciences as numerology (jafr) and alchemy (al-kimiya) — the fact that later authorities in all these fields regard him as the original impulse behind their disciplines speaks to the far-reaching and penetrating influence of both his formal teachings and his personal radiance.

Given that the Islamic intellectual sciences as a whole are so closely identified with Ali (ع), the Prophet's description of Ali (ع) as the 'gate' to prophetic wisdom — cited as our epigraph — reads as both a description of what Ali (ع) was in relation to the Prophet during their own lifetime and a prophecy of the role he would play in the subsequent unfolding of the tradition's sciences. All such sciences are understood as so many formal, outward expressions of the essential, inward spirit of the Islamic revelation — a spirit that Ali (ع) synthetically assimilated and faithfully transmitted. Before proceeding further, however, the main historical outlines of his life should be sketched.

### Biographical Sketch

Ali (ع) was a 'follower' of the Prophet in the deepest sense of the word. He lived physically in the Prophet's shadow and absorbed spiritually everything that radiated from him. The intimacy of their relationship is captured in the words of one of the Imam's own sermons:

⟪ar:لَمَّا كُنْتُ وَلِيدًا ضَمَّنِي إِلَى صَدْرِهِ، فَكُنْتُ أتَّبِعُهُ اتّبَاعَ الفَصِيلِ أُمَّهُ⟫ — When I was but a child he took me under his wing. I would follow him [the Prophet] as a baby camel follows its mother. ⟪ar:يَرْفَعُ لِي فِي كُلِّ يَوْمٍ عَلَمًا مِنْ أَخْلاقِهِ الْكَرِيمَةِ وَيَأمُرُنِي بِالاِقْتِدَاءِ بِهِ⟫ — Every day he would hold up for me a sign of his noble character and command me to follow it. ⟪ar:وَكَانَ يَتَعَاهَدُ غَارَ حِرَاءٍ فِي كُلِّ سَنَةٍ، فَأَرَاهُ وَلَا يَرَاهُ غَيْرِي⟫ — Each year he would go into seclusion at [the mountain of] Hira'. I saw him there, and no one else did. ⟪ar:وَلَمْ يَجْمَعْ بَيْتٌ وَاحِدٌ يَوْمَئِذٍ فِي الإِسْلامِ غَيْرَ رَسُولِ اللهِ صَلَّى اللهُ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلَّمَ وَخَدِيجَةَ وَأَنَا ثَالِثُهُمَا⟫ — At that time no single household was gathered together in the religion of Islam, save that of the Messenger of God, Khadija, and myself as the third among them. ⟪ar:أَرَى نُورَ الْوَحْيِ وَالرِّسَالَةِ، وَأَشُمُّ رِيحَ النُّبُوَّةِ⟫ — I saw the light of the revelation and the message, and I smelt the fragrance of prophecy ...

<!-- page 3 -->

It is Ali (ع) as the faithful follower and transmitter of the prophetic norm that we wish to address in this biographical sketch. We shall therefore look at the early period of his life — from his birth (c. 599 CE) to the death of the Prophet (11/632). The second period, from the Prophet's death to Ali (ع)'s own assumption of the caliphate (35/656), and the third, consisting of his brief caliphate — dominated by tragic civil wars and ending with his assassination in 40/661 — will not be treated here. The issues those periods raise are fraught with historical and historiographical complexity, and engaging with such controversies would deflect us from the primary aim of this book: to introduce the spiritual ethos of this extraordinary figure in a way that demonstrates its universal relevance, both within Islam and beyond it. For this ethos transcends formal religious boundaries just as it rises above the political disputes on which the outward Shi'i–Sunni divergence rests — disputes that serve only to draw attention away from the essential spiritual message of Ali (ع).

What we wish to highlight, by contrast, is the unifying force of his spiritual teachings — teachings whose immense importance and profundity all Muslims, whatever their school of thought, affirm. In what follows we briefly survey the earlier period of Ali (ع)'s life spent with the Prophet, together with the ways in which the Prophet described Ali (ع) in well-attested sayings, since this constitutes the indispensable background against which his role as gate to prophetic wisdom must be understood. The second part of this introductory chapter then addresses some key principles of his spiritual ethos, with particular emphasis on the role of the intellect within it.

<!-- page 4 -->

According to all Shi'i authorities and several Sunni sources, Ali (ع) had the unique distinction of being born inside the Ka'ba in Mecca. His mother was Fatima bt. Asad; his father, Abu Talib, son of 'Abd al-Muttalib, was a leading member of the Hashimite clan. Abu Talib had raised the young orphan Muhammad — son of his brother 'Abd Allah — and would later become his chief protector when Islam was openly preached in Mecca. Some years before the mission began, during a time of drought, Muhammad eased the burden on Abu Talib by taking his young son Ali (ع), then about five years old, into his own household. From that point on, Ali (ع) was like a son to him, and the two were rarely apart.

Ali (ع) is regarded as the first male to embrace Islam, though he was only nine or ten years old at the time. Abu Bakr was the first adult male to enter the new religion, and Khadija, the Prophet's wife, was the first woman. The immediacy of the young boy's response to the prophetic message and his unflinching loyalty thereafter are of great significance. About three years later, when the Prophet was instructed by revelation to 'warn thy nearest kin' (26: 214), he invited the leading members of his clan to a feast and then addressed them: 'O sons of 'Abd al-Muttalib, I know of no Arab who hath come to his people with a nobler message than mine. I bring you the best of this world and the next. God hath commanded me to call you unto Him. Which of you, then, will help me in this, and be my brother, mine executor and my successor amongst you?' All remained silent — except for the youthful Ali (ع), who spoke up: 'O Prophet of God, I will be thy helper in this.' The Prophet then placed his hand on Ali (ع)'s neck and said, 'This is my brother, mine executor and my successor amongst you. Hearken unto him and obey him.' Though the clan's leaders greeted this with mockery at the time, the declaration was taken far more seriously as the years passed and the extraordinary character, nobility, and intelligence of the Prophet's young cousin became ever more dazzlingly evident.

<!-- page 5 -->

Also of importance in this early period is the role Ali (ع) played in the migration (hijra) from Mecca to Medina (622 CE) — the event that marks the beginning of the Muslim calendar. The Prophet's enemies had plotted to assassinate him; knowing this, Ali (ع) risked his own life by pretending to be the Prophet, sleeping in his bed on the night of his departure. Once in Medina, the Prophet adopted Ali (ع) as his 'brother' in the pact of brotherhood he established between the 'emigrants' (al-muhajirun) — the Muslims who had accompanied him on the hijra from Mecca — and the 'helpers' (al-ansar), the Medinan tribes who had embraced the new faith.

During the Medinan period, Ali (ع) distinguished himself on the battlefield, as a scribe of the unfolding Qur'anic revelation, and as a leading companion of the Prophet. His courage and heroism in battles against the overwhelmingly superior forces of the Meccan Quraysh and their allies were second to none. He stood at the forefront of nearly all the major engagements fought under the Prophet's banner and was frequently appointed standard-bearer. He was never defeated in any single-combat duel with which battles customarily began — indeed, he was never defeated in any battle he fought. Both his indomitable courage and his incomparable skill as a warrior became legendary. It is said that during the battle of Uhud, a heavenly voice was heard proclaiming, 'There is no chivalric knight but Ali (ع), no sword but Dhu'l-Faqar (la fata illa 'Alī, la sayf illa dhu'l-faqar)' — the latter being the name of the sword the Prophet had given him. The most celebrated of all battles in establishing Ali (ع)'s reputation as the pre-eminent — indeed invincible — warrior of Islam was that of Khaybar in 7/629. The Muslims had been unable to make any headway against the heavily fortified, apparently impregnable defences of their opponents. The Prophet then declared that he would give the army's banner to one who 'loves God and His Messenger and is loved by God and His Messenger', through whom victory would be granted. He sent for Ali (ع), who was absent because of an affliction of the eyes. The Prophet healed him by placing saliva upon his eyes, and Ali (ع) went on to lead the Muslims to victory. In the thick of the battle, having lost his own shield, he is said to have wrenched a gate from its hinges and used it as a shield — a gate which, after the battle, eight men could barely lift.

<!-- page 6 -->

Ali (ع)'s role as a warrior is only one of many factors that account for his unique place in the Islamic tradition. Far more significant is his closeness, in every respect, to the Prophet and the wisdom and sanctity that flowed from that proximity. Ali (ع) was accorded the immense honour of marrying the Prophet's daughter Fatima, considered alongside her mother Khadija — the Prophet's first wife — to be the most saintly woman in Islam. One of the epithets the tradition bestows on Fatima is Majma' al-Nurayn, 'Confluence of the Two Lights' — the two lights of nubuwwa (prophecy) and walaya (sanctity). After the Prophet himself, Ali (ع) was thus the head of what became known as the Ahl al-Bayt, 'the People of the House', that is, the Prophet's family.

The Ahl al-Bayt are addressed in an important Qur'anic verse as having been purified of all defilement: 'God only wisheth to remove from you all impurity, O People of the House, and to purify you with a complete purification' (33: 33). According to several of the most authoritative early sources, the Prophet called for Fatima, Ali (ع), and their two sons Hasan and Husayn, and declared, 'This is my Ahl al-Bayt.' On one well-attested occasion the Prophet gathered these members of his family beneath his cloak and recited the words of this verse. The event took place when a Christian delegation from Najran was engaged in religious debate with the Prophet and a revelation was received calling for a challenge of mutual imprecation (mubahila) against the Christians. The Prophet brought forward the four persons named above for this mubahila, but the Christians declined the challenge.

The importance of the Ahl al-Bayt is further expressed in a verse in which the Prophet is instructed, 'Say: I ask you for no reward, save love of the near of kin' (42: 23). Several of the most significant Qur'anic commentators identify 'the near of kin' in this verse as the Prophet's Ahl al-Bayt. Love of the Prophet's family is thus explicitly demanded by the revelation — without this excluding the verse's broader import, which encompasses love of all those bound to one by close kinship. The degree to which the Prophet's family is to be revered by all Muslims is further indicated by the fact that blessings are to be invoked upon his al, or progeny, as part of the blessings invoked upon him. The Qur'an instructs the believers: 'Truly, God and His angels bless the Prophet. O ye who believe, bless him and greet him with peace' (33: 56). When this verse was revealed, the Prophet was asked how one should perform this blessing, and he replied that blessings were to be invoked upon him and his al as follows: 'O God, bless Muhammad and the progeny of Muhammad, as Thou hast blessed Abraham and the progeny of Abraham. Truly, Thou art the Praised, the Glorious ...' The importance of this blessing — to be recited during the canonical prayer — together with the obligation to love the Prophet's family, is beautifully expressed in the following lines by Imam al-Shafi'i, founder of one of the four schools of Sunni jurisprudence:

Loving you, O family of the household of the Prophet of God, is an obligation (fard) from God in the Qur'an which He revealed; Sufficient to show the grandeur of your dignity is that one who blesses you not — no prayer has he.

<!-- page 7 -->

It is also important to note the following prophetic saying, which underscores the spiritual significance of the Ahl al-Bayt:

Truly, I am leaving behind amongst you the two weighty things (al-thaqalayn): the Book of God and my Ahl al-Bayt. They will not be parted from each other until they return to me at the [paradisal pool] al-hawd.


In addition to these sayings concerning Ali (ع) as the most important member of the Prophet's Ahl al-Bayt, there are sayings of the Prophet concerning Ali (ع) specifically that deserve to be cited in full, for it is only on the basis of these sayings that one can begin to appreciate the immensely important role of Ali (ع) in the spiritual dimension of the Islamic faith. These sayings or traditions (ahadith, sing. hadith) are presented here as a list with minimal commentary, following the manner in which they appear in most standard hadith collections. Anyone who takes prophetic guidance seriously cannot but take Ali (ع) seriously, and it is largely for this reason that countless Muslims across the centuries have meditated on these sayings, drawing from them diverse interpretations and incalculable inspiration. In a certain sense, these sayings form the prism through which the tradition views Ali (ع), and they serve to deepen one's spiritual receptivity to his teachings. The crucial importance of Ali (ع) in the spiritual firmament of nascent Islam emerges with greater clarity in the light of these prophetic descriptions; they should therefore be regarded as a significant aspect of the 'prophetic background' to Ali (ع)'s seminal contribution to Islamic spiritual and intellectual life, whether in Shi'i, Sunni, or Sufi context.

<!-- page 22 -->

The traditions given below can be found in any standard Shi'i hadith collection, but in order to show that they—together with their implications and the devotion they have inspired—are certainly not confined to Shi'i Islam, we cite them here from two of the most authoritative Sunni traditionists: Ahmad al-Nasa'i (d. 303/915), author of the Sunan, one of the six collections of prophetic traditions in the Sunni corpus deemed 'canonical' (al-kutub al-sitta); and al-Hakim al-Nīsabūrī (d. 405/1014), author of al-Mustadrak 'alā'l-ṣaḥīḥayn (Supplement to the Two Sound Collections). This latter work gathers those prophetic traditions that meet the exacting criteria of authenticity established by Bukhari and Muslim — authors of the ṣaḥīḥayn — but were not included by them in their own collections. The sayings pertaining to Ali (ع)'s distinctions, virtues, and merits — all conveyed by the Arabic word fada'il (sing. fadila) — explain why Ahmad b. Hanbal (d. 241/855), another leading Sunni traditionist and founder of one of the four Sunni schools of law, made the following statement: 'No companion of the Prophet has had such fada'il ascribed to him as those which have been ascribed to Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع).'

'Truly, Ali (ع) is from me and I am from him (inna 'Ali minnī wa ana minhu), and he is the wali (patron/spiritual master) of every believer after me.'

'Ali (ع) is with the Qur'an and the Qur'an is with Ali (ع), إِنَّ عَلِيًّا مَعَ الْقُرْآنِ وَالْقُرْآنُ مَعَ عَلِيٍّ، لَنْ يَفْتَرِقَا حَتَّى يَرِدَا عَلَيَّ الْحَوْضَ. They will not separate from each other until they return to me at the [paradisal pool] (al-hawd).'

'Three things were revealed to me regarding Ali (ع): he is the leader of the Muslims, the guide of the pious, and chief of the radiantly devout (sayyidu'l-muslimin, imamu'l-muttaqin, wa qa'idu'l-ghurra'l-muhajjalin).'

'Gazing upon Ali (ع) is an act of worship (al-nazar ilā 'Alī ibada).'

'May God have mercy on Ali (ع). O God, make the truth revolve around Ali (ع) wherever he turns (adiri'l-haqq ma'ahu haythu dara).'

'I am the city of knowledge and Ali (ع) is its gate; so whoever desires knowledge, let him enter the gate.'

'O Ali (ع), you are a leader (sayyid) in this world and the Hereafter. Your beloved is my beloved, and my beloved is the beloved of God; your enemy is my enemy, and my enemy is the enemy of God. Woe to those who hate you after me [after I have passed away].'

'Whoever desires to live my life and to die my death and to take his rest in the eternal Garden my Lord has promised me, let him orient himself towards Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), for truly he will never lead you away from right guidance, nor lead you into error.'

The Prophet said that Ali (ع) was 'as my own soul' (ka-nafsi).

He said to Ali (ع), 'You are from me and I am from you (anta minnī wa anā minka), أنْتَ مِنِّی وَ أِنَا مِنْکَ.'

'... whoever obeys Ali (ع) obeys me, and whoever disobeys him disobeys me.'

'You will clarify for my community that over which they will differ after me (anta tubayyinu li-ummatī ma'khtalafū fihi ba'di).'

'There is one amongst you who will fight for the ta'wil [spiritual interpretation] of the Qur'an as I have fought for its tanzil [literal revelation], عَلِیٌّ یُقَاتِلُ عَلیٰ تَاوِیلِہِ کَما قَاتَلْتُ عَلیٰ تَنزِیلِہِ.' Abu Bakr asked, 'Is it I?' The Prophet said, 'No.' 'Umar asked, 'Is it I?' The Prophet said, 'No, it is the one who is mending the sandal.' The Prophet had given Ali (ع) his sandal to mend.

'O Ali (ع), there is in you something akin to Jesus, on whom be peace and blessings. The Jews hated him so intensely that they slandered his mother; and the Christians loved him so intensely that they ascribed to him a rank he did not possess.' 'O Ali (ع), whoever separates himself from me separates himself from God, and whoever separates himself from you, O Ali (ع), separates himself from me.'

'Whoever curses Ali (ع) curses me, and whoever curses me curses God.'

The Prophet said to his wife 'A'isha, 'Call unto me the leader (sayyid) of the Arabs.' She asked, 'O Prophet of God, are you not the leader of the Arabs?' He said, 'I am the leader of the children of Adam, and Ali (ع) is the leader of the Arabs.' When Fatima was asked by some women why the Prophet had given her in marriage to a man as poor as Ali (ع), she related this to the Prophet. He said: 'Are you not pleased that God — exalted and glorified be He — surveyed the people of the world and chose two men from among them: one is your father, and the other your husband?'

'The first of you to reach the [paradisal] pool (al-hawd) is the first of you who entered Islam, Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع).'

'Ali (ع) is from me and I am from him ('Alī minnī wa ana minhu), and nobody can fulfil my duty but myself and Ali (ع).'

Ali (ع) himself relates that the Prophet told him that none but a believer will love him [Ali (ع)], and none but a hypocrite will hate him.

When the Prophet was about to depart on an expedition to Tabuk, he left Ali (ع) as his deputy in Medina. Ali (ع) was saddened not to be going with him. The Prophet said, 'Are you not content to stand in relation to me as Aaron stood in relation to Moses — except that there is no prophet after me?'

The Prophet prayed to God to bring 'the most beloved of Thy creatures' to share with him a meal of fowl. Only when Ali (ع) arrived did the Prophet invite him to join him.

<!-- page 23 -->

Among the several Quranic verses on which the Prophet commented with reference to Ali (ع) is 13:7: 'Verily thou art a warner, and for every people there is a guide.' The Prophet said, 'I am the warner ... you are the guide, O Ali (ع). After me, the rightly-guided shall be guided by you.'

Regarding the revelation of verse 55 of Sura 5 (al-Ma'ida) — 'Verily your wali is only God and His Messenger and those who believe, establish the prayer and give alms while bowing in prayer' — the commentators relate this to the occasion when Ali (ع), while bowing in prayer, extended his ring to a beggar who had asked for alms. The Prophet recited this verse upon being told of the incident and then added, in what are perhaps the most celebrated words ever spoken about Ali (ع): 'For whomever I am the mawla [guardian, master, close friend], Ali (ع) is his mawla (man kuntu mawlahu fa-'Alī mawlahu).'

This last saying was expressed on a number of occasions, the most famous of which was after the Prophet's final pilgrimage to Mecca, in the year 10/632, at a pool midway between Mecca and Medina known as Ghadir Khumm. The Prophet assembled all the pilgrims, had a pulpit erected, and delivered an address to the thousands gathered there. The address culminated in the declaration: 'For whomever I am the mawla, Ali (ع) is his mawla.' For Shi'is, this is regarded as an unambiguous designation (nass) of Ali (ع) as successor to the Prophet; for Sunnis it indicates the special closeness of Ali (ع) to the Prophet, but not his nomination as successor in the political domain. That the reference to Ali (ع) as mawla — in some versions, as wali — is of the highest spiritual significance, however, is not seriously disputed. 'Umar is reported to have approached Ali (ع) and said, 'Bakh bakh' — an expression of congratulation — 'you have become my mawla and the mawla of every Muslim.' The polemical debate that has exercised countless minds throughout history, and that continues to this day, turns on the political implications of Ali (ع)'s spiritual authority, his walaya.

Although it is not our purpose to discuss Ali (ع)'s role and place within Shi'i Islam, one should note that his charisma was such that, even during the Prophet's own lifetime, a group came to be known as the shi'a, or 'partisans,' of Ali (ع). Al-Țabarī (d. 310/923), author of the first great commentary on the Qur'an, reports that the Prophet interpreted the verse 'Those who have faith and do righteous deeds, they are the best of created beings' (98:7) as referring to Ali (ع) and his 'shi'a.' Four individuals in particular were renowned for their devotion to Ali (ع) during the Prophet's lifetime and are regarded as the prototypes of early Shi'ism: Salman al-Farisi, Abu Dharr al-Ghifarī, 'Ammar b. Yasir, and Miqdad b. 'Amr. This early manifestation of love for Ali (ع) should not be identified with the theological doctrines that were later elaborated in what came to be known as the Shi'i branch of Islam in subsequent generations; it is nonetheless worth emphasising that, at the core of Shi'ism — in all its different expressions — lies the notion of fidelity to Ali (ع). This fidelity, loyalty, or love constitutes one aspect of walaya; the other, complementary aspect is the spiritual authority or sanctity of Ali (ع). It is equally important to stress that neither devotion to Ali (ع) nor the spiritual orientation toward the walaya he embodies is the exclusive province of Shi'ism. Rather, it extends across the whole of the Islamic faith, for devotion to Ali (ع) as head of the Prophet's Ahl al-Bayt is found throughout the Muslim world and is especially deep wherever Sufism flourishes. As will be discussed in Chapter 3, Ali (ع) is regarded as the first spiritual 'pole' (qutb) of Sufism after the Prophet himself, and he stands at the summit of all the spiritual chains (salasil, sing. silsila) through which the Sufi orders trace their lineage back to the Prophet. To speak of Ali (ع) is to speak not of exclusivist sectarianism but of universal spirituality.


### The Spirit Of The Intellect

What we propose to do in this section is sketch some of the key dimensions of the spiritual ethos of Ali (ع), with particular emphasis on its intellectual aspects. Beyond demonstrating the pivotal role played by the intellect (al-'aql) in the elaboration and assimilation of the Imam's spiritual teachings, this exercise should help to ground more firmly the explorations of the ethos offered in the chapters that follow. One of the key aims here is to illuminate something of the 'spirit of the intellect'—the term ⟪ar:الروح العقل⟫ that the author coins for the relationship between the intellect and the spirit—as it appears in the Imam's worldview: a spirit that surpasses, while containing, the activities of the rational mind, and that encompasses domains not nowadays associated with the intellect, such as moral comportment and aesthetic sensibility. We also hope to show how this spiritual understanding of the intellect is directly related to the Qur'anic conception of the spirit (al-ruh), and to explore some of the fruits of engaging deeply with this unifying spirit that is proper to the intellect.

It may be useful at the outset to explain why we prefer the English word 'intellect' over 'reason' as a translation of 'aql. What we wish to evoke is the original meaning of intellectus in Latin Christendom, a meaning virtually identical to that of nous in the Patristic Greek tradition. The Latin intellectus means 'understanding', and it carries within it the sense of standing under a thing: to understand something is to stand beneath a perception of reality and allow that perception to overshadow and govern one's actions, since our actions are always performed in the shadow of how we perceive things. Intellectus/nous is thus the faculty capable of a direct, contemplative vision of transcendent realities—the very realities of which the Qur'an speaks but which are hidden from the senses—whereas reason, translating the Latin ratio and the Greek dianoia, is indirect and discursive in nature; it works through logic and arrives only at mental concepts of those realities. The root of ratio denotes a relation or comparison between two numbers, and this captures how reason proceeds: by comparison, by a binary logic of affirmation and negation, weighing one thing against another until it has defined what it is considering. Reason, so understood, is the instrument the intellect employs; but it can carry us only as far as the outward form of a thing, only as far as the mental concept or image of a reality. With the intellect one is able to contemplate or 'see' the Absolute; with reason one can only think about it. To know about a reality is one thing; to contemplate and experience it directly is quite another—and it is the intellect, not reason, that is capable of that experience, even if it is through reason that the intellect is brought to it.

This preliminary exploration of the spirit of the intellect in the Imam's ethos will be sketched chiefly through reflections on a short discourse of the utmost importance in the Nahj al-balagha: the discourse the Imam gave to one of his closest disciples, Kumayl b. Ziyad. These reflections employ the same approach used in the two chapters that follow—an interpretive commentary on the Imam's words that draws not only on his other sayings on the theme in question, but also on concordant Qur'anic verses and, occasionally, traditions of the Prophet that either help illuminate the meaning of the Imam's saying under consideration, or are themselves elucidated by it. Before turning to that dialogue, however, it is important to stress three factors inherent to the spirit of the intellectual perspective being considered here: the centrality of revelation, the harmony between revelation and the intellect, and the distinction between the intellect—conceived as the principle of the articulation of consciousness—and reason, understood as one expression of that principle.

Beginning with the first: everything about the Imam's life and teachings is centred on the Qur'anic revelation and its embodiment in the Prophet's conduct. The Qur'an is the centre around which the intellect must revolve, for the intellect needs a centre—principles and definitions to which it can hold fast for proper guidance. Both the thought and the life of the Imam can indeed be read as a kind of spiritual commentary, a ta'wil, on the sacred text. On the doctrinal plane, it can be stated with confidence that Ali (ع) possessed an innate aptitude for the work of ta'wil. On the existential plane as well, his life—from his birth in the most holy shrine of the Ka'ba to his martyrdom in the mosque of Kufa—reads as a dramatic unfolding of the inner meaning of the revealed text. Ali (ع)'s refusal to stop short at the superficial surface of things—whether religious, ethical, or political—manifested an unflinching fidelity to the sacred substance of the religion, a fidelity that entailed not only inward intellectual penetration but also outward combative resistance. The saying of the Prophet cited earlier is highly significant in this connection: Ali (ع) would fight for the ta'wil of the Qur'an just as he, the Prophet, had fought for its tanzil—عَلِیُّ یُقَاتِلُ عَلیٰ تَاوِیلِہ کَما قَتَلُ عَلیَ تَنزِیلِہِ. As noted earlier, Ali (ع)'s short caliphate was dominated by bloody civil wars. A strong case can be made that all three of these wars—those of the Camel (al-Jamal), of Nahrawan, and of Siffin—were fought for the sake of the correct interpretation and application of the Qur'an, for the clarification of its fundamental message of tawhid, and for the realization of that oneness on every level, spiritual and social alike. These were not political battles or mere power struggles; they were battles waged to clarify and enforce the oneness of God according to the principles of the Qur'an—in thought, in speech, and in action. This claim is vividly substantiated by the Imam's response to a bedouin who, just as the battle of the Camel (al-Jamal) was about to begin, approached him and said, 'O Commander of the Faithful (یا أمیر المومنین)! Do you say that God is one?' Those present rebuked the bedouin, telling him this was neither the time nor the place for such questions. The Imam, however, intervened: 'Leave him, for surely what the bedouin wishes is what we wish for the people.'

We can begin our reflections on the spirituality of Ali (ع) by considering this response, which bears directly on the principle that right intention takes priority over outward action, whatever that action may be. Had the Imam been guided by reason alone, he would have agreed with those who rebuked the bedouin and told him to find a more fitting time and place; but he saw with the intellect that stands above reason. For the Imam, the most fundamental intention motivating every action—whether religious in the ordinary sense or military as here—is knowledge of the true nature of ultimate reality. What the Imam 'wishes for the people', then, is precisely this knowledge, which must lie at the very heart of all concerns, motivations, and actions, whether on the battlefield or the prayer-rug. There is therefore no time or place where the question 'What do you mean by saying God is one?' is irrelevant or out of place. It was for the sake of correct knowledge of the divine—and all its ramifications in society—that the Imam was fighting, in fulfilment of the prophetic saying just mentioned. On the very field of battle, then, the Imam delivers a discourse on the meaning of divine oneness. The very first part of his reply contains a key to understanding his entire spiritual ethos. He refutes any notion that God's oneness is numerical or countable. He tells the bedouin that it is not permissible to say 'one' while holding any numerical conception in mind, for 'that which has no second does not enter into the category of numbers'. The moment a thing can be counted as 'one', it immediately admits the possibility of 'two', 'three', 'four', and so a oneness understood in this numerical sense is undone by the very number that would affirm it.

'That which has no second' is not merely a divinity to which no partner can be ascribed, as a theologian might put it, but that reality which has no 'otherness'—the Real, al-Haqq, which is at once transcendent and immanent, beyond all 'things' and manifested by all 'things', simultaneously inaccessible and inalienable. To say that God has no otherness is not to say that things exist which might have been His partners but which believers simply decline to associate with Him; it is to affirm that nothing whatever in existence bears His likeness, nothing that could conceivably be set beside Him. In these few words the Imam effects—for those who have 'ears to hear'—a shift of consciousness from the dogmatic notion of one God versus many gods to the mystical apprehension of the one and only Reality, in the face of which everything else is mere appearance. All things come to be apprehended as outward phenomena calling out to be deciphered, as signs (ayat) pointing beyond themselves to That of which they are manifestations—for an aya is precisely a meaningful token that points beyond itself to another. Retracing these appearances back to their source and origin is the literal meaning of ta'wil: going back to the awwal, 'the First' (عَودَۃ الی الاول). On one hand, the Real is described as the beginning and end of all things, the apparent and the hidden; there is nothing in being but this one Reality: 'He is the First and the Last, the Outward and the Inward' (57: 3). On the other hand, apparent multiplicity is to be 'brought back' to real oneness: 'We shall show them Our signs (sa-nurihim ayatina) on the horizons and in their own souls, so that it be clear to them that He is the Real'—سَنُرِيهِمْ آيَتِنَا فِى ٱلأٓفَاقِ وَفِىٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ حَتَّىٰ يَتَبَيَّنَ لَهُمْ أَنَّهُ ٱلْحَقُّ (41: 53).

In one saying attributed to Ali (ع), the Prophet is described as the first of those whom God taught the science of ta'wil, and who in turn taught it to Ali (ع). According to the Prophet, as noted earlier, 'Truly, Ali (ع) is with the Qur'an, and the Qur'an is with Ali (ع)'—أنَّ علیٌ مع القرآن، و قرآن مع العلی. In another famous saying, often quoted by Sufi commentators, Ali (ع) claimed he could load seventy camels with the pages of commentary he could offer on the Fatiha alone, the opening chapter of the Qur'an. It is worth noting also that Ali (ع)'s cousin, Ibn 'Abbas—often referred to as the 'first commentator'—claimed to have learnt the science of interpretation from Ali (ع).

In this connection, the following saying of the Imam is of fundamental importance, and helps explain why he is held in the highest esteem within the discipline of Qur'anic exegesis, tafsir: 'The Qur'an consists of a book inscribed, between two covers; it speaks not with a tongue, it cannot do without an interpreter (la budda lahu min tarjuman).' The reciprocity between revelation and the intellect is further underlined in the saying, 'The prophet of a man is the interpreter of his intellect (rasul al-rajul tarjuman 'aqlihi).' Elsewhere we find an explicit correspondence between the intellect and the 'inner' messenger: the intellect is 'the messenger of the Real'.

The Imam's teachings, which express his spiritual ethos, should therefore be read essentially as creative interpretations of the Qur'anic revelation. They initiate a movement from the letter of the revelation to its spirit, and from the spirit of the revelation to the spirit of the Revealer—a process of 'retracing', of returning to the source, the origin, the beginning, al-awwal, accomplished in proportion to how fully the spirit of the intellect is enlivened within the interpreter. Yet the Revealer remains necessarily concealed even as He is revealed through the revelation—concealed because the essence of what is revealed, the divine Reality in itself, is ineffable and inexpressible; only the forms of its manifestations are susceptible to revelation. The dialectical interplay between form and the formless, the evident and the hidden, the revealed and the concealed—such antinomies lie at the root of the paradoxical mode of expression for which the Imam is renowned, and which we will encounter throughout this book. The relationship that especially concerns us at present is the second of the three factors mentioned above: that between revelation and 'intellection', by which we mean the creative use of the intellect. This relationship takes on particular importance in a context substantially defined by fidelity to revelation. Whereas rationalists in the strict sense regard intellect and revelation as mutually exclusive, in this perspective they are seen as perfectly harmonious, subsisting in a state of creative reciprocity, each requiring the other for completeness, for integrity, for the 'making one' that is the very essence of tawhid.

Indeed, one of the most striking aspects of the Imam's perspective is the way it exposes the hollowness of many false or excessively polarized dichotomies that later came to characterize—or were imposed upon—Islamic thought. This is due to the highly synthetic quality of the Imam's discourses, viewed as 'pre-philosophical' by some academics, and as 'supra-philosophical'—inspired and evocative—by those within the Islamic spiritual tradition. The apparently insuperable dichotomy between intellect and revelation appears in various polarized forms: reason versus faith, convention (sunna) versus nature (tabi'a), imitation (taqlīd) versus verification (tahqīq), piety versus inquiry, and so on. In Ali (ع)'s ethos, however, intellect and revelation are not mutually exclusive but complementary, and at the highest level inseparable, truth being one. While the human intellect clearly cannot be equated with divine revelation, the spirit animating the intellect is not, in the final analysis, confined by human limitations—as we shall see. It is this spirit that perceives and grasps the divine quality of revelation.

The divine spirit articulating the form and infusing the content of revelation must be fathomable to some degree by human consciousness; without this, revelation would amount to nothing more than a set of rules and regulations for the will, rather than a source of creative inspiration for the intelligence. It cannot be stressed too strongly that divine revelation, far from curtailing the operations of reason and imposing some unintelligible diktat, on the contrary presupposes a creative engagement with all the resources of the intelligence—in particular those processes of reflection and meditation for which the Imam himself is renowned, and which the Qur'an repeatedly calls for. It is precisely the opposite of a decree imposed from without: the Qur'an constantly demands fikr, deep and sustained reflection, and such reflection does not enclose the meaning of the text within its literal surface but discloses it, opening up the inner horizons of meaning folded within the letter.

For the revealed text is itself silent; it speaks only through its interpreter, the intellect. This understanding of the bond between intellect and revelation rigorously excludes all literalism and superficiality—every attempt to confine meaning within a flat, one-sided reading. How deeply the text's signification is plumbed depends on the depth and penetration of the interpreter's own intellect, since the text carries multiple levels of meaning simultaneously. At its most profound, this 'prophetic' intellect rejoins the very source of revelation itself; for it is this 'inner prophet'—the inmost degree of consciousness—that is the ultimate ground on which a person is able to confirm the verses of the outer revelation as true: to validate them through lived experience, and not merely to affirm them by dogma. It is in this sense that the Imam says, in the first sermon of the Nahj, that one of the purposes of God's sending forth of prophets to mankind was 'to unearth for them the buried treasures of the intellects' (dafa'in al-'uqul).


This saying is one of the keys to the spiritual ethos we are considering, and we shall return to it again and again throughout these essays. The intellect is not stifled by revelation; it is brought to life by it. Revelation is a clarion call to the spiritual depths of the intellect, cultivating and bringing to fruition the seeds of consciousness — the seeds of توحید — that God has planted there and that lie buried within us. For the spirit of one's intellect already carries the initial imprint of its Creator: in the world of the spirit it witnessed its Lord and testified to His lordship, and revelation, the ذکر, does no more than remind it of that primordial testimony. A reminder, after all, is never a new piece of information; it is knowledge one already possesses but has forgotten, for one can only be reminded of what one already knows. We were created in this world in a kind of amnesia — and just as a physician tells the family of a man who has lost his memory to surround him with familiar things, with signs (آیات) out of his past, to jog his memory, so God has set His reminders in the horizons and the souls (آفاق و أنفس). The spirit of one's intellect, then, cannot be alien to what descends as revelation from on high; on the contrary, it must be fundamentally akin to it, and it is precisely for this reason that one can be absolutely certain that the revelation truly comes from the Absolute. The point is not to set up a dichotomy between intellect and revelation, but to see the intellect as revelation, or at least as one mode of revelation: the God-given means by which the individual comes to discern, affirm, and plumb the divine 'revelations' outside himself — both those of scripture and those constituted by the 'signs' (ayat) of the natural world. If the human intellect can perceive and recognize revelation, it is because both are rooted in one and the same ultimate source; we came from a spiritual realm, and so did revelation, sent to remind us of what we once witnessed and have since forgotten. Prophetic revelation awakens and quickens the depths of the human intellect; it does not replace or displace it. The messengers do for these buried seeds what the farmer does when he waters what lies hidden beneath the soil, what the miner does when he digs into the mountain for its gold and silver, what the diver does when he descends to the sea-bed to bring up its buried treasures. From this perspective the revealed text is not some extraneous source set against the intelligence, but the objective, outward expression of the very principles that the intellect itself yields up to the 'inner prophet' — the intellect serving as the inner messenger that the outer messenger awakens and nourishes — where by 'intellect' we mean not simply the faculty of reasoning but the source of consciousness that articulates the human spirit.

The intellect, then, holds unsuspected depths — 'buried treasures' — and is far from being exhausted by its surface operations, the cognitions and reasonings proper to its purely mental dimension. These considerations bring us to the third of the factors mentioned above: the relationship between intellect and reason. Just as there can be no contradiction whatsoever between revelation and intellection, so too one must avoid setting up a false dichotomy between reason and intellect. Reason should rather be seen as one of the modes of the intellect; reason no more contradicts intellect than intellect contradicts revelation. Reason is a mode of the intellect, but it is not to be equated with it grosso modo — just as one cannot reduce the universal objectivity of revelation to the subjective specificities of any one individual's intellection, to his or her own 'private' revelation. To equate reason with intellect would be tantamount to denying the very existence of the 'buried treasures' of the intellect, those supra-rational dimensions that are, initially, buried beneath — and thus hidden from — the rational surface of the intellect. The revelation constituted by the intellect is embedded within the creation of the human being, the intellect being that which most critically defines the human being as such. Far from pitting the intellect against revelation, then, each is to be seen in the light of the other: the substance of revelation cannot be engaged except through the intellect, on one hand, and the 'buried treasures' of the intellect cannot be disclosed except through revelation, on the other — a genuine co-dependency of the two. As will be seen below, this understanding of the intellect brings us close to what the Imam refers to as the 'heart' (قلب), that innermost mode of perception capable of 'seeing' God. It is the 'heart' that can 'see' God — not with eyesight, not with بصارۃ, but with insight, with بصیرۃ, that spiritual insight generated by the 'verities of faith' (haqa'iq al-iman / حقائق الایمان). As the Imam puts it, in response to the questioner asking whether he had 'seen' his Lord: 'Eyes see Him not through sight's observation, but hearts see Him through the verities of faith.' We shall have occasion to return to this 'inner vision' of God through the 'verities of faith'.

To return to the relationship between intellect and reason: one can say that between the spiritual depths of the intellect and its rational surface there is both continuity and discontinuity. The aspect of continuity is manifested in the evident fact that all thinking begins with concepts that convey intelligible meaning; thought sets out from ideas that are rationally discernible, and these must bear some relation to the realities they express, or they collapse into ideas that lead nowhere. But there is also discontinuity between the spiritual and the rational aspects of the intellect, in that reason stops short at the ideas it has 'de-fined'. The Arabic word for definition, hadd (حد), expresses — like its English equivalent — both senses at once: that of a conceptual definition and that of a limitation, a boundary, the very boundary that reason draws around an idea by weighing its pros and cons until it is fixed and 'defined'. To go beyond the limits imposed by this rational act of definition, something more than reason is necessary; and it is this 'something more' that makes the ethos we are considering a spiritual one, and not a rationalistic one. It is certainly 'rational', in the sense that the form of the ethos is indeed susceptible to rational scrutiny; but it is not 'rationalistic', inasmuch as its essence is not predicated on reason alone.

Now Ali (ع) frequently hints, in highly allusive terms, at the mysteries and marvels — the unearthed treasures of the intellect — that he has directly witnessed by the eye of his purified heart. He also alludes to this supra-rational mode of awareness indirectly, through a bewildering use of apophatic discourse and paradoxical utterance, of which the corpus attributed to him is full. In this ethos it seems that the higher the truth, the deeper the paradox: the sheer incommensurability between reality as it is in itself and the human conceptions we form of it is effectively evoked by this creative use of paradox. In modern parlance, each human conception of reality needs to be 'deconstructed'; but then, unlike a modern deconstructionism so radically destructive that it has nothing left to 'reconstruct', the Imam's apophasis feeds directly into modes of spiritual intuition, contemplation, and realization of that which reason, on its own, is incapable of attaining. It is this intuition and contemplation which 're-members' — or 'reconstructs', if one likes — the spiritual realities that transcend the categories of reason. The value of the paradoxes, the antinomies, the apophasis, and the negation is entirely predicated on the eminently positive realities which the intellect can plumb, thereby becoming aware of its own hidden depths. The resulting consciousness does not so much negate reason as situate it, revealing the limits of reason's proper sphere of operation: it reveals that reason alone cannot 'comprehend' the higher realities, nor express them fully, however indispensable it may be in initiating the cognitive movement towards those realities.

Thus, when the Imam negates — as he does so often — the possibility of human conceptions encompassing the divine Reality, what is being negated is the limitation that is the inescapable corollary of all such conceptions, and not the positive content, the ma'na or spiritual meaning, of the conception itself; for this conception is a necessary starting point for a process of assimilation that engages a deeper level of awareness than that 'de-fined' by the mind, and which therefore extends far beyond the domain of speculative thought. In this light, the negation of the limitations of human conception should be seen as the prelude to an absolute affirmation: the affirmation of the Absolute, which has no limitation. The spiritual negation of conceptual limitation is of an upward, 'constructive' order, not a nihilistic, deconstructive one. Nowhere is this style of discourse more apparent than in the famous opening lines of the first sermon of the Nahj al-balagha:

Praise be to God, whose laudation those who speak cannot deliver, whose graces those who count cannot number, whose rightful due those who strive cannot render; He who cannot be grasped by far-reaching aspirations, nor fathomed by profound intuitions; He whose attribute has no binding limitation, no existing description, no time appointed, no term extended.

The divine attribute (sifa) has no limit in terms of which it can be defined — laysa li-sifatih hadd mahdud — and yet it is affirmed as real. It has no 'existing description', and yet in some sense it is described (mawsuf), by the very words of the Qur'anic revelation; for in the Qur'an God is described as having such attributes as mercy, compassion, and power. He is the Merciful, the Compassionate, the Almighty — and yet the Imam affirms that these very attributes cannot be defined, bounded, or described. The paradox sharpens still further when the Imam speaks of the perfection of ikhlas, the 'rendering sincere', the purification of one's devotion to God and of one's conception of Him: this perfection, he says, is to divest (nafy) God of all attributes. On the one hand the attributes are affirmed, and on the other they are to be negated. The rational mind can only think of God in terms of the attributes given verbal expression in the Qur'an; and yet here the Imam appears to negate these same attributes, thereby depriving the mind of any convenient conceptual handle by which to grasp the nature of the divine Reality. The simultaneous affirmation and negation of the attributes raises an apparently irresolvable contradiction, and in so doing enhances receptivity to a deeper mode of knowing. This style of discourse appears designed to awaken in the listener some latent awareness inscribed in the heart or the depths of the intellect, and to invite the seeker to dive into those depths, into the knowledge 'buried' in the intellect. The very shock of the apparent contradiction in this apophasis seems intended to precipitate a leap of consciousness — from the formal activity of reason to the supra-rational contemplation of the spirit.

Consider the words spoken by the Imam immediately after affirming that his heart had indeed witnessed God, not by means of outward vision but through 'the verities of faith':

My Lord is not described in terms of dimension (bu'd), nor motion (haraka), nor rest (sukun) ... nor through coming or going: He is subtle in subtlety but not described by subtlety, magnificent in magnificence but not described by magnificence, great in greatness but not described by greatness ...

The attributes are again affirmed and denied. God possesses these attributes but is not reducible to them. We can begin to think of God in terms of these categories, but He is not reducible to our conceptions. The attributes afford the mind something by which the divine nature can be understood, but this understanding is fragmentary, never exhaustive, and it needs to be negated as the prelude to a fuller apprehension. The initial conceptual orientation to the divine Reality needs to be both negated and sublimated: negated as a mental conception, while the positive content of the conception needs to be sublimated through spiritual intuition; only then can the two-dimensional 'portrait' defined by reason be given a third dimension — that of depth. But this third dimension emerges only after the insufficiency of the rational depiction is understood — whence the need for that cognitive shock which paradox produces, a shock intended to reveal the pretension of reason in its effort to disclose the nature of the Absolute, the transcendent source of its own activities.

One sees in the Imam's discourses a repeated combination of affirmation and denial, which mirrors the negation and affirmation found in the first testimony of Islam: la ilaha, 'no god', being the negation (nafy); then illa'Llah, 'except God', coming as the affirmation (ithbat). This helps one to see that the negation of the attributes mentioned by the Imam as being the perfection of ikhlas is in fact an affirmation of the infinitude of the divine Reality. Each of the attributes is to be 'negated', but only insofar as it is conceived by the mind. Such negation is necessary because, at the mental level, each attribute excludes other attributes, and also excludes the object of attribution itself — that is, the divine Essence — such exclusion following inescapably from the very possibility of conceptually distinguishing between the attribute and the object of attribution, between the divine quality and the divine Essence. For any conception to take place in the mind, defined ideas are necessary, and where there are definitions, there are limits and thus exclusions. The reality of the attribute, however, is unlimited, by virtue of its absolute identity with the Essence. The attribute that has been 'negated' is paradoxically affirmed, not in itself but inasmuch as it is nothing but God: illa'Llah. Thus, one can place each of the negated attributes in the position of the nafy of the first testimony and arrive at such formulations as la rahmana illa'Llah, 'There is no All-Compassionate except God'; la rahima illa'Llah, 'No All-Merciful except God', and so on. The apparent negation of the attributes becomes, in fact, an affirmation of their true identity, shorn of any restrictive association with the poverty of mental conceptions. The attributes are not superadded to the Essence but are absolutely one with it, differentiable from it only conceptually, not ontologically. The Essence does indeed comprise all the attributes mentioned — such as greatness, subtlety, and so on — but it is also infinitely more than these attributes; and, for their part, the attributes are infinite only within and as the Essence. The Imam's apophasis here can be seen as a commentary on the Qur'anic refrain, 'Glorified be God above what they describe' (23: 91). The infinitude of God strictly requires, on the human plane, the negation of all that the mind can conceive through reason, but not all that the heart can 'see' through spiritual vision. This very negation on the plane of reason calls out to something deeper, and this in turn can only be understood in the light of spiritual praxis, which is always assumed as the reflective, devotional, or contemplative context for the hearers of the Imam's sermons and discourses. The heart — symbolic seat of the contemplative intelligence — calls out to be 'polished', and this is precisely what the 'remembrance of God' does, this dhikr Allah being, according to the Imam, the 'most excellent form of worship', as we shall see in Chapter 3 of this book. However, even at this level of praxis there is a further paradox: the 'reality' of the remembrance is not attained until 'you forget yourself in your remembrance'.

It is not just the rational mind and the determinate conceptions proportioned thereto that are to be negated or 'deconstructed', but the individual soul itself as the locus of all cognition, rational or supra-rational. This final denouement, or fana', the ultimate form of apophasis, will be discussed further in Chapter 3.

These considerations have been offered to show the extent to which the spirit of the intellect, in Ali (ع)'s perspective, transcends the rational faculty. The intellect must be seen, first and foremost, as a spiritual faculty, and the spirit, in turn, must be appreciated as the source of the intellect, as well as the source of life. Without the intellect the spirit is reduced to unconscious existence; conversely, without the spirit, the intellect tends towards lifeless thought. These aspects of the spirit are clearly expressed in the first sermon of the Nahj, which can be read as a commentary upon the Qur'anic verses describing the creation of man in terms of God's 'breathing' of His spirit into man: 'He began the creation of man with clay ... He fashioned him and breathed into him of His spirit' (⟪ar:ثُمَّ سَوَّىٰهُ وَنَفَخَ فِيهِ مِن رُّوحِهِ⟫, 32: 7–9).

The following lines, from the first sermon, form one of the many passages in which this infusion of the divine spirit into man is commented upon by Ali (ع):

ثُمَّ جَمَعَ سُبْحَانَهُ مِنْ حَزْنِ الاْرْضِ وَسَهْلِهَا، وَعَذْبِهَا وَسَبَخِهَا۔ تُرْبَةً سَنَّهَا بالمَاءِ حَتَّى خَلَصَتْ۔ وَلاَطَهَا بِالبَلَّةِ حَتَّى لَزَبَتْ۔ فَجَبَلَ مِنْها صُورَةً ذَاتَ أَحْنَاءَ وَوُصُولَ، وَأَعْضَاءَ وَفُصُول۔ أَجْمَدَهَا حَتَّى اسْتَمْسَكَتْ، وَأَصْلَدَهَا حَتَّى صَلْصَلَتْ۔ لِوَقْت مَعْدُود، وَأجَل مَعْلُوم۔ ثُمَّ نَفَخَ فِيها مِنْ رُوحِهِ فَمَثُلَتْ إِنْساناً ذَا أَذْهَانُ يُجيلُهَا، وَفِكَر يَتَصَرَّفُ بِهَا، وَجَوَارِحَ يَخْتَدِمُهَا، وَأَدَوَات يُقَلِّبُهَا، وَمَعْرِفَة يَفْرُقُ بِهَا بَيْنَ الحَقِّ وَالبَاطِلِ۔


Then He — glorified be He! — gathered up a quantity of dust, from some hard earth and some soft, some sweet earth and some salty, and poured water onto it until it was pure, then kneaded into it some moisture until it stuck together; then He fashioned from this a form with curves and joints, limbs and parts. He compacted it until it held tightly together, solidified it until it became as sounding clay, assigning to it a measured duration and a known consummation. Then He breathed into it of His spirit, and it stood up as a human being, endowed with intellectual powers with which to reflect, thoughts by which to conduct himself, limbs to put to service, instruments at his disposal, and knowledge with which to discern between the true and the false ...

<!-- page 22 -->

The body had been fashioned entire — its eyes, its ears, the organ of thought all in place — and yet it could neither hear nor see nor reflect until God breathed His spirit into it; only then did it stand up as a human being. It is this 'in-spiration' — literally, the in-breathing and the insertion of the spirit — of God's breath that produces consciousness and life. Death, by contrast, is the 'ex-piration', the departure of the spirit. Life in the absence of 'aspiration' — the intention to be true to one's spirit — is a kind of death. It is through the spirit that inanimate, unconscious matter is brought to life and to awareness of itself: not only does it generate knowledge, but self-knowledge as well. This intimate intertwining between the gift of life and the capacity to know — existence that is conscious, and knowledge that is existential — is one of the foundations of any truly spiritual perspective. The question is not one of setting the spirit against the body, but of recognizing that the infusion of the spirit into the body is precisely what brings the body to life; and it is for this reason that knowledge vivifies, in the very measure that it is spiritual: 'O ye who believe, respond to God and His Messenger when they call you to that which vivifies you' (8: 24). As we have seen, according to the Imam, one of the most important aspects of this call from God to human beings is to 'remind them of the buried treasures of the intellect'.

Existentiation is the bestowal of the spirit, which is consciousness; hence the 'aql, or intellect — the principle of articulation for the spirit — is not merely a mental faculty but is itself constitutive of our true life. The intellect is that of which the mental faculty is one formal expression, one mode of its operation, but by no means the only one. As will be amply demonstrated in the sayings of the Imam below, the 'aql is multi-faceted, comprising aspects that in our times are rarely associated with intelligence. Being true to one's intellect — to the treasures buried deep within it, not merely to the rational functions operative on its surface — is tantamount to being spiritual. For Ali (ع), the true intellectual (al-'aqil, ⟪ar:العاقل⟫) is one who not only thinks correctly but also acts ethically, and, at the deepest level, one who seeks to realize the ultimate Reality. The intellectual is defined as one who 'puts all things in their proper place' (⟪ar:العاقل من وضع شئی حق محلہ⟫) — one who aligns his acts with the intentions of his Creator. As will be seen in Chapter 2, this is the very definition of justice (⟪ar:عدل⟫) as well; only the true intellectual can therefore be fully just (⟪ar:عادل⟫), for only one who sees things as they truly are is able to put them in their right place. Thought, action, and realization are all the concern of the true intellectual, the 'aqil. Correct thinking, impeccable virtue, and authentic being are inextricably bound up with the intellect in the spiritual ethos considered here. These aspects of the ethos come into sharper focus in the light of the Imam's discourse to Kumayl, to which we now turn.

### The Discourse To Kumayl

This discourse has been the subject of numerous commentaries through the centuries. Its importance lies, among other things, in its highly esoteric character. The Imam mysteriously takes one of his disciples, Kumayl, by the hand and leads him outside Kufa to a graveyard; when the city lies behind them he draws a deep breath and imparts the following private counsel.

يَا كُمَيْل بْن زِيَادٍ، إِنَّ هذهِ الْقُلُوبَ أَوْعِيَةٌ فَخَيْرُهَا أَوْعَاهَا فَاحْفَظْ عَنِّي مَا أَقُولُ لَكَ۔ النَّاسُ ثَلاَثَةٌ- فَعَالِمٌ رَبَّانِيٌّ وَمُتَعَلِّمٌ عَلَى سَبِيلِ نَجَاةٍ، وَهَمَجٌ رَعَاعٌ أَتْبَاعُ كُلِّ نَاعِقٍ يَمِيلُونَ مَعَ كُلِّ رِيحٍ، لَمْ يَسْتَضِيئُوا بِنُورِ الْعِلْمِ، وَلَمْ يَلْجَؤُوا إِلَى رُكْنٍ وَثِيقٍ

O Kumayl ibn Ziyad, truly these hearts are vessels — containers for knowledge and wisdom — and the best of them are those which hold the most. So retain from me that which I say to you. People are divided into three types: a lordly knower ('alim rabbani); one who seeks knowledge (muta'allim) for the sake of deliverance; and the common folk (hamaj ra'a) — the word names the moth that hurls itself at any flame — following just anyone, swaying with every current, neither desiring to be illumined by the light of knowledge nor seeking refuge in any strong support.

<!-- page 23 -->

O Kumayl, knowledge is better than wealth, for knowledge guards you, while you must guard your wealth; and wealth diminishes as it is spent, while knowledge only increases as it is disbursed, replenishing itself the more it is given away; the fruits of wealth vanish when the wealth itself is gone — the friends bought with money, the comforts it purchased, all fall away with it — while no one can take a man's knowledge from him. O Kumayl ibn Ziyad, the wise apprehension of knowledge (ma'rifat al-'ilm) is a religion by which God is worshipped — for din, religion, is kin to dayn, a debt, and it is by acquiring knowledge that a man repays the debt his own intellect has incurred to its Maker. Through it man acquires obedience in his life and a good name after his death. And knowledge is a judge, while wealth is judged: knowledge is active and wealth passive, for the one who knows takes command of what he owns, his knowledge directing him how to handle it, whereas wealth is forever something ruled upon by its possessor.

O Kumayl ibn Ziyad, those who hoard wealth perish even while they live, but the knowers endure for as long as time subsists; their material forms are absent, but their images in the hearts of men are present. Ah, what abundant knowledge is here — and he pointed to his breast — would that I could find one to bear it. Yes, I found one who was quick to understand, but he could not be trusted with it, exploiting the tools of religion for the sake of worldly gain, empowering himself with the graces of God against His slaves and with His proofs against His friends; for when the vessel of the heart is corrupt, this knowledge — pure and precious as it is — becomes a weapon turned against the very friends of God. Then there was another, obedient to the bearers of truth but lacking heartfelt insight, in whom, at the first appearance of obscurity, doubt was kindled in the heart. So alas, neither this one nor that! Nor one who is greedy for pleasure and submissive to passion, nor one obsessed with acquisition and accumulation — neither of them is a guardian of religion in any respect. They resemble nothing so much as grazing cattle. Thus does knowledge die with the death of its bearers, when its trust falls to the worthless and the self-serving.

But indeed, O my God, the earth will never be empty of one who establishes the proof of God, whether overtly and in the open or fearfully and in obscurity, lest God's proofs and elucidations come to naught. Yet how many are such as these, and where are they? By God, they may be the smallest in number, but with God they are the greatest in rank — for here authority is not the possession of the many, and truth is not settled by the count of those who hold it; the guardians of the proof are few, and it is they who stand highest in the sight of God. Through them God preserves His proofs and elucidations, so that they may entrust them to their compeers and sow them in the hearts of those who resemble them. Through them, knowledge penetrates the reality of insight; they take hold of the very spirit of certainty; they make easy what the extravagant find harsh, and they befriend that by which the ignorant are estranged; with their bodies they keep company with the world, while their spirits are bound to the transcendent realm. They are the vicegerents of God upon His earth, the summoners to His religion. Ah, how I long to see them! Go now, Kumayl, as you will.

<!-- page 24 -->

For our purposes here it suffices to reflect upon the four sentences of the penultimate paragraph, in which we are given the description of those who bear the title of lordly knower (alim rabbani): those who are fewest in number yet greatest in the sight of God, and through whom His religion is sustained. His religion — that is, the divine reality of religion, the pure and immutable essence of the faith, perfect and unchanging — as against religion in the ordinary sense, whose formal institutions are subject to the vicissitudes of human history. From Adam to the Seal of the prophets those outward institutions have shifted with the conditions of men, but the essence, the message, and the purpose have never altered: the salvation of the soul through willing submission to God, as the Quran declares, ⟪ar:إِنَّ ٱلدِّينَ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْإِسْلَمُ⟫ — "Truly, religion in the sight of God is submission" (3:19). It is by means of the knowledge of the true knowers that religion is true to itself, and only through such sages that religion truly lives in the world.

"Through them, knowledge penetrates the reality of insight." He does not say that their knowledge penetrates it, but that through them knowledge penetrates the reality of insight. Knowledge, as we noted, can remain merely abstract, far removed from the reality of insight; there is the outward, surface knowledge of a thing — its zahir — and the inner, hidden knowledge of the same thing — its batin. Yet knowledge contains a potential that calls out to be realized; it needs to be enlivened so as to penetrate to something deeper than the words and concepts that articulate its own outward form, the meaning that lies ready on the surface. A man may have a surface knowledge of a verse of the Quran, of a saying, of why he prays and fasts and how many cycles each prayer requires; but when he presses past that surface — asking what the bowing and the prostration truly represent — a world of knowledge opens that is wholly unlike the bare information he began with. Degrees of knowledge are plainly implied here, and with them the avoidance of all literalism and superficiality, of the facile equation of factual information with actual knowledge, let alone with wisdom. The shift of consciousness required for this penetration is shown graphically by the Imam's gesture toward his breast: for it is not the chest but the heart hidden behind it that holds knowledge; the chest is the outward form, the zahir that conceals the heart within its batin, and to reach the heart one must first penetrate the chest.

<!-- page 25 -->

The word chosen to translate hajama is "penetrate," but the primary sense of the verb is to assault, to take by storm, to charge. In the warfare of that age the battle lines were drawn as a right wing, a left wing, and a center — the heart of the army (qalb al-'askar) — where the king or commander stood; the aim of the assault was to break through the wings and reach that heart, for once the king was taken the battle was won and the victor took command of all the resources of the defeated: the kingdom, its treasury, its army, its people. The chessboard preserves the same rule. So the word helps us to see that there is in conceptual knowledge a latent force — one might almost call it an explosive power — deriving from the reality of knowledge, its haqiqa or spiritual essence. It is this that calls out to be made real through tahaqquq, realization in depth rather than mere verification on the surface; and the striving by which one storms the outer wall of words to reach the heart of their meaning is tahqiq, investigation. This haqiqa can be indicated, but never captured, by the words and concepts that make up its formal, communicable aspect, just as the chest can only be opened to reach the heart. By means of this inner force — which its outer conceptual form at once hides and evokes — knowledge is able to effect a shift of consciousness from one degree of knowledge to another: a man begins to see the very realities of this world differently from those who rest in the surface play of cause and effect, so that the same act which is a burden to one is a delight to another. An indirect, mental conception of reality thus paves the way for a penetrating, existential perception of reality. This is the difference between mere sight and true insight: sight reaches only the outward, superficial reality, whereas the reality of insight opens the intelligence to reality as such — ordinary insight, like abstract knowledge, remaining only at the superficial level of understanding. The same idea is expressed in a saying of Ali (ع) concerning the true understanding that comes to plumb, and so truly to know, the buried treasures of knowledge: "He who understands knows the depth of knowledge (man fahima 'alima ghawr al-'ilm)." And the effort that true knowledge demands, as against the mere accumulation of facts, is likewise indicated: "Through learning and assimilating knowledge, until it becomes deeply rooted in one's consciousness, is knowledge attained (bi'l-ta'allum yunalu'l-'ilm)."

The urgency of realizing this immanent power within knowledge — of moving from the abstract to the concrete, the potential to the actual, the static to the dynamic, the formal to the essential — is powerfully evoked by hajama, a word bound up with battle. It warns us against taking the first understanding of an idea for its full comprehension, and its full intellectual comprehension for its dynamic interiorization in the spirit. For when knowledge at last storms the surface of religion and takes the heart, a transformation begins from within: the heart, now illumined by the realities it has seized, takes command of the limbs and the actions — which are the soul's own resources — exactly as the victor takes command of the kingdom he has won. This interiorization is a process of infinite unfolding within, not the mere registering of some fact from without. It requires the effective integration of knowledge within being; knowledge from without must be transformed into being from within, woven into the deepest texture of one's existence. Knowledge thus demands not merely the attention of the mind but the total orientation of the soul.

<!-- page 26 -->

In this ethos, then, the effective integration (tawhid) of knowledge within being is unattainable without the participation of the whole soul; and this in turn requires the elimination of all the vices that flow from egocentricity. Thus the 'aqil, the true intellectual, is described by the Imam as one constantly engaged in struggle against his own soul — against the lower inclinations, the passionate whims, and the distracting caprices of the ego. All these vices, far from being confined to the plane of morality, encroach upon the domain of knowledge, and this is understood in the very measure that knowledge is grasped as a concrete vision of things rather than a merely notional or conceptual apprehension. The vices are then seen as so many clouds obscuring the vision proper to the reality of insight. According to the Quran, evil actions become a rust that covers over the heart: "Nay, their hearts have become rusted by that which they have done" (83:14). The 'aqil, then, is engaged in a constant inner struggle against the tendencies that not only sully the soul but blind the heart. As a virtuous man he opposes vice on the moral plane, because it is contrary to virtue; but vice is opposed for a further reason, situated on the higher plane of spiritual reality — it contradicts the nature of the Real, it obscures the reality of insight, blinding the eye of the heart which alone can see God.

This is not a question of denying the validity of the moral perspective; the stress falls rather upon the deeper motivation that propels the 'aqil toward virtue, a motivation that transcends the moral plane while comprising it. Morality emerges here as the fruit or effect, and spirituality as the seed or cause. By drawing attention to the intellectual aspect of the 'aqil's motive for shedding the vices, the Imam shows more clearly how immorality is rooted in ignorance; and the uprooting of that ignorance, for the true intellectual, is tantamount to dissolving the very substance of the vices. This uprooting demands great effort, a relentless battle within — indeed the most formidable of all battles, al-jihad al-akbar, the greatest struggle, as the Prophet defined it.

One should take careful note here of the following sayings of Ali (ع) on the importance of this inner struggle:

"Struggling against the soul through knowledge — such is the mark of the intellect."


"The strongest of people are those who are strongest against their own souls."

"Truly, one who fights his own soul in obedience to God, and does not sin against Him, has the rank of the righteous martyr in the sight of God."

"The ultimate battle is that of a man against his own soul."

"He who knows his soul fights it."

"No jihad is more excellent than the jihad of the soul."

In another saying the Imam clarifies what the Prophet meant by defining the greatest jihad as the jihad of the soul (al-nafs). It is not so much a matter of fighting against the soul as of the battle for the soul itself. This emerges from the metaphor by which the Imam defines the struggle: al-'aql, the intellect, is the leader of the forces of al-Rahman, the Compassionate; al-hawa — whim, caprice, desire — commands the forces of al-shaytan, the devil; and al-nafs, the soul, vacillates between them, drawn by both (mutajadhiba baynahuma), falling under the sway of whichever of the two prevails.
