These sayings make clear that for the true intellectual the soul in its entirety must be "conquered" and enlisted on the side of the intellect. The aim is not to destroy the lower soul but to free it from the destructive influence of its caprice — its egoistic whims, its subjectivism, its individualism — all of which are connoted by the word hawa. The Prophet, the Qur'an tells us, does not speak from hawa; rather: "It is naught but a revelation revealed" (53: 3–4). The divine objectivity of revelation is here contrasted with the human subjectivity of passional whim. In the context of the holy war within, the Imam's sayings bring an analogous polarity into focus: the objectivity of the intellect must prevail over the subjectivity of individual caprice and egotistic desire. It is the intellect that represents — in the sense of "makes present" — the divine power of al-Rahman, and that combats the forces of al-shaytan as embodied in al-hawa.

The word hawa is used further in the Qur'an to denote desires that spring from the ego, by which the ego becomes a kind of false god: "Hast thou seen him who maketh his desire his god?" (25: 43; almost identical at 45: 23). This is the subtle or hidden idolatry (al-shirk al-khafi) that the Prophet described as being more hidden than a black ant crawling on a dark stone in a moonless night. To eliminate this idolatry of the ego, a force higher than the ego itself must be brought to bear, since it is impossible for the ego to be controlled by anything belonging to the ego; and that higher force is the divine Reality, access to which is made possible through the power of the intellect and the graces of faith. This complementarity between the effort to overcome the egoism of hawa and the influx of divine grace is well expressed in the following Qur'anic verse: "As for he who fears the station of his Lord, and restraineth his soul from whim (hawa), verily, Paradise will be his abode" (79: 40–41).

The soul's fundamental energy is not to be destroyed but converted and redirected — away from the transient objects of individualistic desire and the impulses of al-shaytan, and toward the one true object expressed by al-Rahman. This "conquest" of the soul involves far more than arriving at some mental conception. It is not only the mind, the will, and the character that need to be transformed; the soul's very deepest disposition must be integrated into the truth perceived by the intellect, if one is to be capable of perceiving that truth in all its dimensions and ramifications across the different planes of being.

It is important to stress that for the Imam the need to win the soul over in its totality follows naturally from his understanding of tawhid, the most fundamental principle of Islam. Tawhid is a verbal noun, and its translation as mere oneness or unity fails to capture its full force: it literally means "affirming one" — the affirmation of and belief in God's oneness, which is the outward, theological meaning — or "realizing one" — the realization that there is but one reality, which is the inner, spiritual meaning. In the Imam's perspective, therefore, the absolutely Real is perceived not only as being one but also as making one, as integrating. The Real is not only unique; it is also unifying. Tawhid thus comes to mean not merely affirming the oneness of God, but being one oneself so as to reflect the One perfectly — to know God is to be "integrated." Here one observes a link with the etymology of 'aql, whose primary meaning is "to bind." Yet whereas the later mystical tradition tends to emphasize the limitative aspect of this word — the intellect as that which defines and encloses meaning, in contrast to the heart, which intuits and discloses spiritual truth — here the intellect is that which "binds together" the disparate elements of the soul into an organic unity. This principle is expressed in the first of the following series of the Imam's sayings, each of which merits deep reflection:

"He who knows God integrates himself (man 'arafa'Llah tawahhad)."

"He who knows his soul disengages himself (man 'arafa nafsahu tajarrad)."

"He who knows people isolates himself (man 'arafa'l-nas tafarrad)."

"He who knows the world withholds himself (man 'arafa'l-dunya tazahhad)."

Elsewhere this total adherence to the Real is described by the Imam as "sincerity" — sidq or ikhlas — terms that denote far more than simple truthfulness, as is already evident from the root of ikhlas. The verb khalusa means to be utterly pure, true to one's essence, unadulterated by any extraneous element. This dimension of the meaning comes through clearly in the following saying of the Imam: "Make purely for God (akhlis li'Llah) your action and your knowledge, your love and your hatred, your taking and your leaving, your speech and your silence."

To be totally sincere, then, means to make oneself "purely" for God — to have no "stain" of impurity, understood here as whatever is other than God, in one's devotion, action, intention, or even sentiment. One must love and hate only in relation to one's bond with God. Of all the tendencies that insinuate themselves into one's motivations and intentions, the most pernicious is egotism. Ikhlas — pure sincerity — involves the active effort to be, think, and act purely for God, that is, with selflessness, which is why the Imam places such stress on the holy war within the soul as the "ultimate battle." One who wins this battle attains the rank of the "righteous martyr," for the victor here is a martyr in the truest sense: he has sacrificed his lower life for the sake of God, with whom is the real life — the life witnessed by the intellect, the only element within human consciousness capable of looking upon the soul as "the other" with genuine objectivity.

To repeat: "struggling against the soul through knowledge — such is the mark of the intellect." The intellectual engaged in this battle is also one who bears "witness" (al-shahid, meaning both martyr and witness, as in the original Greek sense of the word). He bears witness to God through the intellect's sacrifice of whatever opposes the truth of God — and the chief opponent is his own egotism, his own "false life." To possess perfect ikhlas, then, is to be rid of all hypocritical self-preoccupation and to replace it with a sincere theocentricity, a mode of being centred upon God rather than oneself. Ikhlas, therefore, means being for God, or belonging to God, in the sense indicated by the Qur'anic verse: "Say: Truly my worship and my sacrifice, and my living and my dying, are for God, Lord of the universes" (6: 162).

The fact that worship is the first thing mentioned in this verse is of particular significance for anyone seeking to understand Ali (ع)'s spiritual ethos. What must be emphasized in presenting this ethos is the indispensable role of worship and devotion. In our second chapter, this role will become evident in the context of the quest for the fundamental virtue of justice. The ultimate fruits of worship and devotion — of which the quintessence is the remembrance of God — will be the subject of Chapter 3. One of the defining features of the Imam's spiritual ethos is indeed the emphasis he places on prayer, taking that term in its widest sense to include all the means — from formal worship and supplication to meditation and reflection, remembrance and contemplation — by which the individual is oriented and attuned to the Real, al-Haqq.

Worship is therefore an integral aspect of ikhlas, precisely because it relates to and sustains a fundamental dimension of the human soul. That within the soul which yearns to worship, and is nourished only by worship, must be given its due. In a spiritual context, if worship is marginalized, one's sincerity is compromised: one is no longer being sincere with God, no longer being "just" to God, no longer rendering God His due. And the spiritual logic of the Imam holds that if one is not just with God, one cannot be just to His creatures. "Whoso establishes well-being between himself and God," he says, "God establishes well-being between him and mankind."

One might also cite here the Qur'anic verse: "Worship thy Lord until certainty cometh to thee" (15: 99). The quest for knowledge demands total dedication if it is to yield its inner riches, and that dedication includes the quasi-alchemical transformation of consciousness wrought by total concentration on the divine Reality — prayer in one of its deepest modes. In this form of prayer, the intensity of dedication is such that nothing else can occupy one's consciousness alongside its object: the "remembrance" (dhikr) of God strictly implies the forgetting of everything else, including, above all, oneself. As noted above, the spiritual reality of the dhikr can only be attained when "you forget your soul in your remembrance." Concentration upon the divine Reality as the supreme object of knowledge — rendered present through the Name or Names of God — is thus a mode of prayer, one which, to be efficacious, requires both presence of mind and a genuine engagement of the will. It is far from sufficient to pray absent-mindedly or mechanically. In this connection the Imam says, "He who prays without exertion is like one who shoots [arrows] without a bow." Equally, there is nothing passive or easy about acquiring knowledge: "Knowledge calls out for action; if it is answered [it is of avail], otherwise it departs."

The same idea is expressed more forcefully in this saying of the Imam: "When you hear knowledge [being imparted] cleave to it, do not mix it up with jest, lest hearts spew it forth." Knowledge that is not taken seriously will be of no avail; it remains "undigested" and cannot be assimilated by the "heart," that innermost faculty which has access to the "reality of insight" and which can thus "see" God. For it is the heart that sees Allah — not by eyesight, not by ⟪ar:بصارۃ⟫, but through the spiritual insight, the ⟪ar:بصیرۃ⟫, generated by the true principles of faith, the ⟪ar:حقائق الایمان⟫. Indeed, it is not the individual who "assimilates" knowledge to himself; it is knowledge — or rather the reality of being expressed through true knowledge — that assimilates the individual to itself: "Acquire knowledge, and [true] life will acquire you." The relationship between life and consciousness, so central to the intrinsic function of the spirit as discussed earlier, is again at the heart of this maxim. At first, the individual seeks knowledge and cultivates consciousness; then, life "seeks" the individual as being makes itself more fully real within him. Put differently, the individual first makes an active effort to integrate knowledge within his being; but at a certain point, this activity is transformed into passive receptivity — being, "true life," integrates the individual within itself. To return to the Imam's guidance to Kumayl: "Through them knowledge penetrates the reality of insight." It is not the knowers who penetrate; it is knowledge that penetrates to the reality of insight, doing so through them, and this in the very measure of their effacement within knowledge — an effacement that is the shadow cast by their effective integration within the life that "acquires" them.

"They rejoice in their intimacy with the spirit of certainty."

The next sentence in the discourse to Kumayl is of particular importance for appreciating the sheer happiness that flows from the "life of the spirit." In the Arabic expression wa basharu ruh al-yaqin, the primary meaning of the word bashara is to have intimate experience of something, to come into direct physical contact with it. This form of the verb also carries connotations of joy; hence "rejoice in intimate contact" better conveys something of what this single word expresses. To touch the "spirit of certainty" is not to arrive at some cold logical fact, the conclusion of a purely mental process; rather, this contact with the spirit of certainty means entering deeply into the very object of one's certainty — ultimately nothing less than the divine itself. Since this reality is infinitely more beatific than any conceivable paradisal bliss — for the Qur'an affirms that the divine ridwan (beatitude, contentment) is "greater" than Paradise (9: 72) — it cannot but bestow joy upon the soul that opens itself effectively to it. In the words of Ali (ع), "Learning and reflecting upon knowledge is the delight of the knowers."

This is what one might call an "epistemology of joy," for "the most joyous of mankind is the intellectual (al-aqil)." This joyous epistemology — which stands in sharp contrast to modern scientistic conceptions of knowledge as dry, cold, and abstract — deserves careful consideration, for it reveals a crucial dimension of the spiritual knowledge proper to the Imam's perspective, and indeed to all spiritual traditions worthy of the name. Engaging with truths ought to impart happiness, and it does so in the measure that the truths in question are fundamental to one's own being or to Being as such. These two modes of being are ultimately identical, given the uniqueness — the "one-and-onliness" — of Being. That which has no second, no other, cannot enter the category of number; being is indivisible. Hence the being of the individual cannot ultimately differ from Being as such, however great the existential disparity — or rather, incommensurability — between the individual and God. True knowledge is knowledge of Being; it is therefore inevitably a form of self-discovery, even though knowledge of the Absolute is impossible without absolute self-abandonment. The highest discovery calls for the deepest effacement.

In the initial stages of this process, however, true knowledge as acquired by the intellectual is a kind of self-discovery that brings the happiness of a homecoming — a return from the exile of ignorance. This follows because "the most intellectual of people" is "the one who is closest to God," and "the one who is most alive." To be close to God is to be fully alive, and this true life gives profound joy — the joie de vivre, the sheer joy of living. It is a joy proper to existence as such and, as will be seen below, independent of particular experiences that may or may not be pleasurable.

Here again we see the crucial relationship between life and consciousness: between the fruits of the "in-spiration" of God's spirit on one hand, and between this life of spiritual consciousness and joy on the other. Those who are true to the spirit of God within them genuinely possess joy. It is also by reason of their intimate and unshakeable certainty of the divine goodness and compassion that the true faqih — not so much "the jurist" as "one who truly understands" — is defined by the Imam as "he who does not make people despair of the mercy of God, and does not make them lose hope in the gracious spirit of God (rawh Allah)." Such is the sage's immersion in the joyous certainty of God's mercy that there is no room for despair. To despair of this mercy is itself a mark of unbelief: "...and who despaireth of the mercy of his Lord except those who stray in error?" (15: 56). The divine assurances of mercy in the Qur'an — "My mercy encompasseth all things" (7: 156) and "thy Lord has prescribed for Himself mercy" (6: 54) — come fully alive in the souls that possess a profound and not merely notional knowledge of the beatific nature of the divine Reality. These are the ones who truly "understand," the true fuqaha'.


<!-- page 56 -->
This opening to joy through intimate knowledge of God's mercy is inseparable from beauty, the perception of which is another unexpected concomitant of the intellect in the Imam's perspective. The appreciation of beauty is not merely a matter of subjective aesthetics; it is an objective dimension of the intellect's own function. Beauty also leads directly to the heart of ethics. The Arabic *ihsan* is often translated simply as 'virtue' or 'excellence', but one of its key literal meanings is 'making beautiful'—*husn* means 'beauty'. Virtue, *ihsan*, can thus be understood as that which 'makes the soul beautiful'. In this light, the relationship among intellect, ethics, and aesthetics becomes far clearer: 'The excellence of the intellect,' says Ali (ع), 'is in the beauty of things outward and inward (*jamal al-zawahir wa'l-bawatin*).' The outward beauty of forms and actions must be complemented by inward beauty—beauty of soul, or virtue in its most integral and penetrating sense. All of this follows naturally from two sayings attributed to the Prophet: 'Truly God is beautiful and He loves beauty,' and: 'Assume the character traits of God.'

This cultivation of 'beautiful traits' within oneself makes one lovable to God, since He 'loves beauty'. The process of making oneself lovable to God is inseparable from emulating the virtues brought to perfection in the soul of the Prophet. 'Say: O ye who believe, if ye love God, follow me [the Prophet]; God will love you' (3: 31). This drawing of God's love through making oneself beautiful in the fullest sense is alluded to in another saying of the Imam: 'The foundation of the intellect is the assimilation of love (*awwal al-'aql al-tawaddud*).' Rather than referring simply to love as such (*wudd/mawadda*), the Imam draws attention to the need to make oneself lovable to God by using the form *tawaddud*, alluding thereby to the process of integrating and realizing the principle of love within oneself, as well as expressing outwardly the love that is experienced inwardly—love being appreciated at once as outward radiance and inner attraction.

To 'follow' the Prophet, and thus make oneself receptive to the divine influx of love, means, among other things, cultivating the 'prophetic' virtues. These virtues are prophetic not in the sense that they belong exclusively to the Prophet, but because he is held to have realized virtue in all its plenitude and is therefore the exemplar, par excellence, of virtue as such. It is therefore unsurprising to find that beauty and love are not the only qualities that articulate the intellect's receptivity to the divine Reality; kindness and contentment,120 courtesy,121 generosity and modesty,122 and wise forbearance (*hilm*) are equally essential. None of these moral qualities is absent from the soul of the true intellectual. There can be no wisdom or intellect in the fullest sense if the soul lacks them. It is precisely the presence of these virtues that enables the intellect to develop the dimensions of depth and breadth without which it remains confined to the flat, horizontal plane of its own operations—confined, that is, to the activity of reason alone, which, until touched by faith and deepened by grace, remains unaware of what lies beneath the surface of its own field of activity, oblivious of the 'hidden treasures' of the intellect, and blind to the need to 'integrate' the whole soul within the mould of that 'integral', or 'unifying', virtue which bestows integrity upon intelligence. Such integration—this 'making one'—is absolutely fundamental to *tawhid*, now grasped as a dynamic process of spiritual unification demanded by the very idea of God's oneness. Truth must clearly inspire the soul; but it is equally necessary that, conversely, virtue should 'aerate' the intelligence, providing the 'leaven' that allows it to expand to its full dimensions.

<!-- page 57 -->
Sinful, immoral qualities are understood in this perspective not only as vices but as so many blind spots and intellectual dysfunctions. The heart is 'diseased' by vice; its 'eye' is blinded by them and thus rendered unable to 'see' the Real. As the Qur'an says: 'In their hearts is a disease and God increased them in disease' (2: 10), and 'It is not the sights that are blind, but blind are the hearts' (22: 46).

As noted above, the vices are all comprised within egotism, which is in turn often described simply as 'caprice' (*hawa*). In one saying, the Imam refers to the conquest of caprice as tantamount to salvation: 'He attains deliverance whose intellect dominates his caprice.' Deliverance—*falah*, which carries the sense both of salvation and beatitude—is thus to some degree accessible here and now; it is not a grace granted only posthumously. This deliverance involves tasting the joy that emanates from certainty, from intimate contact with the spirit of that certainty. As for the means of cultivating receptivity to this spirit of certainty, what is required—over and above obedience to the rules and regulations of the revealed Law—is that combination of purity of intention and sincerity of devotion expressed by the word *ikhlas*. Without this sincerity or purity, compliance with formal rules is nothing but hypocrisy (*nifaq*) and pretension (*riya*), which the Imam in turn defines as the gravest of all sins, *shirk*—the attribution of 'partners' to God: 'Know that the slightest pretension (*riya*) is polytheism.' This sentence can be read as a commentary on one of the Qur'an's most important condemnations of hypocrisy. The verses in question refer to those who deny the essence of faith while outwardly conforming to it:

Hast thou seen the one who belieth religion? He is the one who repelleth the orphan and urgeth not the feeding of the poor. So woe be to those who pray, those who are heedless of their prayer, who want only to be seen, and withhold small kindnesses (107: 1-7).

This short sura makes clear that formal acts of devotion have no value whatsoever unless they are integrated into a life of virtue, and unless they are performed with the right intention—that is, with *ikhlas*. It also helps explain why the Imam's ethos is described here not merely as 'religious' but as 'spiritual': it takes the ideals of religion to their most profound application. To be spiritual in this sense is precisely to be true to one's intellect—true to the treasures buried deep within it, and not merely to the rational functions operating on its surface. Without this dimension of depth, religion is meaningless. 'There is no religion for one who has no intellect,' says the Imam. Likewise: 'Action without knowledge is error.' What counts is the intention motivating action, for 'action—all action—is dust, except what is purified [accomplished sincerely] within it.' This detachment from action as such, and the orientation instead toward an inner state of being from which good actions cannot but emerge, clearly reflects the Qur'anic teaching: 'It is not their [sacrificed] flesh nor their blood that reacheth God, but it is piety from you that reacheth Him' (22: 37).

<!-- page 58 -->
'There is no religion for one who has no intellect.' No group perhaps better exemplified the principle in this saying than the Kharijites. Among those who rebelled against the Imam were numerous 'readers' (*qurra'*, sing. *qari'*) of the Qur'an—people who enjoyed considerable religious prestige on account of their Qur'anic knowledge and intense worship. Their piety, however, was more apparent than real; it was, in fact, a 'zeal of bitterness', a caricature of true piety. When his supporters mentioned the long night vigils performed by members of this group, the Imam gave a sharp reply: 'Sleeping with certainty is better than praying with doubt.' Certainty, as we have seen, imparts peace and joy; thus sleep, for one immersed in what the Imam calls 'the spirit of certainty', is a bodily repose that only deepens the happiness flowing from a good conscience and spiritual certitude—the latter understood as a kind of permanent 'wakefulness', in the sense of being awake to higher realities. Bodily sleep does not, therefore, contradict spiritual wakefulness. As the hadith of the Prophet has it: 'My eyes sleep, but my heart sleeps not.'

In relation to formal worship, the true intellectual—the *'aqil*, whom the Imam defines as the one who puts each thing in its proper place (العاقل من وضع شئی حق محلہ)—is one who obeys the divine injunction to pray. But rather than praying in mechanical fashion and supposing that the action alone suffices, he knows that such obedience is a necessary but not sufficient condition for achieving receptivity to grace. There must be consciousness of what one is doing and why one is doing it; it is this awareness, together with virtue—'inward beauty'—that attracts divine acceptance. 'The most worthy knowledge for you,' says the Imam, 'is that by virtue of whose presence action is accepted by God.'

'They make easy what the extravagant find harsh; they befriend that by which the ignorant are estranged.'

This sentence takes us, among other things, to the impact on the soul of true sages made by recollection of the inevitability of death and the consequent necessity of renunciation within this world. In the first place, what the extravagant—the avid, worldly types—find 'harsh' is outward adversity, difficulty, and deprivation. These are all circumstances which the Qur'an views as the trials that inevitably accompany life in this world:

And We shall try you with something of fear and hunger and loss of property and life and fruits; but give glad tidings to the patient—those who, when struck by affliction say: 'Verily, we belong to God and verily unto Him we are ever-returning.' These are the ones upon whom there are blessings from their Lord, and mercy; and these are the ones who are rightly guided. (2: 155-157)

<!-- page 59 -->
The wise, the rightly-guided (*al-muhtadun*), perceive the 'blessings' showered upon those who are patient in the face of affliction—a patience that is itself a concomitant of their knowledge of the truth: 'By the Age, truly man is in a state of loss—except those who believe and do virtuous deeds and exhort one another to truth and exhort one another to patience' (103: 1-3). These are the believers who remain steadfast in the face of trials, firmly rooted in their awareness of their origin and end, and in the knowledge that 'unto Him we are ever-returning'. For this reason, they can 'make easy' the trials and difficulties of life, whereas the 'extravagant' experience them solely in their aspect of harshness and bitterness. The 'ease' that inheres in every difficulty is hidden from the ignorant but discerned by the wise: 'Verily, with hardship cometh ease; verily with hardship cometh ease' (94: 5-6). The wise go even further than merely anticipating 'ease' as the inevitable fruit of hardship; they taste this fruit already as the seed hidden within hardship itself, for they actually 'befriend that by which the ignorant are estranged'.

One level of meaning here is illuminated by this saying of Ali (ع): 'Death is more intimate to me than the breast of the mother is to the suckling babe.' In the verse cited above, the mention of trials and difficulties is preceded by a reminder that those slain 'in the path of God' are not truly dead—'nay, they are alive but ye perceive not.' This is not simply an affirmation of the life hereafter; it is also an allusion to the esoteric meaning of being 'slain' for God: *al-fana' fi'Llah*, 'extinction in God'. The Imam's intimacy with death can be understood in relation to this 'death' while still alive, which he also alludes to in another saying: 'I am the death of the dead.' Because life in the world is itself a kind of death—a limitation, and hence a privation, in contrast to the infinite plenitude of the divine realms—dying to this death is tantamount to true life. 'And the life of this world is nothing but sport and play,' says the Qur'an, 'and verily, the abode of the Hereafter—that is [true] life, did they but know' (29: 64).

<!-- page 60 -->
It is important to stress, however, that it is not the physical world as such that is to be negated; rather, it is worldliness—egotistic attachment to the world—that must be overcome. Here we must dwell on one of the key principles of the Imam's perspective, one stressed repeatedly throughout the *Nahj al-balagha*: the principle of *zuhd*, which no single English word can render adequately. It comprises several meanings—asceticism, austerity, abstention, detachment, 'doing without', and 'making little of'. The following passage from the *Nahj* conveys well the attitude of the *zahid* toward this world:

The world is an abode for which annihilation is ordained, and for its people departure from it is decreed. It is sweet and green, and it hastens to one who seeks [it], and confounds the heart that gazes upon it. So set out from it with the best of the provision available to you, and ask not from it above what suffices and seek not from it more than what can be attained.136

What is this 'best of provision' (*zad*)? In many places in the *Nahj* it is described as *taqwa*—another word that resists simple translation, its meaning encompassing piety, fear of God, awe, and God-consciousness: 'I exhort you, O slaves of God, to *taqwa*, which is the provision,' says the Imam. This provision for the next world is attained only in the measure of one's detachment from the perishable aspects of 'the life of this world', for 'whoso desireth that [life of the world] which hasteneth away, in that [life] We hasten for him what We will ...' (17: 18).

The despicable aspect of the world is described in vivid terms by the Imam: 'By God, this world of yours is more contemptible in my eyes than a [chewed-up] bone of a pig in the hand of a leper'; 'an image of the world is the snake—soft to touch, but virulent poison within';139 the world is 'as worthless to me as that which drips from the nose of a goat.' It is the grandeur and beauty of God that renders the world insignificant and distasteful; it is only in the light of the Absolute that the pleasures and attractions of the world appear fleeting and illusory: 'The greatness of the Creator for you belittles creatures in your view.' In a similar vein, the Imam speaks of a 'brother in God': 'What made him great in my eyes was the smallness of the world in his (*kana yu'azzimuhu fī 'aynī sighar al-dunya fi 'aynihi*).'142

<!-- page 61 -->
This, according to the Qur'anic usage of *zahid*, is the real meaning of *zuhd*. In the sura named after Joseph (*Yusuf*), the brothers of Yusuf 'deemed him of little value' in selling him for a small price (12: 20); they are described in relation to him as '*zahidin*'. In the Imam's perspective, the world is likewise deemed of little value—not altogether valueless, but rendered all but insignificant in relation to the Hereafter, to eternity, to ultimate reality. All created things are reduced to infinitesimal, fleeting shadows destined for annihilation: 'Everything perishes except His Face' (28: 88). Yet the world is not deprived of all value; indeed, its significance lies precisely in what it cannot help but signify: 'And there is nothing that does not hymn His glory with praise' (17: 44). Everything in the world, every creature in existence, proclaims the infinite creativity of the Creator. To help us imaginatively fathom the mystery of this universal praise, the Qur'an tells us: 'And the birds in flight—each one knoweth its prayer and glorification' (24: 41).

It is therefore not at all contradictory for the Imam to despise the world and yet wax lyrical in his descriptions of the beauties of virgin nature, including that unsurpassable marvel, the peacock. This comes in a sermon that also includes descriptions of birds in general, as well as ants, serpents, and elephants. It is noteworthy that the sermon concludes with a galvanizing description of Paradise, juxtaposing the marvels of this world with the delights of the next; in the light of the latter, all terrestrial phenomena pale into insignificance. For once the 'eye of the heart' is directed toward Paradise, 'your soul would become averse to the marvels of this world—its passions, its pleasures and its embellished scenery'.

It might seem contradictory to bring together such apparently opposed qualities as joy in certainty, discussed above, and the 'befriending' of hardship. But the latter is in fact the fruit of the former. All difficulties without exception are endured through the spiritual resources made available by faith combined with intellectual assimilation—or rather, in the measure that faith is intellectually assimilated and thus becomes *ma'rifa* or gnosis, comprising a certainty so deep and so complete that 'even if the veil be lifted' it cannot increase, according to the famous saying noted earlier. It is possible to believe, at one level of the mind, that 'there is no god but God'. The Imam, however, draws attention to what this apparently simple belief should imply when it is 'sincere' or 'pure': 'I bear witness that there is no god but God alone Who has no partner—a testimony whose sincerity has been tested and whose essence has been assimilated with conviction (*mu'taqadan musaffaha*).'


The word *musaffa* refers to the choicest and purest part of any substance — its very essence. The root *massa* means "to suck," the idea being that the liquid or juice of a substance or fruit holds its essence, that which is extracted from it. In the present context, the Imam is stressing the spiritual substance of the testimony, as distinct from mere verbal affirmation or mental comprehension of the words. He affirms a heartfelt connection with the reality to which the testimony bears witness; the words that define it are an echo of the reality they convey, and are not to be confused with that reality itself. He continues: "We cling to it always, for as long as we endure, storing it up [as protection] against the terrors that will befall us. For truly, it renders faith resolute, opens up spiritual virtue, pleases al-Rahman (the All-Compassionate), repels al-shaytan (the devil)."

Returning to "befriending that by which the ignorant are estranged," one might say that this points not only to the trials of life — which are a source of strength for the wise and a source of repulsion for the ignorant — but also to the ineluctability of death. For the wise, death means release, the encounter with God, blissful eternity — in short, a mode of tawhid, whereby the soul enjoys ever deeper degrees of integration within higher principles. For the ignorant, by contrast, death is perceived as a frightful disintegration, the end of their world rather than the beginning of endless life. One should also mention here the prefiguration of death in life already noted above: fana', "extinction," "effacement," the "perishing" of egoistic consciousness before the Absolute. The ignorant recoil in horror at the prospect of losing what they falsely take to be "their" life, not realizing that this sacrifice of egoistic and limited self-consciousness is the very ground on which infinite consciousness is glimpsed or "tasted." One can only "taste" the ocean of the Real by being drowned in it.

As we shall see in Chapter 3, one could even say that the means to this ultimate attainment is likewise embraced by the wise and feared by the ignorant — namely, the act of pure prayer, total concentration on the Real. This concentration strictly precludes all worldly or egotistic preoccupations; the sheer intensity of focus upon the Absolute burns away thoughts of the relative, and for those who ignorantly identify with relativities, both inward and outward, this amounts to a kind of death. For the wise, by contrast, this concentration on the Absolute — the unsullied "remembrance of God" — is a joyous contemplation of reality before which the ego is displaced and all illusions are dispelled. This leads directly to the next sentence of the passage: "With their bodies, they keep company with the world, while their spirits are tied to the transcendent realm."

This sentence becomes more concrete when read alongside another well-known expression of the Imam. He describes himself as belonging to a group whose spiritual degree is such that "their hearts are in the Gardens [of Paradise] while their bodies are at work." Being "tied" or "attached" to that realm — encompassing the paradisal abodes and extending up to the divine Presence itself — means that they are already "within" that transcendent realm more fully than they are within this earthly one. This helps explain the saying quoted earlier: God keeps man perpetually in that which his soul desires. Those whose hearts or spirits are "in" the transcendent domain are those whose consciousness is substantially — not merely conceptually — determined by transcendent realities. Those whose consciousness, by contrast, is shaped by worldly concerns are "in" this world in the sense of being enclosed by its formal limitations; their orientation and imagination are trapped within a worldly framework. Their actions bear the mark of this worldliness, whereas the actions of the wise are the fruits of their immersion in a higher reality. One might say that their outward actions in the world belie their inward transcendence of all action; they act, yet are not bound by the fruits of their action. As noted earlier, they know that "action — all action — is dust, except what is purified (accomplished sincerely) within it."

At this point it is worth pausing to consider the role the notion of the Hereafter plays within the Imam's ethical framework. As we saw above, and will see further in what follows, the Hereafter is repeatedly invoked in the Imam's discourses. Is this simply a matter of holding out a heavenly reward and threatening a hellish punishment in order to "enjoin the good and forbid the evil"? Even if the answer is partly affirmative, there is a deeper dimension to this apparently straightforward use of divine warnings and promises as levers for moral action.

The ethical orientation here is defined not merely by belief in God but by the spiritual reality of the Hereafter as well — both objectively, in itself, and subjectively, insofar as it is assimilated by the individual. It thereby becomes a dynamic dimension — a process, an orientation, a reflux — within the believer's own soul, rather than a bare belief or hope. Anticipation of the Hereafter characterizes every believer in principle; its realization characterizes the soul of the wali Allah, the friend of God, the saint and the sage. As noted earlier, the Imam speaks in a famous sentence that bears witness to his total immersion in the reality of which he is certain: "Were the veil to be removed, I would not increase in certitude (law kushifa'l-ghita', ma azdadtu yaqinan)." The anticipation — or better, the foretaste — of the Hereafter deepens, rather than diminishes, in proportion to one's virtue, piety, and sanctity; it does not become a merely heuristic device whose value is exhausted once its moral impact is achieved. The following moving passage from the Nahj deserves reflection in this light. What we have here is the description of a blissful vision of the reality of Paradise, not merely a rhetorical flourish in the service of moral exhortation:

If you were to cast the eye of your heart towards what is described for you of the Garden, your soul would become averse to the marvels of this world — its passions, its pleasures and its embellished scenery. And your soul would be rapt in contemplation of the swaying trees whose roots lie hidden in dunes of musk on the banks of the rivers of the Garden, and the clusters of dazzling pearls hanging down from the branches of its trees … 149

It is, of course, true that the highest degree of spirituality transcends the desire for Paradise and the fear of Hell, along with the moral conduct proportioned to them. But this means that when such higher degrees of realization are attained, the soul's state can properly be described as "paradisal" — that is, as being already so utterly content in the beatific presence of God that it desires nothing more. This state of soul is called in the Qur'an al-nafs al-mutma'inna (89: 27), "the soul at peace in absolute certainty."

The Imam frequently echoes, implicitly if not explicitly, the Qur'anic principle that "the Hereafter is better and more lasting" (87: 17). The Hereafter is not only the "time" at which divine warnings and promises reach their consummation; it is also the "space" in which all positive qualities — human, angelic, divine — are manifested in their fullness, those qualities that appear on earth as reflections of higher realities and that encompass and subtly penetrate the entire sphere of terrestrial existence. The Qur'an, perhaps more than any other scripture, furnishes the imagination with numerous and complex descriptions of paradisal bliss. One of the key functions of these descriptions is alluded to in the Qur'an itself: "Assuredly, We purify them with a pure quality — remembrance of the Abode (of the Hereafter)" (38: 46). The mere thought of Paradise is itself a purification of the mind and heart, a means of guarding the soul against the ever-present temptation to seek its ultimate happiness and well-being in this world alone. Conversely, one's well-being in the herebelow, in regard to action, lies in the performance of good deeds, and such well-being is proportioned to the depth of one's belief that "Whatever good ye send before for your souls, ye will find it with God, better and more tremendous as reward" (73: 20). In other words, a sincere orientation toward the Hereafter is transformed into a perception of heavenly beatitude already in the herebelow, in the form of those very acts and attitudes which, being good, share the same nature as the felicity to which they lead. In this light, morally good actions and noble intentions are grasped by one who sees beyond the veil of this world as vivid prefigurations of heavenly realities, not merely as ethical prerequisites for salvation. Goodness, in other words, does not simply lead to a heavenly reward; it already participates in that reward: "Is the reward of goodness (ihsan) aught but goodness?" (55: 60). The Imam's saying, "The fruit of good action is like its root," can be read as an echo of the idea that the end is already present in the means that lead to it. Finally, let us quote the following verse of the Qur'an, which provides a fitting close to this chapter and an introduction to the next:

And give good tidings to those who believe and perform virtuous deeds, that for them are Gardens underneath which rivers flow. Every time they are given to eat from the fruits thereof, they say: "This is what we were given to eat before." And they were given the like thereof (2: 25). The "fruits" of the paradisal gardens are thus experienced already in the herebelow, in the form of all goodness, beauty, and truth, and the diverse modes of happiness that flow from them. All such experiences are so many foretastes of the ultimate beatitude in Paradise. On the ethical plane, the performance of good deeds — and, even more directly, the realization of intrinsic virtue, ihsan — is therefore not simply a prerequisite for posthumous salvation, but is already a kind of deliverance, here and now: a deliverance from the imprisonment of sin on the outward plane, and from the bondage of egocentricity on the inward plane. This theme of salvation here and now, grounded in unshakeable certitude, is fundamental to the spirit of the Imam's teachings and will be explored further throughout the rest of this book. In the following chapter, the cardinal virtue of justice will be considered within Ali (ع)'s perspective, rooted as it is in ihsan — a virtue defined by the Prophet as worshipping God as if one saw Him, and concretely affirmed by the Imam as real and not merely hypothetical, for God is indeed "seen" by the pure of heart "through the verities of faith."


## A Sacred Conception of Justice in the Letter of Ali (ع) to Malik al-Ashtar

The discussion of justice in this chapter is not simply an exercise in theoretical speculation about what justice means in Ali (ع)'s letter to Malik al-Ashtar written fourteen centuries ago — the kind of definition one finds in a dictionary, where justice amounts to little more than fairness, equality, and fair play. The aim is, first and foremost, to bring the discussion of justice into alignment with the concept and reality of the sacred, the reality of the sacred One, ⟪ar:القدس⟫. On one hand, the principle of justice carries a sacred and spiritual dimension that contemporary discourse so often overlooks; on the other, a sensitive appreciation of the meaning of the sacred opens a deeper understanding of justice's roots within the divine nature itself, and a more profound awareness of why justice is necessary — in the world and in the soul. The basic argument of this essay is this: in Ali (ع)'s worldview, the ethical orientation toward justice is immeasurably deepened when it is consciously linked to the spiritual precepts of the Islamic faith. The spirit underlying moral rules and ethical values is strengthened by a commitment to transcendent principles — even though, or precisely because, those principles surpass the realm of action in which the rules and precepts themselves operate. To make this concrete, imagine the ⟪ar:شریعۃ⟫ as having a body and a spirit, just as we do: its body consists of various actions — prayer, fasting, the paying of ⟪ar:زکوۃ⟫ — but the spirit animating those actions rests on the nature of the divine, which is justice. Adherence to the ⟪ar:شریعۃ⟫ is therefore a training in becoming like the divine through the principles of justice, and so our actions must be complemented by contemplation of the divine. Contemplation and action are seen here as complementary, not contradictory, for this complementarity goes to the very heart of the Islamic message of tawhid, ⟪ar:توحید⟫ — an integrating oneness that makes theory and practice one, so that a person becomes a true ⟪ar:موحد⟫. This unity is embodied with dazzling clarity in the life of the Imam, as it is in the life of the Prophet. The harmony between the two principles — contemplation and action, being and doing, theory and practice, ideal and reality — pervades the remarkable document that is the subject of this essay.

Secondly, the reflections offered here are intended to be read against the background of contemporary ethical discourse and may be seen as a modest contribution, from an Islamic perspective, to the scholarly debates on virtue and ethics. In particular, this essay should be understood in light of one of the most serious and far-reaching critiques of modern and postmodern conceptions of virtue in general, and of justice in particular — that of Alasdair MacIntyre. Put simply, his critique holds that for several centuries ethical discourse in Western society has drifted far from its moorings in absolute values. He argues compellingly that debates about morality and ethics, together with their philosophical foundations, are no longer grounded in conceptions of 'the good life' or of 'the Good' as such. As a consequence, MacIntyre holds, various shades of relativism, nihilism, utilitarianism, and opportunism have emerged de facto as the shifting bases on which ethical values are formulated — if they are formulated at all. Relativism holds that knowledge, truth, and morality are not absolute but relative, existing only in relation to a culture, a society, or a historical context; nihilism rejects all religious and moral principles and takes life to be essentially meaningless; utilitarianism counts an action right only if it is useful or benefits a majority, so that good and bad rest on nothing more fundamental than a calculation of greater benefit or loss; and opportunism seizes whatever benefit circumstances offer, regardless of any plan or principle — the way of the manipulator, who flatters, performs, or deceives in order to get what he wants. Whatever value these human-made theories occasionally yield, none of it is rooted in the divine, and so even where they happen to mirror the values of faith they are baseless, without spirit — dead corpses. The practice of virtue on an ad hoc basis is not virtue properly speaking; it is a series of pragmatic adaptations to changing circumstances in the absence of any genuine principle of virtue. Take the virtue of truthfulness: if one resolves always to speak the truth without grasping the underlying reason for it — that truth is the very nature of our Creator, ⟪ar:الحق⟫, the absolute truth, and that our goal in this world is to take on the virtues of our Creator — then truthfulness will in the end become situational and self-serving, something to be compromised whenever circumstances seem to warrant it, spoken when it profits and abandoned when it costs. But once truth is seen as the nature of the divine, it becomes everlasting and eternal, indifferent to consequence, because one speaks it in emulation of the immortal nature of God; the virtue itself becomes immortal, and the need to practise it on an ad hoc basis falls away. 'We are looking,' writes MacIntyre, 'for a conception of the good which will enable us to order other goods, for a conception of the good which will enable us to extend our understanding of the purpose and content of the virtues, for a conception of the good which will enable us to understand the place of integrity and consistency in life ...'

It is at this level, in particular, that the insights of Ali (ع) and other sages of the Islamic tradition are especially relevant. Not only does MacIntyre's proposed solution to the crisis — the reformulation of an Aristotelian conception of ethics grounded in a Thomistic worldview — share a great deal with the Islamic tradition of ethical discourse (akhlaq); one can also argue that a careful engagement with the Imam's legacy yields far more than a merely theoretical approach to the question of justice, and this in two respects. First, the Imam was himself a paragon of justice and thus a living embodiment of this and other fundamental virtues. In his life one observes a perfect, indeed unflinching, adherence to this cardinal virtue — a life not governed by his ⟪ar:نفس⟫ but governed entirely by the divine nature of God. The remarkable actions we read about and marvel over emerge as the fruit of his irresistible attraction toward the nature of ⟪ar:الحق⟫, for his existence was perfectly oriented toward God; his focus was never on the action itself but on the source of the principles generating it, so that in fixing upon the beauty of the divine nature and striving to take it on, good actions arose from him naturally and inevitably. Although this work does not enter into many details of his life and its lessons, we will have occasion to refer to certain incidents and events that help illustrate this point. Secondly, and more importantly for our purposes, the discourses, letters, and sayings attributed to Ali (ع) offer a rich source of reflection on the dynamic spirituality that lies at the root of a life of virtue. One is afforded a glimpse into a way of life totally governed by the sacred — one in which the desire for the 'good life' emerges as the fruit of an irresistible attraction toward the Good as such, which is identical to the absolutely Real, al-Haqq.

In other words, one is ethically inspired by 'the Good' in direct proportion to one's existential orientation toward the Real. If moral rules are grounded in ethical perspectives, and if those perspectives are rooted in a conception of the 'good life', then that conception of the Good is itself derived from the moral agent's fundamental understanding of what life means. Amyn B. Sajoo, in a recent thoughtful work on Muslim ethics, draws a careful distinction between ethics and morals. He argues that ethical discourse is concerned more with conceptions of 'the good', while morals pertain more specifically to rules about right and wrong. Ethics deals with the soul and the spirit of the good, the ⟪ar:باطن⟫, and it is out of ethics that morals are born. 'Certainly,' he continues, 'the concepts overlap substantially in theory and practice; but it is well to bear in mind the more encompassing sense of reality that typifies ethics.' Situating ethics within the wider framework of spiritual virtue — in the sense of ihsan, ⟪ar:احسان⟫, as we shall see shortly — expands even further the compass of both ethics and morality, lending both types of discourse a dimension of existential depth and grounding them in a more organic and all-encompassing framework. For there are three components to the spiritual life: ⟪ar:اسلام⟫, which supplies the outer aspects of morality; ⟪ar:ایمان⟫, which is the framework of ethics; and the union of the two, serving as their underlying principle, which issues in ⟪ar:احسان⟫ — the good life, the beautiful life. Abstract questions of right and wrong come to be rooted in the more definitive principle that emerges in response to the question: what is reality?

We should bear in mind here the discussion in the previous chapter on the deeper meanings of divine unity. In the Imam's spiritual vision of tawhid, one perceives — according to one's capacity — an ultimate reality that not only 'has no second' but also has no moral 'opposite'. We tend to picture the world in terms of good against evil, God against ⟪ar:شیطان⟫, always thinking in opposites; but the Imam teaches that God has no opposite at all. ⟪ar:ابلیس⟫ does not stand against God, for nothing has the capacity to stand against God; he stands against God's creation, and his entire effort is to drive people away from their inherent divine nature by beautifying its opposite — beautifying arrogance where God's nature is modest, lewdness where it is decent, boastfulness where it is humble. For the goodness that is proper to God is not the moral opposite of evil; rather, it occupies an entirely different plane — that of al-Haqq, ⟪ar:الحق⟫, the absolutely Real, the only 'opposite' to which is unreality or nothingness. While goodness is at one with the pure positivity of the Absolute, evil derives solely from a negative capacity to negate the Real, the Good. In other words, the essence of all goodness and beauty is identified with the Creator, while evil is a modality of the created — the corrupted state of a creation that has deviated from the divine nature. This is strongly implied in the Qur'anic verse of ⟪ar:سورۃ الفلق⟫ which calls the believer to seek refuge in God from 'the evil of that which He created' (113: 2): قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلْفَلَقِ ۱ مِن شَرِّ مَا خَلَقَ ۲ — 'Say: I seek refuge with the Lord of the daybreak, from the evil of what He has created.' This verse is often mistranslated as 'from the evil which He created', which is wrong both theologically and lexically. The expression 'min sharri ma khalaqa', مِن شَرِّ مَا خَلَقَ, clearly refers to the evil that derives from creation, not to creation as such. In theological terms, God creates morally free agents and it is they who are the authors of evil in creation; when creation chooses, through its own free will, to negate the divine nature of its Creator, evil is born. God may be said to 'create' all actions only in the metaphysical sense that He is the source of all being, and thus of all modalities within being, and actions constitute one such modality; God cannot, however, be said to have created evil as such, only the conditions within which evil emerges. Evil is thus the negation of the Good, which is at one with the Real; as such, evil has no ultimate ontological principle — it is but a temporary state of deviation from that pure and divine nature, an illusion witnessed by the 'eye of the heart'.

The verse, then, can be understood as a call to seek refuge in the Real from the unreal, there being no common measure in terms of being between goodness and evil. Goodness is opposable only by the appearance of evil, an appearance that the 'eye of the heart' grasps as illusion — that eye which truly 'sees' God through 'the verities of faith'. This vision of the relative reality of evil, far from engendering indifference toward it, greatly empowers the spiritual resolve to overcome it, for the moral effort to be good intensifies in proportion to one's conviction of accountability to that goodness. This conviction, in turn, is carried inward from the mind to the heart by the certainty that goodness is 'more real' than evil; and this certainty itself is nourished by the spiritual vision of the good as the Real. The perception of the radical incommensurability between good and evil therefore does not lead to indifference in the face of evil, but to a more existentially compelling orientation toward the good. In the Imam's perspective, the notions of 'right' and 'rightful due' that are comprised within the meaning of haqq, ⟪ar:حق⟫, are infused with an aura of absoluteness: understanding what is right leads to the commitment to fulfil one's obligations, while what is rightfully due is the restoration of reality through the elimination of illusory evil — and both are equally infused with reality, which is justice. The moral notions of rectitude and propriety are expressions of a principle that transcends morality, a principle pertaining to ultimate reality. One's commitment to fulfilling one's obligations — to giving every being its rightful due — becomes infused, likewise, with a powerful charge of reality; the resolution to be just is rendered more absolute by the very quality of absoluteness proper to the Real.

This duty to give what is due to each and every being relates to the most explicit definition of justice given by the Imam — وَضَعَ الشَّئُی حَقَّ مَحَلِّہِ — 'Justice puts everything in its right place.' One is 'just' insofar as one gives everything its proper due, renders the haqq owed to each person, indeed to each and every thing in existence; and the ability to be 'just' in this demanding and universal sense is enhanced in proportion to one's attunement to al-Haqq, the Real. As the discussion in the previous chapter makes clear, this spiritual conception of virtue not only encompasses one's duty to all, but engages all that one is. One has to be good before one can do good; and the only way 'to be', is to be at one with the Real — that which alone truly 'is'.

The Imam's comment on justice evokes the more famous definition of Plato: '...we have laid down, as a universal principle, that everyone ought to perform the one function in the community for which his nature best suited him... that principle, or some form of it, is justice.' There are indeed many remarkable parallels between Plato's *Republic* and the Imam's letter to Malik al-Ashtar. In this chapter we shall occasionally draw attention to some of the more obvious correspondences and note, at the end, the way in which the Imam's explicit spirituality illuminates what remains implicit in Plato's philosophy — namely, how one can actually attain the vision of the Real, which Plato calls 'gazing upon the Good'. It is this contemplation of the Good as such that functions as the source of one's capacity to act ethically at every level, for it allows one to take the Good 'as a pattern for the right ordering of the state and of the individual'.

The influence of Platonic and Neoplatonic conceptions of justice on the formulation of ethical discourse in Islam has been amply attested in the sources. Within this discourse, justice is not seen as one virtue among several, but as the perfection or sum of all the other virtues — especially the three principal Platonic virtues of temperance, courage, and wisdom, which must characterize, respectively, the concupiscent, the irascible, and the rational dimensions of the soul. When all the dimensions of the soul, each 'filled out' by its proper virtue, are in their right place, the soul may be called 'just'.


What has not been given sufficient attention, however, is the degree to which there exists a spiritual correspondence between Plato's chief means of arriving at the vision of the Good — anamnesis — and the cardinal role of dhikr (⟪ar:ذکر⟫) in Islam, both terms being translatable by the English word "remembrance." And how is this justice achieved? What unlocks its power is precisely this remembrance, this recollection of ultimate reality and of immutable principles at their different levels of application. It is not a matter of learning things anew, but of recollecting principles that are inherent in the very substance of the intellect — principles by which all things can be understood, and which are ultimately rooted in the Sovereign Good itself. Such remembrance should be regarded as the cognitive and philosophical foundation of ethical thought. That its importance has been largely ignored is symptomatic of the severing of ethics from spirituality in modern discourse, which treats each discipline as though it were hermetically sealed from the other. Whereas ethics is still taken seriously as a branch of philosophy and as a discipline in its own right, spirituality is all too often dismissed as irrelevant to serious scholarly inquiry and relegated to the realm of the imaginary, the vague, and the mythological. This marginalization of spirituality from putatively serious discourse is closely related to the reductive view of knowledge that has increasingly characterized Western thought since the Renaissance — a reduction that goes hand in hand with the progressive emptying out of the sacred dimension of life.

In the Islamic tradition, the realms of ethics and spirituality are intimately connected. Nowhere is this more apparent than in the Sufi tradition, which is increasingly and most aptly referred to as the ihsani tradition — understanding the word ihsan (⟪ar:احسان⟫: excellence, virtue, goodness) in the sense given to it by the Prophet in a tradition of central importance concerning the three fundamental aspects of the Islamic way. It is known as "the hadith of Gabriel," for the questioner in this exchange — unbeknown to the Companions who were present — was the angel Gabriel (⟪ar:جبرئیل⟫), in human form. He approached the Prophet as he sat among his Companions and said:

'O Muhammad, tell me about submission (al-islam)' — يَا مُحَمَّدُ، أخْبِرْنِي عَنْ الإسْلاَمِ. The Prophet replied: الإِسْلاَمُ أَنْ تَشْهَدَ أَنْ لاَ إِلهَ إِلاَ اللهُ وَأَنَّ مُحَمَّداً رَسُولُ اللهِ، وَتُقِيمَ الصَّلاَةَ، وَتُؤْتِيَ الزَّكَاةَ، وَتَصُومَ رَمَضَانَ، وَتَحُجَّ الْبَيتَ إِن اسْتَطَعْتَ إِلَيْهِ سَبِيلاً — 'Al-islam is to testify that there is no god but God and Muhammad is the messenger of God, to perform the prayers, to pay the poor-due (al-zakat), to fast in Ramadan, and to make the pilgrimage to the House if you are able to do so.' 'You have spoken truly (⟪ar:صَدَقْتَ⟫),' Gabriel said — and we, the Companions, seeing only a man and not knowing he was an angel, were astonished that he should question the Prophet and then declare that he had spoken truly. 'Then tell me about faith (al-iman),' he said — ⟪ar:فَأَخْبِرْنِي عَنِ الإِيْمَانِ⟫. The Prophet said: أَنْ تُؤْمِنَ بِاللهِ، وَمَلاَئِكَتِهِ، وَكُتُبِهِ، وَرُسُلِهِ، وَالْيَوْمِ الآخِرِ، وَتُؤْمِنَ بِالْقَدَرِ خَيْرِهِ وَشَرِّهِ — 'It is to believe in God, His angels, His books, His messengers, and the Last Day, and to believe in divine destiny, both the good and the evil thereof.' 'You have spoken truly (⟪ar:صَدَقْتَ⟫),' he said. 'Then tell me about virtue (al-ihsan),' he said — ⟪ar:فَأَخْبِرْنِي عَنِ الإِحْسَانِ⟫. He said: أَنْ تَعْبُدَ اللهَ كَأَنَّكَ تَرَاهُ، فَإِنْ لَمْ تَكُنْ تَرَاهُ فَإِنَّهُ يَرَاكَ — 'It is to worship God as though you see Him, and if you see Him not, yet truly He sees you.'

The word ihsan can also be literally translated as "doing what is beautiful," its root (⟪ar:حَسَنَ⟫) being related primarily to beauty, husn; and virtue and vice are often referred to in Islamic ethical discourse as husn and qubh (⟪ar:قبح⟫) — literally beauty and ugliness. Ultimately, it is the vision of divine beauty that inspires virtue, which is beauty of soul, just as it is departure from the divine qualities that leaves the soul ugly (⟪ar:قبیح⟫). It is therefore unsurprising to find that, broadly speaking, while the specialists in the domain of outward action according to Islam have been the jurists, the fuqaha', and the specialists in the domain of formal belief have been the theologians, it fell to the mystics to be the custodians of spiritual virtue — of "making beautiful." Indeed, it is no coincidence that the overwhelming majority of artists in the Islamic world — calligraphers, painters, poets, musicians, and the rest — have been practising Sufis.

From the Prophet's definition of ihsan as "worshipping God as if one could see Him," one sees with clarity the crucial relationship between worship and virtue, between spirituality and ethics, between devotion to the Creator and goodness toward creatures: you cannot have the one without the other. Understanding this relationship takes us to the very heart of the spiritual tradition in Islam. Conversely, it is the denial and subversion of this tradition by some modern Muslims today that — more than any other single factor, we would venture to say — is ultimately responsible for the atrocities currently being committed by Muslims in the name of Islam.

It should be noted that both the theory and the practice of ethics have always been integral to Sufism. Even some of those who wrote treatises in more philosophical and less explicitly spiritual terms, such as al-Farabi (d. 872/950), were known also to be practising Sufis. The most influential figure in whom the domains of spirituality and ethics meet is, of course, al-Ghazali (d. 505/1111), whose magisterial *Ihya' 'ulum al-din* (Revival of the Sciences of Religion) stands as a monument to the impact of Islam's deepest spirit upon the ethical disposition of the believing soul. He has aptly been called the greatest thinker on ethics in Islam; and given how thoroughly his thinking is shaped by Sufism, this alone bears testimony to the close relationship between Sufism and ethics. Indeed, it would not be an exaggeration to say that the majority of treatises written by Sufis are, in fact, treatises on "ethics" — if ethics is understood not merely as akhlaq in the narrow sense of the term, but also as the fruit of ihsan, or spiritual virtue. The various spiritual stations (maqamat) described by the Sufis frequently bear the names of particular virtues, such as patience (sabr), gratitude (shukr), contentment (rida'), generosity (karam), and so on.

The thread running through all these "stations" is a transformative awareness of God — the principle of being and acting "as if you could see God," that is, conducting and composing oneself in the consciousness of being constantly in the divine presence, for even if you cannot see Him, "He sees you." The imperative of ihsan thus lies at the very root of a life of virtue in the spiritual universe of Islam. As we shall see in this and the following chapter, Ali (ع), in his sermons in the ⟪ar:نھج البلاغۃ⟫, repeatedly draws the reader back to the "vision" of God — at whatever degree — as the true basis of a life of virtue, and repeatedly warns of the dangers of its opposite — the engrossing vision of oneself — as the source of all vice. On one hand, the Imam tells us, "Know that your every action is dependent upon your prayer" — that is, upon your way of "gazing upon the Good," to use Platonic terms; on the other, he warns us to beware of vanity, conceit, and the lavish praise of oneself by others, for these are Satan's (⟪ar:شیطان⟫) most reliable opportunities to efface the virtue, the ihsan, of the virtuous (⟪ar:المُحسنین⟫). The true, objective vision of al-Haqq (⟪ar:الحق⟫), the Real — realized through the effacement of oneself in prayer, for in the prostration (⟪ar:سجدۃ⟫) of prayer we efface our own ego, our nafs (⟪ar:نفس⟫) — is here juxtaposed with the distorted, subjective vision of the ego produced by the inflation of pride, which is precisely what Iblis (⟪ar:ابلیس⟫) desires. The first leads to justice, putting all things in their proper place, including — most fundamentally — the ego itself; the second leads to all the injustices that flow from self-aggrandizement, including fanaticism and bigotry, for Satan, the epitome of pride and disobedience, is described as "the leader of the fanatics and the forerunner of the proud" (⟪ar:امام المتعصبین و سلف المستکبرین⟫, imam al-muta'assibin wa salaf al-mustakbirin). Bigotry is an intolerance toward those who hold different opinions; and fanatical bigotry is a particularly intense form of injustice, for it entails that pernicious prejudice which subordinates the objective requirements of justice to the putative prerogatives of one's self — whether that "self" is defined in terms of the individual, the family, the clan, the tribe, the nation, or the religion.

The Imam's epistle appointing Malik al-Ashtar as his governor of Egypt appears as number 52 in most editions of the Nahj al-balagha. In broad historical terms, the letter has served as a source of inspiration across the centuries, being read as an ideal constitution for Islamic governance. It complements — through its relatively detailed description of the duties and rights of the ruler, the various functionaries of the state, and the main classes of society — the more general framework of principles enshrined in the famous "Constitution of Medina" dictated by the Prophet. As will become clear in what follows, however, the advice contained in the letter far exceeds the parameters conventionally set by political or legal texts on governance. Although addressed in the first instance to the ruler or governor of the polity, much of the advice in fact pertains to universally applicable ethical principles and is therefore as relevant to the governed as to those who govern. The letter is as much a statement of ethics as of politics — not a discursive "system" or theory of ethics, to be sure, but an inspiring expression of the spiritual ethos that the Imam embodied and radiated, an ethos flowing directly from the sources of the Islamic revelation, and one that therefore discloses the roots of virtue and ethics within a climate governed by the all-embracing principle of revelation. It is possible, accordingly, to read this document also as a commentary on that revelation — one that in turn encourages the reader to delve more deeply into the meanings enshrined in the revealed texts. Malik al-Ashtar was a long-standing and dedicated follower of the Imam and was referred to as one of his "right-hand" men. Mu'awiya, the Imam's main antagonist in the civil war (37–41/657–661) that dominated his caliphate, was among those who used this term — notably after hearing of the success of his mission to have Malik poisoned before he could take up his post as governor of Egypt. Mu'awiya is reported by Tabarī as remarking: "Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع) had two right hands. One of them was cut off at Siffin, and the other today." For his part, the Imam's response to the news of Malik's assassination was as follows: "Malik — and what was Malik! By God, were he a mountain, he would be the solitary one soaring above all others, and were he a rock, he would have been impenetrably solid. No horse could ascend such a mountain, no bird could fly up to it."

Let us return to the Imam's definition of justice noted earlier: "Justice puts everything in its right place." In everyday speech, justice means little more than fairness, equality, and fair play — a serviceable but essentially formal account of the word. The conception at work here is of an altogether different order: it aligns justice with the sacred, with ⟪ar:القدس⟫, the All-Holy, and locates the very roots of justice within the divine nature itself. What connects this conception of justice to the sacred is the imperative of first putting one's relationship with God in its right place; everything else derives from this spiritual imperative. The same order of precedence is clearly established in the following important statement in the Imam's letter to Malik: "Be just with God and be just with people [giving them what is their due] from yourself." Being just with God means, among other things, conforming as best one can to His own nature, which is not merely the source of justice but is justice. And the emphasis falls on that word is: justice is not simply one attribute among His many attributes, standing alongside His mercy, His wrath, and His love — it is the very essence of His nature, so that all His other qualities flow from it. His mercy is an expression of His justice, and so too are His anger, His love, His creating, and His destroying. "I bear witness," says the Imam in one of his sermons, "that He is Justice ('adl) and He acts justly." It might be objected that between the divine reality of justice and its human expression there is a radical disjuncture. In God, justice means that everything is in its right place; for man, justice entails the effort to put everything in its right place — an immutable metaphysical quality on the divine side, and a dynamic, volitional effort on the human side. One response to this objection is as follows: man's moral effort to act justly in a world where things are not in their right place is rooted in an innate spiritual predisposition toward justice. Just action thus expresses the principle of justice, which — in man as in God — is that immutable principle by which everything is in its right place. Just as divine action in creation, judgement, and other domains is an expression of absolute justice, so in man just action expresses not only a moral, volitional, and intellectual effort to realize justice, but also a spiritual affinity with the ultimate nature of reality — with things as they truly are in God, and therefore as they ought to be here on earth. Applied to state and society, one of the most obvious consequences of this conception of justice is the necessity of piety and rectitude on the part of rulers. As the Imam says in another sermon: "The most momentous of the reciprocal rights that God has made obligatory is the right of the ruler over the subjects, and the right of the subjects over the ruler — a reciprocal obligation which God has ordained for them." He then makes the following point, which is altogether indispensable to any discourse on good governance in Islam: "The subjects will not be righteous except through the righteousness of the rulers, and the rulers will not be righteous except through the uprightness of the subjects. So if the subjects render to the ruler his rightful due and he renders them theirs, then will right be honoured among them, the ways of religion will be upheld, and the banners of justice will be erected." This insistence on the piety of the ruler is, as one would expect, a central aspect of the Imam's message to his governor; but as will be clear from what follows, it involves far more than a simple exhortation to abide by the formal rules of Islamic law.

The whole of the Imam's life can be seen as a quest to conform as perfectly as possible to the divine nature and to this quality of justice in particular. There are, accordingly, far too many dimensions of this principle — social, economic, political, juridical — as they appear in the Imam's life and teachings to address within the brief compass of this essay. Let us simply refer to one defining moment in his caliphate that illustrates the rigour with which he adhered to justice at whatever cost. The revolt of Mu'awiya, and the civil war into which the fledgling Muslim empire was thereby plunged, can be seen as having begun not at Siffin but in Medina. Upon assuming the caliphate, Ali (ع) was advised by his cousin Ibn al-'Abbas to temporarily confirm all of 'Uthman's governors in their posts, and then, once he had consolidated the new order, to remove them. The Imam's reply to this proposed strategy was reported as follows: "I do not doubt that this would be best for the sake of reconciliation in this world. But there is my obligation to the Truth (alladhī yalzamanī min al-haqq) and my knowledge of 'Uthman's governors — so, by God, I shall never appoint one of them."

Thus was the die cast: the Imam dismissed Mu'awiya along with all of his predecessor's governors. It was clear to the Imam that Ibn al-'Abbas' proposed solution had every chance of success — he knew that all Mu'awiya wanted was worldly power and that Mu'awiya would have been content to govern Syria had the Imam allowed it. While commentators have cited this incident, which made the revolt of the governors all but inevitable, as evidence of Ali (ع)'s lack of political acumen and his intransigence, it ought instead to be understood as an expression of his disdain for worldly ease when that ease must be purchased at the price of essential principles. As he says in one of his aphorisms: "People renounce no part of their religion for the sake of rectifying their world without God opening them up to something worse than it" — that is, worse than the very defect they were seeking to rectify.


It is clear that confirming in power those governors whose corruption was beyond both doubt and redemption would have amounted, for the Imam, to 'renouncing' a part of his religion — for religion without justice is not religion. Supporting unjust governors, even as a tactical manoeuvre, constituted a compromise of religious principle that was simply inconceivable to him. This attitude was embodied in countless episodes of the Imam's life, all of which demonstrate that the principles enshrined in the letter to Malik al-Ashtar were far from merely theoretical.

<!-- page 78 -->

In the sections that follow, we turn first to the analysis of a particular dimension of justice — one that is political on the surface, ethical in its outward expression, but spiritual in its depth. This is the justice of the political ruler, understood in relation to its most clearly definable opposite: injustice, tyranny, oppression, and corruption. The argument will be that a deep assimilation of the Imam's spirituality can bestow an existential power upon the moral effort to live justly — a power that is absent when justice is conceived exclusively in terms of individual or horizontal values, that is, in terms of a secular moral philosophy divorced from religious or spiritual principles. The crucial role of devotion and worship — both formal and supra-formal — is then discussed. The contention is that contemplation of the divine Reality is the fountainhead from which integral virtue flows spontaneously and ceaselessly. This point will be sharpened by a brief comparison with Plato's 'gazing' on the Sovereign Good as the source of all virtue.

The analysis draws on key passages from Ali (ع)'s letter to Malik al-Ashtar, alongside relevant material from his other letters, sermons, and sayings, and from certain traditions of the Prophet and verses of the Qur'an that illuminate the themes the letter raises. A full translation of the Imam's letter is provided in Appendix II of this work.

### Justice Versus Tyranny

The letter is addressed to one of the Imam's closest and most trusted companions, so his warnings about injustice and oppression carry a very precise force, in addition to expressing a universal theme. These warnings apply to all those who, while formally subject to Islam and its laws, and initially possessing every right intention, remain nonetheless vulnerable to the temptations of political power — taking full account of the unfortunate but proven truth captured in the maxim 'power corrupts', or, in the Imam's own words, 'he who rules, appropriates (man malaka ista'thara)'.

The Imam's understanding of justice clearly transcends both its historical context and the formal boundaries of the Islamic faith.
