The letter can be read as addressing not only those who wish to govern according to just principles, but also all those who, though not corrupt at the outset, are corruptible — a category that embraces the overwhelming majority of humanity.

<!-- page 79 -->

**Human virtue and ultimate reality**

A note of sombre realism sounds from the very opening of the letter. The potential for evil in man, when left to his own devices and deprived of divine grace, dominates the tone of the preamble — hence the emphasis on abiding by God's wajibat, the religious obligations binding on every Muslim. Malik is instructed to place obedience to God above all else, 'to abide by what He has commanded in His Book ... for no one prospers except through abiding by them, and no one is wretched except through repudiating and neglecting them'. The preamble is introductory but by no means perfunctory: obedience to the divine commands is to be understood not only as legally binding but as spiritually liberating. This obedience plays a crucial part in freeing one from the tyranny of the 'soul which incites to evil' (al-nafs al-ammara bi'l-su'), to which the Imam refers immediately after calling Malik to obedience: 'For truly, the soul incites to evil, unless God has mercy.' This sentence is almost identical to the words of the Prophet Joseph in the Qur'an (12: 53), and it is of the utmost importance in setting the scene for the ethical injunctions and socio-political instructions that follow. The essential relationship determining the spiritual substance of the soul — and, a fortiori, its moral comportment — is the relationship between the human soul and divine grace. Without grace the soul tends toward evil, but with God's assistance it is liberated from its base inclinations and guided back to its true, primordial nature, its fitra, to which the Qur'an refers in the following verse:

So set thy purpose for religion with unswerving devotion: the nature of God (firat Allah), according to which He hath created man. There is no altering God's creation. That is the right religion (al-din al-qayyim), but most men know not. (30: 30)

<!-- page 80 -->

The actual state of the fallen soul, then, is called to give way to the ideal state of original human nature — a nature that is both the origin and the end of the human condition, and at the same time the true substance of every human soul, always accessible in principle even if, for most people, it remains clouded by fallen human nature in practice. In the quest to revive this primordial nature, one returns to the complementary functions of the human intellect and divine revelation. In the first sermon of the Nahj, as cited earlier, the Imam tells us that God sent to mankind 'His messengers, dispatching prophets in succession, in order to claim from them [His creatures] the fulfilment of the covenant of His creation; to remind them of His forgotten graces; to remonstrate with them through communication [of His revelation]; to unearth for them the buried treasures of the intellects (dafa in al-uqul).'

These 'buried treasures' can be identified with the original, God-given knowledge proper to the fitra. From this integral human nature, in complete harmony with the divine nature, all the essential virtues flow spontaneously and unhindered. 'Let your most beloved treasure be the treasure of virtuous acts', the Imam writes. Virtue will indeed be the 'most beloved treasure' for one who reverses the natural inclinations of the egoistic soul and establishes a supernatural orientation toward its own deepest nature — and by that very token, toward the divine Reality that furnishes the ontological foundations of all authentic virtue. For al-Haqq, the Real, is not only the deepest foundation of virtue; it is the true substance of every positive quality and, in the last analysis, of every single existent entity. As one of the most celebrated supplications attributed to the Imam puts it:

By Thy tremendousness (bi-'a matika), which has filled all things ... by Thy Names which have filled the foundations of all things, by Thy knowledge which encompasses all things, by the light of Thy face, through which all things are illumined ...

<!-- page 81 -->

From this vantage point, every virtue can be regarded as the reflection on the human plane of a divine attribute — what is denoted as a divine 'Name'. Hence the fundamental theme found throughout the ihsanī tradition — the path of spiritual excellence (⟪ar:احسان⟫) that the Prophet defined as worshipping God as though one could see Him, knowing that even if one does not see Him, He sees us — finds expression in the saying: 'Assume the character traits of God (takhallaqu bi-akhlāqi'Llah)'.

The relationship between human virtue and divine reality is altogether indispensable in the Imam's perspective. In subjective terms, the practice of virtue attracts the corresponding divine quality; in objective terms, the divine quality is the source and — in accordance with true tawhid — the actual substance of all human virtue. Every positive quality, both in itself and in its manifestation, pertains to God, the One who 'has no partner' theologically and 'has no second' ontologically. This mystical assimilation of the principle of tawhid is strongly implied in the Qur'anic verse, 'And thou threw not when thou didst throw [a handful of dust], but God it was who threw' (8: 17). These divine qualities are not to be viewed as purely transcendent — as though they were absolutely one with the divine essence and utterly beyond the reach of the human soul. They are also to be grasped as exemplars, as patterns of perfection for the soul to emulate to the fullest extent possible. The goal is to become as much 'like' God as possible, yet this development can unfold only within the compass of one's servitude to Him. One is generous for the sake of God, because God is ⟪ar:الکریم⟫, the Generous; one is compassionate and merciful because He is ⟪ar:الرحمن الرحیم⟫ — never out of any claim that 'I am generous' or 'I am merciful', for the aim is to emulate the qualities of God, not to claim to personify Him. The individual must therefore be effaced before God in order to be truly virtuous, and must at the same time participate in and embody the very qualities he wishes God to manifest toward him — remaining lowly, humble, and effaced throughout, in the certainty that no measure of virtue or intelligence can ever diminish or equal the qualities of God, there being no proportion between the Absolute and the relative, the Creator and the created. His effort to embody these qualities is empowered to the degree that he apprehends the divine attributes — through intuition and existentially, rather than merely through reason and theoretically. It is like gazing into a mirror: what one sees is a reflection, but an inverted one. The aim is to catch, on the human plane, the inverted reflection of divine transcendence. For man is at once ⟪ar:خلیفۃ اللہ⟫, God's vicegerent upon the earth, and ⟪ar:عباد اللہ⟫, His slave. The consciousness of servitude ensures that, while representing God, man does not slip into competing with Him in grandeur; the active commitment to representing God's attributes preserves him from the spiritual paralysis of neglecting his duties toward the world. Such is the meaning of the verse, يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ أَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ إِلَى ٱللَّهِۖ وَٱللَّهُ هُوَ ٱلْغَنِىُّ ٱلْحَمِيدُ — 'O mankind, you are the destitute before God, and God is the Self-sufficient, the Praiseworthy' (35: 15). There must be a synthesis between the active human virtues and the spiritual consciousness of one's nothingness before the divine Reality. The relationship between intelligence and humility deserves emphasis here: intellectual perception of the divine attributes produces humility, and the effacement of the soul in turn displaces attention from the ego and directs it toward the transcendent reality as the source of all virtue and truth.

<!-- page 82 -->

It is worth dwelling on the notion of the 'soul which incites to evil' and on the Qur'anic story of Joseph (Yusuf) within which this notion arises, for the Imam could assume that his readers were familiar with this context, and he clearly intended it to be borne in mind by Malik — and by any other person reading this letter on governance.

Joseph offers this description of man's base inclinations after his innocence of all charges has been established before the Egyptian king. He says, nonetheless, 'I do not exonerate myself. Truly, the soul incites to evil, unless my Lord has mercy.' Indeed, on the two occasions when he was subjected to the seductive temptations of Zulaykha, the Qur'an makes clear that his successful resistance was due not to his own moral resources but to the grace of God.

This theme of human helplessness in the face of attractive but immoral gratification is heavily implied in the Imam's repetition of the Qur'anic phrase, 'Truly the soul incites to evil, unless God has mercy.' These, then, are the two poles of attraction for the ordinary soul — the soul not yet 'at peace': its own base inclinations toward the negative possibilities of the fleeting world of appearances, and the higher orientation toward the grace of God, drawing the soul back toward the pure positivity of ultimate reality. The very sharpness of the contrast within this powerful Qur'anic sentence galvanizes an awareness of the soul's absolute and unconditional need of God in every circumstance. It is a most fitting way to open a letter of instruction to someone being vested with authority over a vast and wealthy country where temptations lie in wait on every side. It stands as a solemn reminder of the soul's susceptibility to allurement on all planes — gross and subtle alike — a susceptibility that cannot be neutralized by the soul itself but only by the mercy and grace of God. And yet, returning to Joseph's words, the 'vision of the evidence' of God implies active discernment and, as its moral consequence, resolute self-control. The human effort to avoid sin therefore does not contradict the necessity of grace for the attainment of virtue; rather, it expresses a grace already bestowed, already present within the conscientious soul. The very capacity of the human will to strive for virtue is itself a pre-existent grace embedded within the spiritual constitution or fitra — though it is a grace that must be rendered present, or actualized, through the sincere effort to draw upon this God-given power.

<!-- page 83 -->

The manner in which this grace enters the soul as a determining element of moral and spiritual life will be made clearer later in the Imam's letter. For now, however, the negative dimension of the question requires further elaboration, for the ever-present temptation on the part of the ruler to abuse his power and perpetrate injustice and tyranny deserves more than a passing reference. It might indeed be argued that the question of how to refrain from injustice is far more difficult and subtle than its positive counterpart — how to act with justice. The Imam sets out the positive rules of political governance in a direct and straightforward manner, but what is far less straightforward is the actual capacity of any holder of political office to maintain fidelity to those rules when confronted with the temptations that power offers. One might frame laws to punish corruption, but what cannot be legislated is the will or the capacity to remain incorruptible in the face of political authority's blandishments. This helps explain why the Imam's exhortations take on such a markedly negative character in the early part of the letter — as in the command to 'break the soul in the face of passionate desires', to 'dominate your inclination', to 'withhold yourself from that which is not lawful for you'. It also explains why the ethical imperative of justice must be firmly situated within the context of a lived spirituality. Without that grounding, injunctions to be fair, just, and honest will lack the existential quality that transforms them from abstract postulates into unquestionable realities, and that transmutes formal obedience to externally imposed rules into spiritual affinity with the source of those rules.

Living according to just precepts then ceases to be a matter of compliance with something imposed from without and becomes instead the moral concomitant of one's inner being. In other words, an integral approach to justice leads to an identification with what is right — with al-Haqq as such — rather than merely to the doing of what is right. There is a shift of consciousness from doing to being, from action to contemplation, without this in any way implying that action is weakened or undermined. On the contrary, action comes to reflect, on its own plane, the divine object of contemplation, and thereby becomes more, not less, effective. An orientation toward the Real imparts a deeper degree of reality to what one does in the realm of action, and to one's own mode of being. One does what is right insofar as one is real; this process of realization is expressed in the Islamic spiritual tradition as tahqīq — 'making real'. One's ethical comportment is 'made real' through spirituality, and spirituality, for its part, is expressed by and rendered more concrete through the practice of virtue.


<!-- page 22 -->
The Imam then issues a stern warning: وَلاَ تَنْصِبَنَّ نَفْسَكَ لِحَرْبِ اللهِ — 'Do not set your soul up for war with God.' One might well ask what this means, and what kinds of actions or attitudes place the soul at war with God. The allusion seems to reach deeper than simple disobedience — deeper than the many violations of religious obligation mentioned at the letter's outset. While that is the most obvious meaning of 'fighting' against God, it does not exclude subtler modes of opposition to divine reality: attitudes that may accompany outward acts of obedience yet contradict their very spirit. Take, for example, the cardinal vice of pride (takabbur, or kibr), against which the Imam warns repeatedly — the psychic state in which a person feels superior and behaves highhandedly toward others, its signs so visible in his conduct that they cannot be hidden. A ruler's pride is a misappropriation of a divine attribute, an arrogation of a greatness that belongs exclusively to God, who declares: الْكِبْرِيَاءُ رِدَائِي، وَالْعَظَمَةُ إِزَارِي، فَمَنْ نَازَعَنِي وَاحِدًا مِنْهُمَا، قَذَفْتُهُ فِي النَّارِ — 'Pride is My cloak and greatness is My robe; whoever contends with Me over either of them, I shall cast him into the Fire.' God alone has the right to takabbur, and God alone may be called, without offence, al-Mutakabbir, 'the Proud'. To vaunt oneself before others is tantamount to inwardly defying God's authority and thus amounts to an implicit denial of His unique and incomparable majesty. This denial of a concomitant of divine sovereignty can itself be understood as a kind of 'war' with God — for a subject who openly defies his king is, in effect, waging war against him. In short, enmity toward transcendent reality is the invisible substance pervading all pride and every act of arrogance or oppression a ruler commits against those beneath him.

The paradox here, one that only a sense of spiritual values can illuminate, is that an attitude of superiority toward those 'below' constitutes, in fact, an act of rebellion against that which is 'above'. An injustice toward creatures not only draws down upon its perpetrator the justice of the Creator — it also manifests, in and of itself, outright opposition to the Creator. Thus what appears to be a horizontal vice — 'horizontal' in the sense of belonging to this world — is in reality a spiritual disorder. The outward immorality of corruption is merely the symptom of an inner spiritual malaise: an implicit denial of the absoluteness, and hence the uniqueness, of the source of all power and authority, a forgetting of the truth expressed in the Qur'anic verse, لِّمَنِ ٱلْمُلْكُ ٱلْيَوْم لِلَّهِ ٱلْوَحِدِ ٱلْقَهَّارِ — 'To whom belongeth the dominion this day? To God, the One, the All-Conquering' (40:16). This truth should resound constantly in the ears of every ruler, 'this day' understood as every day, not only the Day of Judgement. In the Imam's sermon known as al-Qași'a (القاسیۃ) — 'the Abasement', so named for its humiliation of the proud — Iblis (Satan) is described as the first of those who 'competed with God' in grandeur, and is thus the embodiment of the principle of pride and arrogance. When commanded to prostrate before Adam, Iblis refused, as the Qur'an recounts: إِذْ قَالَ رَبُّكَ لِلْمَلَٓئِكَةِ إِنِّى خَلِقٌۢ بَشَرًۭا مِّن طِينٍۢ ۝ فَإِذَا سَوَّيْتُهُۥ وَنَفَخْتُ فِيهِ مِن رُّوحِى فَقَعُوا۟ لَهُ سَجِدِينَ ۝ فَسَجَدَ ٱلْمَلَٓئِكَةُ كُلُّهُمْ أَجْمَعُونَ ۝ إِلَّآ إِبْلِيسَ ٱسْتَكْبَرَ وَكَانَ مِنَ ٱلْكَفِرِينَ — 'When your Lord said to the angels: Truly I am going to create man from clay; and when I have fashioned him and breathed into him of My spirit, then fall down before him prostrate. So the angels prostrated themselves, all of them together; save Iblis: he was proud and was one of the disbelievers' (38:71–74). He refused because 'vehement passion' overcame him; he deemed himself superior to Adam on the grounds that he was made of fire while Adam was made of clay — خلقتنی من نار و خلقتہ من طین. Taking pride in this presumed superiority and thereby rebelling against God's command, Iblis is not only the first 'enemy of God' but also, as noted earlier, 'the leader of the bigots and the forerunner of the proud' (imam al-muta assibin wa salaf al-mustakbirin — امام المُتَعَصِّبِین و سلف المستکبرین). The Imam warns his listeners: 'Beware lest he infect you with his disease' through his insinuations, whisperings, and machinations. He urges them: 'Be resolute in placing self-abasement over your heads, casting self-glorification beneath your feet, and removing pride from your necks. And take up humility as the fortified watchtower between you and your enemy.'

<!-- page 23 -->
Humility, then, is the principal weapon in the war against Iblis, whose chief aim — inversely — is to inculcate pride, for pride is not only the worst of vices but the poison that infects and destroys all virtue. Virtue itself is converted into vice the moment pride accompanies it. 'The sin that grieves you is better, in the sight of God, than the virtue that makes you proud.' One should also recall what was noted above about the dangers of excessive praise and the conceit it generates, these being among 'Satan's most reliable opportunities to efface the virtue of one who is virtuous'.

Taken together, these statements constitute a commentary on the Qur'anic account of Iblis' disgrace, grasping a simple act of disobedience at its spiritual root in a way that brings home the ever-present danger of falling prey to that 'hidden' form of shirk (polytheism) to which the Prophet refers in these words: الشِّرْكُ فِي هَذِهِ الْأُمَّةِ أَخْفَى مِنْ دَبِيبِ النَّمْلِ علی حَجَرِ الأَسوَد فِی لِیلَۃِ الظُّلْما — 'The creeping of shirk in my community is more hidden (akhfa) than the creeping of a black ant over a hard rock on a dark night.' The superficial notion of shirk as the worship of material idols — شرک الجلی, the open and manifest form — must be understood more subtly as شرک الخفی, the hidden form: false gods concealed within one's intentions, attitudes, and orientations — all the elements of hidden pride, vanity, ostentation, and pretentiousness. As the Imam says, 'Know that the slightest pretension (riya — ریا) is polytheism.' To act for the sake of earning others' praise is to act for others rather than for God; it is therefore an implicit attribution of partners to God: 'Whoso acts for other than God, God entrusts him to those for whose sake he acted.'

In this light, the meaning of Iblis' act of disobedience can be more deeply understood. It is the outward moral consequence of an inner spiritual malaise rooted in that pride which is tantamount to 'hidden polytheism' and which brings in its wake a disgraceful humiliation. Iblis is disgraced because he disobeyed God, and he disobeyed God on account of pride. Pride is therefore, in and of itself, a disgrace — a state of 'dis-grace', a perversion of the human condition that carries within it the seed of its own inexorable inversion. 'Do you not see how God humiliated him through his pride?' the Imam asks. Being proud is already, in reality, a form of humiliation, for self-aggrandizement is tantamount to self-destruction.

<!-- page 24 -->
Returning to the pride of those in power, the Imam succinctly expresses the train of thought set in motion within the mind of a ruler who is stepping onto the slippery slope toward oppression: وَلاَ تَقُولَنَّ: إِنِّي مُؤَمَّرٌ! آمُرُ! فَأُطَاعُ! إِنَّ ذلِكَ إِدْغَالٌ فِي الْقَلْبِ، وَمَنْهَكَةٌ لِلدِّينِ — 'Do not say, "I have been given authority, I order and am obeyed", for this leads to corruption in the heart and the erosion of religion.' It is worth noting that this self-glorification is seen as producing first an inner, spiritual corruption — that of the 'heart' — and only then the destruction of religion. This order of priority underscores that the outward prescriptions of religion require the right inward attitude if they are to be applied with integrity and maintained with stability. The Imam's idea might be restated thus: when power goes to the head, faith departs from the heart; conversely, for faith to be properly manifested in the world, it must flow from a heart suffused with humility. This is another aspect of 'putting each thing in its proper place' — وضع کل شئی حق محلہ.

The Imam continues: وَإِذَا أَحْدَثَ لَكَ مَا أَنْتَ فِيهِ مِنْ سُلْطَانِكَ أُبَّهَةً أَوْ مَخِيلَةً، فَانْظُرْ إِلَى عِظَمِ مُلْكِ اللهِ فَوْقَكَ، فَإِنَّ ذلِكَ يُطَامِنُ إِلَيْكَ مِنْ طِمَاحِكَ، وَيَكُفُّ عَنْكَ مِنْ غَرْبِكَ،يَفِيءُ إِلَيْكَ بِمَا عَزَبَ عَنْكَ مِنْ عَقْلِكَ — 'If the authority of your position engenders vanity and arrogance, then look at the grandeur of God's dominion above you … this will calm your ambition, restrain you from your own vehemence, and restore to you what had strayed from your intellect.' The relationship between intellect and humility deserves careful attention here. The part of the intellect that 'strays' when vanity and arrogance take hold is precisely the part that is conscious of God's absolute reality and of the relative, derivative, and ultimately illusory character of everything else. It is significant that the word for 'arrogance', makhila (مَخِيلَةً), derives from the same root as the word for imagination, khayal (خیال), and is thus intimately connected with the realm of the imaginary, the fanciful, the illusory, and the unreal: when imagination fastens upon the unreal it becomes infused with pride and turns into مخیلۃ, arrogance. Arrogant pride is therefore an intense form of self-delusion rooted in an intellectual defect; it manifests as a vice, but its cause runs deeper than the moral level, for it involves not merely an overestimation of oneself but, more fundamentally, an underestimation of the Absolute. In this light, the overt egotism of the tyrant can be seen as an intensification of the congenital egocentricity that clings to anyone who has not realized the truth of God's sole reality and the ultimately illusory nature of all else. The sage alone fully assimilates the truth expressed in such Qur'anic verses as: كُلُّ شَىْءٍ هَالِكٌ إِلَّا وَجْهَهُ — 'Everything is dying away except His countenance' (28:88); كُلُّ مَنْ عَلَيْهَا فَانٍۢ ۝ وَيَبْقَىٰ وَجْهُ رَبِّكَ ذُو ٱلْجَلَلِ وَٱلْإِكْرَامِ — 'Everything that is thereon is perishing, and there subsisteth but the Face of thy Lord, possessor of Glory and Bounty' (55:26–27); and وَقُلْ جَآءَ ٱلْحَقُّ وَزَهَقَ ٱلْبَطِلُ ۚ إِنَّ ٱلْبَطِلَ كَانَ زَهُوقًۭا — 'The Truth hath come and falsehood hath passed away; indeed, falsehood is ever passing away' (17:81). Only the sage draws from this truth the humility that dissolves all individualism and egocentricity — the roots of pride, vanity, and arrogance that are, in turn, the lifeblood of tyranny and oppression.

<!-- page 25 -->
One can now better appreciate the Imam's warning: إِيَّاكَ وَمُسَامَةَ اللهِ فِي عَظَمَتِهِ، وَالتَّشَبُّهَ بِهِ فِي جَبَرُوتِهِ، فَإِنَّ اللهَ يُذِلُّ كُلَّ جَبَّار، وَيُهِينُ كُلَّ مُخْتَال — 'Beware of comparing [yourself] with God in greatness and likening [yourself] to Him in might, for God abases every tyrant and disgraces every braggart.' One who does not act with justice toward God and His creatures — for the Imam commands, أَنْصِفِ اللهَ وَأَنْصِفِ النَّاسَ مِنْ نَفْسِكَ، وَمِنْ خَاصَّةِ أَهْلِكَ، وَمَنْ لَكَ فِيهِ هَوىً مِنْ رَعِيَّتِكَ، فَإِنَّكَ إِلاَّ تَفْعَلْ تَظْلِمْ — will find that not only His creatures but God Himself becomes his adversary: وَمَنْ ظَلَمَ عِبَادَ اللهِ كَانَ اللهُ خَصْمَهُ دُونَ عِبَادِهِ، وَمَنْ خَاصَمَهُ اللهُ أَدْحَضَ حُجَّتَهُ، وَكَانَ لله حَرْباً حَتَّى يَنْزعَ وَيَتُوبَ — 'He remains at war with God until he desists and repents.' The tyrant will eventually be compelled to repent, even if only at the moment of death and imminent judgement. Even if he appears to have escaped the consequences of his tyranny in this life, he cannot escape them in the Hereafter.

As for the nature of the tyrant's war against God in this world — that is, before repentance or judgement overtakes him — it consists in his implicit opposition to ultimate reality, his violation of the deepest nature of things, of the true nature of his own being, his ⟪ar:فطرۃ⟫. This war takes various forms.

One form is constituted by the very persistence of tyranny itself, for the longer it continues, the more wretched the tyrant's soul becomes and the more firmly the seeds of misery are planted in his being. This is what is implied by the Qur'anic doctrine of istidraj (⟪ar:استدراج⟫), the drawing out of punishment by degrees. The word derives from ⟪ar:درج⟫, meaning stages — as even in Urdu one says ⟪ar:درجہ بدرجہ⟫, step by step. God employs it in Surat al-Aʿraf: ‏وَمِمَّنْ خَلَقْنَآ أُمَّةٌۭ يَهْدُونَ بِٱلْحَقِّ وَبِهِ يَعْدِلُونَ ‎۱۸۱‏وَٱلَّذِينَ كَذَّبُوا۟ بِـَٔايَتِنَا سَنَسْتَدْرِجُهُم مِّنْ حَيْثُ لَا يَعْلَمُونَ ‎۱۸۲ — 'Of those We have created are people who guide with truth and dispense justice therewith. But those who reject Our signs, We will lead them on by degrees [to punishment] from whence they know not' (7:181–2). The punishment meted out by degrees can take the form of apparent worldly success, as is clear from the verse: ‏وَلَا تُعْجِبْكَ أَمْوَلُهُمْ وَأَوْلَدُهُمْ ۚ إِنَّمَا يُرِيدُ ٱللَّهُ أَن يُعَذِّبَهُم بِهَا فِى ٱلدُّنْيَا وَتَزْهَقَ أَنفُسُهُمْ وَهُمْ كَفِرُونَ ‎۸۵ — 'Let not their wealth nor their children please thee. God desireth only to punish them thereby in the world, that their souls shall pass away while they are disbelievers' (9:85). A tyrant or oppressor can be a Muslim in the formal sense and yet simultaneously guilty of a mode of disbelief — taking the word kafir in its strictly etymological sense, as one who 'covers over' the truth and its moral concomitants, both in his very being and in his actions. This connects directly with the contrast drawn in the verses just quoted (7:181–2): between those who dispense justice in accordance with the truth and 'those who reject Our signs' — the oppressors and tyrants, precisely.


### Moral Conscience And Spiritual Consciousness

Another form this enmity with God takes is the inner war against one's own conscience — what is called in Urdu the ⟪ar:ضمیر⟫. The voice of conscience — the very essence and sum total of secular ethics — can here be grasped in its fuller context, rather than merely as the somewhat hypertrophied foundation of individual morality to which secular ethical discourse has reduced it. That fuller context is the supra-individual, metaphysical source from which individual conscience springs: the divine infusion of spiritual discernment within the soul, the divine inspiration that underlies all ethical discrimination. The divine quality of justice is thus translated, at the level of the morally responsible soul, into the voice of inner conscience: ‏وَنَفْسٍۢ وَمَا سَوَّىٰهَا ‎۷‏فَأَلْهَمَهَا فُجُورَهَا وَتَقْوَىٰهَا ‎۸ — 'And the soul and that which perfected it, inspiring it with [consciousness of] its wickedness and its righteousness' (91:7–8). The war against one's conscience, even if the oppressor barely acknowledges it, is in fact — and a fortiori — a war against God.

At a deeper level, then, this war against God stands revealed as a war against the very reality that defines the soul as such. For the soul not only contains a moral conscience — an intrinsic and inalienable awareness of the difference between good and evil, instilled within it by God — it also contains an innate consciousness of the reality of divine lordship, together with the corresponding creaturely servitude. Spiritual consciousness is thus the source of moral conscience. The Qur'an describes the immanence of this spiritual consciousness within each soul in terms that emphasize its supra- or pre-existential nature, evoking a 'moment' at the very dawn of creation — a moment, or ontological degree, that precedes the human condition as such yet enters into the creative articulation of the human soul: ‏وَإِذْ أَخَذَ رَبُّكَ مِنۢ بَنِىٓ آدَمَ مِن ظُهُورِهِمْ ذُرِّيَّتَهُمْ وَأَشْهَدَهُمْ عَلَىٰٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ أَلَسْتُ بِرَبِّكُمْ ۖ قَالُوا۟ بَلَىٰ ۛ شَهِدْنَآ ۛ أَن تَقُولُوا۟ يَوْمَ ٱلْقِيَمَةِ إِنَّا كُنَّا عَنْ هَذَا غَفِلِينَ ‎۱۷۲ — 'And when thy Lord brought forth from the children of Adam, from their loins, their seed, and made them testify against their souls [saying], "Am I not your Lord?" They said, "Yes, verily, we testify." [This was] lest ye say on the Day of Resurrection, "Truly, of this we were unaware"' (7:172). The moment He created them He made them witness His glory and testify to it, so that they could plead no ignorance on the Day of Judgement.

What we observe here is that the source of morality transcends the human level even while forming part of the very definition of humanity. From this perspective, what renders the human soul fully human is precisely that element of transcendence which infinitely surpasses the soul and yet, mysteriously, furnishes its own deepest being. In this light, tyranny and oppression take on a far more nuanced meaning, these vices being brought much closer to the lived reality of every soul rather than being confined to those who possess and abuse political power. The tyrant is, first and foremost, one who violates his own soul — one who allows it to be dominated by 'the soul which commands evil', al-nafs al-ammara (⟪ar:النفس الامارۃ بالسوء⟫). Thus the statement the Imam cites as epitomizing the attitude of one who is on the way to corrupting his own soul and becoming an oppressor — وَلاَ تَقُولَنَّ: إِنِّي مُؤَمَّرٌ آمُرُ فَأُطَاعُ, 'I order and am obeyed' — is not only to be understood in social terms but must also be interpreted microcosmically. It thereby becomes the statement of the evil element within the soul: the nafs al-ammara, which literally 'orders' (amara) and is obeyed by every other faculty of the soul — intelligence, imagination, will, sentiment, sensibility, and the rest.

The combat against one's own soul (mujahadat al-nafs, ⟪ar:مجاھدۃ النفس⟫) is a theme the Imam returns to repeatedly. This necessity of struggling with and overcoming one's own faults is an essential dimension of justice in the sense defined earlier: placing each thing in its proper place begins with rectifying oneself before presuming to reform or govern others — it means restoring the divine nature within oneself. As the Imam says, 'If your aspiration is to reform the people, begin with yourself, for your pursuit of the reform of others while your own soul is corrupt is the greatest of faults.' In his letter appointing Muhammad b. Abī Bakr (⟪ar:محمد بن ابی بکر⟫) — Malik's (⟪ar:مالک اشتر⟫) predecessor as governor of Egypt — the Imam draws a firm connection between the bestowal of power and the obligation to resist one's own soul: 'And be aware, O Muhammad b. Abi Bakr, that I have appointed you as governor over the most immense of the forces at my disposal, the people of Egypt, so you are obliged to oppose your soul and protect your religion.' The relationship between the magnitude of the power placed at one's disposal and the spiritual responsibility that follows from it deserves careful attention. One must be on guard, first in relation to one's own soul and its desires, and secondly in regard to the religion. The greater the power available, the greater the obligation to control and restrain oneself; for if the soul yields to its desires and gratifies them through the power at its disposal, the result is the corruption not only of oneself but of the very religion one is charged to uphold. Here again we see priorities ordered by an essentially spiritual principle: the outward form and application of religion depends for its integrity upon the inward disposition of the Muslim's soul. This is another expression of the principle of putting each thing in its proper place. The commanding soul that arrogates to itself the right of autonomy and dominion is described with lapidary precision by the Qur'anic verse: ‏كَلَّآ إِنَّ ٱلْإِنسَنَ لَيَطْغَىٰٓ ‎۶‏أَن رَّآهُ ٱسْتَغْنَىٰٓ ‎۷ — 'Truly man is rebellious, in that he deemeth himself independent' (96:6–7). Insofar as the soul pretends to self-sufficiency and attempts to sever itself from its total dependence upon God, it sets itself up as a god in its own right — the person actually comes to believe that he himself is God — and herein lies the true portrait of the tyrant, the one who, as the Qur'an puts it, 'taketh his own desire as his god' (45:23): ⟪ar:أَفَرَءَيْتَ مَنِ ٱتَّخَذَ إِلَهَهُۥ هَوَىٰهُ⟫. Knowledge of one's nothingness before God, and consequently of one's unconditional need of Him, may be taken here as the principal means of loosening the grip of the soul that commands to evil. For only the soul that already commands itself to evil will command others to evil; and only the soul that is lowly before God will be humble, compassionate, and just in its dealings with people.

This principle emerges clearly from the following words of the Imam: 'Let the most beloved of affairs to you be those most centred upon the right, 62 the most comprehensive in justice, and the most inclusive of popular approval, for the disapproval of the common folk undermines the approval of the elite ...' This emphasis on the importance of the common people is not only sound practical counsel for fair and effective governance; it can also be read as a reflection of the spiritual imperative to recognize man's actual lowliness before God, and thus as a way of urging the ruler to identify with the poor rather than the rich, in accordance with the principle — يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ أَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ إِلَى ٱللَّهِۖ وَٱللَّهُ هُوَ ٱلْغَنِىُّ ٱلْحَمِيدُ — 'O mankind, ye are the poor before God, and God, He is the Rich, the absolutely Praiseworthy' (35:15). The Imam's own poverty and rigorous austerity were indeed proverbial. That this was far from a simple shunning of the world for its own sake, however, is made clear in his exchange with an ascetic, 'Aşim b. Ziyad, whose family complained to the Imam that he was excessively abstemious. The Imam told him to think of his family and not to cut himself off from the good things (al-tayyibat) that God has permitted. 'Asim retorted, 'O Commander of the Faithful, and yet here you are, in your rough clothes and your coarse food!' The Imam replied, 'Woe to you — I am not like you. God, the Exalted, has made it incumbent on true leaders (a'immat al-haqq) that they proportion themselves to the weakest among the people (yaqaddiru anfusahum bi-da'fati'l-nas), so that the poverty of the poor will not engender covetousness.' Here we have a rigorous expression of the duty of the most powerful to identify themselves with the most powerless in material terms. It is difficult to conceive of a more effective way of diminishing worldliness and enhancing spirituality in society than this deliberate self-lowering, in purely material terms, to the level of the weakest people over whom one rules. He who could have the most chooses to have the least. The Imam is implying that if a leader is to stand among the a'immat al-haqq, he must manifest his attachment to al-Haqq — the ultimate spiritual reality in whose light material wealth is of no consequence. Thus, even though the most powerful and the most powerless may both be poor in material terms, they alike have access to the treasures of the spiritual domain, in proportion to their realization of the truth expressed in the verse already cited: 'O mankind, ye are the poor before God, and God, He is the Rich, the absolutely Praiseworthy'. It should also be noted that, on the plane of social psychology, the Imam's insistence that rulers live like the poor — as exemplified in his own life — is an effective means of ensuring that the inevitable inequalities of society will not breed envy among the poor.

The compassion of justice

Identification with the poor also deepens the ruler's awareness of his own perpetual need of God, helping him to live more fully in reliance upon divine mercy, in hope and trust; and the natural concomitant of such a life is the bestowal of mercy and compassion upon one's fellow human beings. The capacity to act with compassion in no way conflicts with the demands of justice; rather, it is an intrinsic dimension of justice conceived ontologically — that is, in accordance with the fundamental nature of reality. To act with compassion accords with justice not only in the sense of putting things in their right place — one must extend compassion wherever and whenever it is called for — it also conforms to justice by being at one with the true nature of things, with the intrinsic character of the Real. It is for this reason that at the head of every chapter of the Qur'an (except one) the name 'Allah' is followed by 'the infinitely Compassionate, the ever-Merciful' (al-Rahman, al-Rahim). Put differently, the capacity to act with compassion stems not so much from personal sentiment as from an innate affinity with the very nature of divine reality — that higher reality in which, as the Qur'an tells us, 'My mercy encompasseth all things' (7:156); a reality in which 'thy Lord has prescribed for Himself mercy' (6:54); a reality whose essential character is determined by the principle, 'My mercy takes precedence over My wrath'. Among the many sayings of Ali (ع) on the necessity of showing mercy one finds: 'The dispensing of mercy brings down [divine] mercy.' 'As you grant mercy, so will you be granted mercy.' 'I am astounded by the person who hopes for mercy from one above him, while he is not merciful to those beneath him.' 68

Neither the rigorous aspect of the nature of things nor the 'wrathful' side of God is being denied here, but both are clearly subordinated to the higher ontological principle of mercy. There is, in other words, no common measure between the relativities that are subject to wrath and punishment and the realities, intrinsically beatific, to which divine mercy and compassion give access. One is therefore more 'real' insofar as one's soul reflects this predominance of mercy, both in spiritual orientation and moral conduct. And in the realm of human society, it lies in the very nature of justice — conceived in this sacred manner — to tend towards compassion wherever possible, even though there must also be a place for the rigorous application of corrective penalty where that is unavoidable. Indeed, in his letter the Imam goes on to instruct Malik to inflict corporal punishment on any executive officer found guilty of misappropriating public funds. The Imam's counsels and letters are replete with stern warnings of swift and severe retribution to his governors, the severity of these warnings being proportionate to his unflinching fidelity to the requirements of fairness and honesty in the administration of public revenues. For example, he writes to Ziyad b. Abihi, deputy to the governor of Basra, Ali (ع)'s cousin Abd Allah b. al-'Abbas:

I swear by God an oath in all sincerity: if news reaches me that you have misappropriated the revenue of the Muslims, whether a small or large amount, I shall inflict a severe punishment upon you, one which will lighten your wealth, burden your back, and degrade your affair.

The Imam was equally severe toward his own relatives when there was even a hint of a request for favours requiring the abuse of public funds. In one sermon he refers to an incident in which his blind brother, 'Aqil b. Abi Talib, made a request for funds from the treasury beyond what was his due. 'He thought I would sell my religion for him,' the Imam said. His response was to bring a red-hot piece of iron close to his brother's body — telling him that by making such a request he was urging Ali (ع) himself to enter into a far more intense fire. Similarly, when informed of the misappropriation of funds by his cousin and long-standing ally 'Abd Allah b. al-'Abbas, 73 the Imam's response was a thundering remonstrance. He ordered his cousin: Fear God and return their property to these people. If you do not, and God gives me authority over you, I excuse myself before God in regard to you [and your wrongdoing], and I shall indeed strike you with my sword, with which whomever I have struck enters the fire [of Hell]. By God, even if Hasan and Husayn had done the like of what you have done, they would not have been granted any leniency by me. 74

The mention of the Prophet's grandsons is significant. However close the relationship with a wrongdoer, however exalted their status, the principle of justice demands absolute impartiality — there are to be no favours for one's favourites. This attitude is not only the precise opposite of the nepotism that had undermined the administration of the Imam's predecessor, 75 it is also exactly what the Qur'an instructs: 'O ye who believe, be staunch in justice, witnesses of God, even though it be against yourselves, your parents or your near of kin ...' (4:135).

Returning to the theme of mercy, the following passage is remarkable for placing the necessity of compassion in a universal context. It stands as one of the Imam's most important statements on the unity of the human race and the equality of all human beings, and as a corrective to every form of prejudice, particularism, and sectarianism that would restrict the application of justice or compassion to members of one's own group, however defined:

Infuse your heart with mercy for the subjects, love for them and kindness towards them. Be not like a ravenous beast of prey above them, seeking to devour them. For they are of two types: either your brother in religion or your like in creation. Mistakes slip from them, defects emerge from them, deliberately or accidentally. So bestow upon them your forgiveness and your pardon, just as you would have God bestow upon you His forgiveness and pardon; for you are above them, and the one who has authority over you is above you, and God is above him who appointed you ... and through them He tests you.

The universal scope of compassion is here allied to a reminder of God's absolute sovereignty. No man — whether a governor or the ruler who appointed him — is anything other than a slave of God, utterly dependent upon His mercy. Thus every person who finds himself in a position of relative superiority over others must constantly remember his own inferiority before the Absolute; and this awareness both moves him to compassion toward those beneath him and draws to himself the compassion of God above him.

One might also cite an incident that demonstrates well the Imam's compassionate implementation of justice and serves as a vivid expression of the principle noted earlier: all people are 'your like in creation'. The Imam came across an old, blind beggar and inquired about him. He was told that the beggar was a Christian. He said to those around him, 'You have employed him until he is old and infirm, and now you refuse to help him. Give him maintenance from the public funds (bayt al-mal).' This sentence also expresses succinctly the Islamic principle of social welfare (maslaha), rooted in redistributive justice and a policy of strict non-discrimination between Muslims and others. Social justice and religious equality flow from compassion conceived not merely as a sentiment but as an intrinsic dimension of the Real — and therefore translate into a fundamental religious duty incumbent upon all Muslims, rulers and citizens alike.
