It should be noted that the final sentence of the passage cited earlier, warning the governor that he should regard his power over his subjects not simply as a privilege but as a trial from God, clearly expresses the Imam's own attitude to rulership. In the famous sermon al-Shiqshiqiyya, he says that he accepted power only as an unavoidable duty:

Had God not taken from the learned [a promise] that they would not acquiesce in the rapacity of the tyrant nor in the hunger of the oppressed ... I would truly have flung its reins [that of the caliphate] back upon its withers ... and you would indeed have discovered that this world of yours is as insignificant to me as that which drips from the nose of a goat.

Here we see a striking embodiment of Plato's ideal attitude toward power — the attitude of the true 'philosopher', who acts as ruler solely for the sake of the people, 'not regarding it as a distinction, but as an unavoidable task ... caring only for the right and the honours to be gained from that, and above all, for justice as the one indispensable thing in whose service and maintenance they will reorganize their own state.'

The principle underlying this approach to authority and governance is plainly that of noblesse oblige. When one has been granted the grace, the blessing, or the privilege of knowledge together with a clear sense of justice, a correspondingly greater obligation is created. One's duty to God grows in proportion to the graces bestowed. The Imam gives this principle memorable expression in one of his sermons, delivered after he had been praised by one of his supporters: 'The blessings of God upon a person do not become immense without the right of God over him becoming immense.'

'A good opinion of God'

We come now to a subtle point in the Imam's letter, one that can all too easily be overlooked, given the extreme conciseness with which it is expressed and the fact that its positive counterpart is not explicitly stated. It arises when the Imam is instructing Malik on the kinds of advisers he should keep close to him. He tells him to avoid misers and cowards:

وَلاَ تُدْخِلَنَّ فِي مَشُورَتِكَ بَخِيلاً يَعْدِلُ بِكَ عَنِ الْفَضْلِ، وَيَعِدُكَ الْفَقْرَ، وَلاَ جَبَاناً يُضعِّفُكَ عَنِ الاْمُورِ، وَلاَ حَرِيصاً يُزَيِّنُ لَكَ الشَّرَهَ بِالْجَوْرِ، فَإِنَّ الْبُخْلَ وَالْجُبْنَ وَالْحِرْصَ غَرَائِزُ شَتَّى يَجْمَعُهَا سُوءُ الظَّنِّ بِاللهِ.

Do not include among those you consult a miser who would hold you back from generosity and warn you of destitution; nor a coward who would make you feel too weak for your affairs; nor a greedy person who would make the amassing of wealth by evil means seem attractive to you. For miserliness, cowardice and avarice are so many diverse inclinations, all united by a bad opinion of God.

The phrase 'a bad opinion' (⟪ar:سُوءُ الظَّنِّ بِاللهِ⟫, sū' al-zann) points to a fundamental misunderstanding of the divine nature, one that gives rise to a series of misconceptions about the qualities of God. When the divine nature is misunderstood, these very vices follow: the miser holds the bad opinion that God may not provide in the future; the coward believes that God may not protect him; and the greedy person feels that God has given him less than he needs. From this perspective, all human vices trace back to an intellectual dysfunction, which is itself the consequence of weak faith — or even outright atheism, since disbelief in God is a denial of His reality and therefore a form of 'bad opinion' regarding Him. The converse follows logically: a strong and deep faith generates a 'good opinion' (⟪ar:حسن الظن⟫, husn al-zann) of God — an accurate intellectual and contemplative perception of the divine Reality — and this good opinion in turn causes the soul to be truly impressed, and not merely superficially so, by the divine qualities that Reality contains. These qualities must be understood not only as transcendent, being absolutely one with the divine Essence and utterly beyond the reach of the human soul; they must also be grasped as models, exemplars, patterns, and perfections for the soul to emulate within the measure of its possibilities.

This is the very opposite, it should be noted, of 'comparing oneself with God in greatness'. One must be as much 'like' God as possible, yet this spiritual development unfolds entirely within the unalterable context of one's servitude to Him — it is the emulation of God's qualities, not the personification of Him. In other words, one must be generous because God is al-Karim (⟪ar:الکریم⟫), the Generous, and compassionate and merciful because He is al-Rahman (⟪ar:الرحمن الرحیم⟫), the Compassionate — not because I am generous or merciful of myself. But one must equally be lowly, humble, and self-effacing, for no degree of virtue or intelligence can reduce the incommensurability between the Absolute and the relative, between the Creator and the created. All such virtues as humility and self-effacement are ways of expressing the absolute dependence of the human being upon God, or of manifesting the transcendence of the Absolute. Thus, between the human and the divine there is both positive and inverse analogy: in terms of the first, 'He who shows not mercy will not have mercy shown to him'; and in terms of the second, as cited above:

يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ أَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ إِلَى ٱللَّهِۖ وَٱللَّهُ هُوَ ٱلْغَنِىُّ ٱلْحَمِيدُ

'O mankind, ye are the poor before God, and God, He is the Rich, the absolutely Praiseworthy' (35: 15). There must therefore be a synthesis between the active human virtues, as positive reflections of the divine qualities, and the spiritual consciousness of one's nothingness before the divine Reality — that inverted reflection, on the human plane, of divine transcendence. It is like looking into a mirror: what one sees is oneself, yet seen in reverse; and when one looks into the divine mirror, the goal is to see, on the human plane, an inverted reflection of divine transcendence. One is simultaneously slave and representative of God, 'abdu'Llah (⟪ar:عباد اللہ⟫) and khalifatu'Llah (⟪ar:خلیفۃ اللہ⟫). A consciousness of one's servitude ensures that one does not slide — even unconsciously — from representing God to rivalling Him in grandeur; while an active commitment to representing God ensures that this awareness of servitude does not collapse into a forgetfulness of the dignity and responsibilities that belong to the human state, giving rise to spiritual paralysis and the neglect of one's duty toward the world. The equilibrium between these two dimensions of human existence is itself another aspect of sacred justice — of placing things in their right order — and it attunes the quality of moral action to the harmony of pure being.

State and society

The Imam then sets out in his letter a description of the different classes of people within state and society: the army, the scribes, the judiciary, executive officers, tax-collectors, peasants, merchants, artisans, and finally the destitute — each class with its particular rights and duties. The instructions in this section of the letter can be regarded as justice at its sharpest edge in actual practice, taking full account of the demands of social and political life. The governor is to regard each class under his authority as possessing both rights and obligations, yet the essential character of the governor's relationship to society as a whole is, once again, determined by his primary duty to God — for it is God who has prescribed to each class its share and has ordained, as a binding covenant with us from Him, the limits and duties of each, according to His Book or the Sunna of the Prophet:

وَكُلٌّ قَدْ سَمَّى اللهُ سَهْمَهُ، وَوَضَعَ عَلَى حَدِّهِ وَفَرِيضَتِهِ فِي كِتَابِهِ أَوْ سُنَّةِ نَبِيِّهِ (صلى الله عليه وآله) عَهْداً مِنْهُ عِنْدَنَا مَحْفُوظاً

God has ordained the share of every one of them and laid down His covenant about the limits of each in His Book and the Sunna of His Prophet by way of a settlement which is preserved with us.

The governor's duty to society is therefore part of the universal covenant between man and God, the 'ahd (⟪ar:عہد⟫) encountered so frequently in the Qur'an. Here again, one senses that by invoking this Qur'anic term to reinforce the obligation of acting justly toward all classes of society, the Imam knew that the force of his words would be deepened immeasurably through their resonance with verses such as:

‏ أَفَمَن يَعْلَمُ أَنَّمَآ أُنزِلَ إِلَيْكَ مِن رَّبِّكَ ٱلْحَقُّ كَمَنْ هُوَ أَعْمَىٰٓ ۚ إِنَّمَا يَتَذَكَّرُ أُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْأَلْبَبِ ‎۱۹‏ ٱلَّذِينَ يُوفُونَ بِعَهْدِ ٱللَّهِ وَلَا يَنقُضُونَ ٱلْمِيثَقَ ‎۲۰

'Is one who knoweth that what is revealed to thee from thy Lord is the truth like one who is blind? But only men of substance take heed, those who abide by the covenant ('ahd) of God, and break not the tryst (mithaq)' (13: 19–20); and:

‏ وَلَا تَقْرَبُوا۟ مَالَ ٱلْيَتِيمِ إِلَّا بِٱلَّتِى هِىَ أَحْسَنُ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ أَشُدَّهُۥ ۚ وَأَوْفُوا۟ بِٱلْعَهْدِ ۖ إِنَّ ٱلْعَهْدَ كَانَ مَسْـُٔولًۭا ‎۳۴

'Approach not the wealth of the orphan except with that which is more right, until he attains maturity; and abide by the covenant — truly, the covenant will be asked about' (17: 34).

It is also significant that, immediately before the instructions cited above, the Imam advises Malik to study at length with the 'scholars' (al-ulama) and to converse at length with the 'sages' (al-hukama):

وَأَكْثِرْ مُدَارَسَةَ الَعُلَمَاءِ، وَمُنَافَثَةَ الْحُكَمَاءِ، فِي تَثْبِيتِ مَا صَلَحَ عَلَيْهِ أَمْرُ بِلاَدِكَ، وَإِقَامَةِ مَا اسْتَقَامَ بِهِ النَّاسُ قَبْلَكَ

This should be done so as to 'consolidate that which brings well-being to your lands, and to further entrench that which has already been established by your predecessors.' The distinction between scholars and sages matters here. The scholars (⟪ar:علماء⟫) are those who possess knowledge of the formal rules as established in the Qur'an and the Sunna — the letter of the law, the Sharia (⟪ar:شریعۃ⟫); the sages (⟪ar:حکما⟫) are those who know how to apply that knowledge, which is the spirit of wisdom — for wisdom is knowledge put into practice. It is the distinction between the letter of the law and the spirit behind it, the spirit without which the rules cannot be applied with care and sensitivity.

There follows a passage in which the interdependence of each class or segment of society is described with admirable conciseness. It begins with the army as the foundation of the state and ends with the governor himself as the centre around which the different elements revolve. The image is that of a wheel: the governor is the hub, operating on the horizontal plane with creation; the various classes — the army, the scribes, the executive officers, the dispensers of justice, the tax-collectors, the merchants, the artisans, and the lowest class of the needy and destitute — are the spokes radiating from him and connecting to the rim. Yet the hub itself is nothing without the axle that turns it. This horizontal centre is nothing without the vertical axis that connects it — and through it, the whole of society — with the Real. Just as a hub is useless without its connection to the axle, all the governor's efforts are useless without his connection to the Absolute, upon which he must be wholly dependent if society is truly to flourish. The soldiers are described as the 'fortresses' of the subjects, and at the close of this passage the governor is told that he cannot accomplish what God requires of him without resolute determination and recourse to Him (al-ihtimam and al-isti'ana). This crucial point will be reinforced later in the letter when the needs of the poor are addressed. As regards the instructions pertaining to the different categories of state and society, it suffices for our present purposes to draw attention to a few salient points. Concerning the commander of the troops, Malik is told to select for this position the person who is 'the most sincere in relation to God, the Prophet and your Imam, the purest of heart, the one most excellent in forbearance (hilm), who is slow to anger, happy to pardon, kind to the weak, severe with the strong; one who is neither stirred by violence nor held back by weakness.'

This portrait of the ideal commander, needless to say, reflects the Imam's own character. What deserves notice is the emphasis placed on kindness, gentleness, and forbearance — qualities not ordinarily associated with fighting men. One discerns here a spiritually refined approach to an activity which, left to its own devices, easily runs roughshod over the norms of decency and propriety. Once again, one cannot help noting the resemblance between the Imam's description of the perfect warrior and Plato's description of the 'guardian', the protector of the ideal state. Plato identifies the central difficulty in forming forces for the protection and defence of the city as follows: how to ensure that men whose natural disposition is 'spirited' and courageous are 'to be kept from behaving pugnaciously to one another and to the rest of their countrymen'? For 'they must be gentle to their own people and dangerous only to their enemies.' This echoes the Qur'anic description of those who are with the Prophet as being 'severe against the disbelievers, compassionate amongst themselves' (48: 29) — whence the Imam's insistence on the gentle qualities required in a warrior as the essential complement to the martial virtues.

Turning to the next category, the judges, the Imam identifies the following as key virtues that must be present in those who dispense legal justice: they must not be confused by complexities or provoked by litigants; they must be unflinching in the face of truth, free from greed, dissatisfied with superficial solutions, rigorous in argumentation, steadfast in the search for the truth of every matter, and immune to praise and temptation. 'Such people', the Imam adds, 'are indeed rare.' He also recommends that judges be paid generously, 'so that any deficiency will be removed, thus diminishing the need for help from people' — a prerequisite for maintaining the incorruptibility of any judiciary.

As regards the administrators, Malik is told to test their honesty before employing them and to be scrupulous in ensuring that no partiality or favouritism influences his choices. He must continue to monitor and investigate their conduct. As noted earlier, stringent punishments are to be administered whenever there is evidence of abuse of privilege or misappropriation of public funds.

Concerning those who till the soil and from whom the land tax is collected, the Imam offers this wise maxim: 'Let your concern with the cultivation of the land outweigh your concern with the collection of the tax, for no tax will be collected if there be no cultivation. And whoever exacts the tax without cultivating the land ruins the land and destroys the people.' The Imam's further exhortations to attend to the needs of the tillers of the soil and to be fair to all who seek redress of wrongs are concluded with the following warning:

The devastation of the land only comes about through the destitution of its inhabitants; and the destitution of its inhabitants only comes about when the desire to amass wealth rules the souls of the governors, when they have doubts about what endures, and when they profit little from exemplary teachings.

As regards the phrase 'having doubts about what endures (su' zannihim bi'l-baqa)', the commentators suggest it may mean either that the governors think they will endure forever, forgetting that they will die and be judged in the Hereafter, or that they doubt their position will last for long and therefore exploit whatever opportunities are available to them while they can. The first explanation seems more likely, given how closely it echoes the following Qur'anic verses, which may themselves be counted among the 'exemplary teachings' (ibra) the Imam mentions at the very end of the sentence: 'Woe be to every slandering backbiter, who amasseth wealth and counteth it. He thinketh that his wealth will render him immortal. Nay, but he will be flung to the all-consuming Fire' (104: 1–4).

The Imam uses the same word jama'a for the amassing of wealth that appears in this verse. The lesson is plain: greed is a symptom of spiritual myopia. Forgetfulness of what truly endures gives rise to varying degrees of greed — understood here as desire for a false material richness in place of a true spiritual plenitude — and this in turn breeds exploitation and oppression. Once more one observes how, in the Imam's vision, the vice of injustice not only produces corruption but is itself the product of a chain of causes rooted in a fundamental inversion of spiritual values — a failure to perceive the true nature of things.

The advice given regarding the appointment of scribes is likewise sealed with a spiritual message. Malik is told he must resist the temptation to appoint only those who have shown him their best side. The governor must examine each candidate's prior record together with the impression he has left on ordinary people, and then appoint only those well-known for their trustworthiness. Doing all of this is described as 'proof of your sincerity towards God.'

Merchants and craftsmen are then described by the Imam as those who bring diverse benefits to society and must therefore be treated well. On the whole they are peaceable and should be left in peace to pursue their work, but the vices to which they are prone must be kept in check: hoarding, miserliness, and cheating.


<!-- page 115 -->
The Imam now turns to a point in the letter he wants Malik to take especially to heart: the poor, those most in need of justice. He opens the passage with the exclamation 'Allah, Allah' to signal its urgency. The instruction concerns those with no resources whatsoever — the destitute, the crippled, orphans, the elderly — those who are 'most in need of justice from you' and who must be treated in a manner such that 'God may excuse you on the day you meet Him.' All of these, along with others who are in need but too proud to beg, should be provided for by the governor, who ought to appoint an officer with the specific charge of uncovering the needs of the most destitute and attending to them. The Imam then adds words that go to the heart of the matter: 'This is onerous for the governors, and [the fulfilment of] all rights is onerous, but God makes it light for those who aspire to the Hereafter, who restrain their souls in patience, and trust in the truth of that which is promised them by God.'

What deserves emphasis here is that the spiritual dimension is precisely what makes a otherwise crushing ideal practicable: divine assistance is assured for those whose attention is not confined to this world alone but whose aspirations extend beyond it to the Hereafter. It is only when this world is seen through to the next that a fully just attitude toward this world becomes possible. Those whose talab — their aspiration — does not reach beyond this world are far more likely to take the easy path of neglecting the destitute, because in the horizontal calculus of political calculation the costs to a ruler of pursuing a policy of charity toward the poor may well outweigh any discernible benefit to himself. This is where an ethics tied to an exclusively this-worldly assessment, to pragmatic politics, or to personal interest exposes its inherent flaws. But when the notion of right in relation to the poor is charged with firm faith in God's inexorable justice and infinite compassion, and suffused with aspiration for the Hereafter, an attitude of unflinching fidelity to the needs of the destitute will be generated and continually renewed.

Such an attitude goes far beyond conventional notions of charity or generosity. A person who has realized this attitude to perfection will not be susceptible to any contrary suggestion rooted in self-serving pragmatism or individualism, let alone in indifference or wilfulness. With this attitude firmly in place, the next quality the Imam mentions — patience — follows naturally. In the light of a concrete rather than merely notional presentiment of the absolute values at stake, patience can be extended indefinitely, no longer dependent on personal will alone but sustained by the empowering grace that flows from heartfelt faith. The Imam completes his point by noting that those with the right aspirations consequently trust in the truth of God's promise: if that promise — of His reward, His unfailing justice, His infinite compassion — is true, then the motivation behind the governor's effort to extend mercy to the poor and deal with them justly is immeasurably deepened. The task the Imam describes as 'onerous' — the obligation to help the weak and helpless, those from whose assistance no political benefit may be derived — is transformed into an inescapable duty that follows directly from one's spiritual conviction. That conviction rests on the certitude that such generosity and compassion are not merely good, right, and proper, but that these virtues accord with the true and inalienable substance of ultimate reality. The moral implications of spiritual reality — and equally the spiritual repercussions of virtue — are hidden from worldly rulers by their covert prejudices at best and their overt vices at worst. Such rulers are also blind to the truth of the divine promise of ultimate felicity, and equally blind to the warnings of retribution for the oppressors, al-zalimun. Here the zalim may be understood as one who defies the divine commandments on justice and, more fundamentally, violates the quality of compassion that lies at the very heart of the divine nature.

These considerations open onto the altogether crucial question of intention. Even if the just person's effort to live according to justice is frustrated by the unpredictabilities of worldly life, the right intentions motivating him — together with his awareness of the immutable divine qualities as models and principles guiding his endeavour — produce a real, inward 'taste' of the grace that furnishes the spiritual foundation of his moral life. If God's promise is true, and one is absolutely certain of that truth, then the impact of that truth on the soul of the sincere believer transforms a personal ethic into an unconditional imperative and a desirable value into an incontrovertible reality. Morality is thus deepened by — and into — spirituality: an existential, all-encompassing mode of being within which the burden of individual 'duty' is not only immeasurably lightened but transformed into an accompaniment of one's 'taste' of spiritual reality. One then acts justly out of gratitude for what one has already received — the 'joy of spiritual certainty', as seen in Chapter 1 — and not only out of hope for something not yet attained.

The Qur'an speaks of the paradisal reward as something already granted to the true believer; for one whose certainty of Paradise is absolute, the future has already arrived: 'We have given thee [the paradisal fountain of] al-Kawthar; so pray to thy Lord and sacrifice ...' (108: 1–2). Worshipping God as a consequence of the certainty of the beatitude to come — and which has thus, in one respect, already come — is well expressed in the following saying of the Imam:

Indeed there is a group who worship God out of desire [for something not yet attained]; and this is the worship of the merchants. And there is a group who worship God out of fear, and this is the worship of the slaves. And there is a group who worship God out of gratitude, and this is the worship of the free.

<!-- page 116 -->
Furthermore, the just person is liberated from the material consequences of his actions — in proportion to the rectitude and sincerity of his intentions — since in the Islamic perspective actions are evaluated not by their consequences but by their intentions. One's intention is to be just: not for the sake of any earthly reward or tangible consequence in this world, but purely for the sake of justice itself. And this essence or principle of justice is in turn inseparable from the divine nature. For justice is at one with God not simply because whatever God commands is just; rather, God commands just acts precisely because they are just, and because justice is one with His own nature, as we noted at the outset: 'I bear witness that He is Justice and He acts justly.' The point is worth pressing: justice is not one attribute among others, something merely present in the divine nature — it is that nature. Were it only in His nature it would stand alongside His other qualities; but because it is His very essence, all His other qualities essentially flow from it, so that His mercy no less than His wrath, His creation no less than His destruction, all proceed from His justice. If God acts justly by virtue of His very nature — which is pure justice — then the spiritual person will act justly out of a desire to conform to God's nature, and not simply out of obedience to God's commands, and never out of desire for any earthly reward. This can be seen in any single virtue. If one speaks the truth without grasping the reason for it — that truth, ⟪ar:الحق⟫, is the very nature of the Creator, and that our goal in this world is to take on the qualities of that Creator — then truthfulness soon becomes situational and self-serving, open to compromise whenever circumstances seem to warrant it: I speak the truth when it benefits me and lie when that benefits me. But once truth is seen as the nature of the divine, it becomes everlasting and eternal, and the consequences of speaking it cease to matter; one speaks it simply in emulation of ⟪ar:الحق⟫, the absolute truth. Virtue practised on an ad hoc basis is no virtue at all — merely a series of pragmatic adaptations to shifting circumstances. This is why the life of Ali (ع), when we study it, reflects an unflinching adherence to the divine virtues: his was a life governed not by his ⟪ar:نفس⟫ but entirely by the divine nature of Allah, his every celebrated action the fruit of an irresistible attraction toward the nature of ⟪ar:الحق⟫. His gaze was never fixed on the action itself but on the source of the principles generating it; by fixing his attention on the beauty of the divine nature and seeking to take it on, good actions emerged from him naturally and inevitably. As the Qur'an says:

The most pious will be far removed from it [the punishment of Hell], he who giveth of his wealth to purify himself. And nobody possesseth, in the sight of such a person, any favour that might be bestowed on him as reward [for his generosity]. [His action is performed] seeking only the Countenance of his transcendent Lord, and he will be content (92: 17–21).

The same principle is expressed in the description of the righteous, the abrar. Commentators on the Qur'an agree that the occasion for the revelation (sabab al-nuzul) of the following verses was when Ali (ع) and his family went three consecutive days without food. At the end of each day, just as they were about to break their fast, someone in greater need came and asked for what they had. The Qur'an relates this as follows: 'They feed, out of love for God, the needy, the orphan and the prisoner, saying, "We feed you only for the sake of God; we desire from you neither reward nor thanks"' (76: 8–9).

<!-- page 117 -->
In the very measure that good action is performed for the sake of that absolute goodness which God is, the one performing it is imperturbably content — untouched by the blame or praise of others, neither deflated by outward adversity that thwarts his good actions nor inflated by their successful outcome. This understanding of the relation between action and intention helps us appreciate the meaning of the Imam's saying cited above: 'their hearts are in the Gardens [of Paradise] while their bodies are at work.' Here again one observes the difference between a secular ethic of justice — one devoid of all spiritual or transcendental points of reference — and a spiritual conception of justice. A secular ethic stands or falls according to the degree of outward success in realising its ideal of justice; a spiritual conception already sees the fulfilment of its ideal in the sincere intention to be just. That said, the fulfilment of a spiritual ideal of justice essentially requires an unyielding struggle to achieve it — for without that struggle the intention is shown to lack sincerity. Without concordant action, aspirations and intentions remain shallow and purely theoretical. To hold an intention with total sincerity strictly requires diligent, ceaseless striving toward its realization. Thus, however formidable the obstacles, however compelling the arguments of political pragmatism, however thoroughly one's strenuous efforts are undone by the unavoidable exigencies of outward life, the just person never despairs; he never abandons the effort to achieve justice, precisely because achieving justice in this world is not the whole of his aim. He knows that the intention is already, in an invisible but spiritually palpable way, its own fulfilment; and he knows, with sober realism, that the outward realization of any intention depends on circumstances beyond his control and, ultimately, on the grace of God.

Now, while this may appear on the surface to excuse the failure to achieve justice in society — and thus to be exploitable as a subterfuge by which actual injustice is legitimated by appealing to good but thwarted intentions — the very opposite is in fact the case. Far from implying any lack of resolve on the part of one motivated by a sacred conception of justice, such a person's efforts will on the contrary be as ceaseless and unyielding as the eternal and unfailing reward in which he fervently believes. Those efforts will also be sustained by an awareness of the subtle continuity between just intentions and the divine quality of justice, and thereby the divine nature itself. An inalienable sense of the sacred, then, sustains the just person in his pursuit of justice. The argument here is not that all secular or atheistic conceptions of justice are without value; only that, compared with a sacred conception, they will lack those immeasurable and inexhaustible spiritual resources that are nourished by sincere faith. One need only consider the theories that dominate the present age. Relativism holds that knowledge, truth, and morality are not absolute but exist only in relation to culture, society, or historical context. Nihilism rejects all religious and moral principle and holds that life is essentially meaningless. Utilitarianism counts an action right only insofar as it is useful or benefits the majority, reducing good and bad to a calculus of gain and loss with no fundamental principle beneath them. And opportunism takes whatever advantage presents itself regardless of principle, in the manner of the manipulator who flatters, performs, and pretends — saying or doing whatever it takes to get what he wants. These are human constructions, and the world arrived at them by severing practice from principle. Whatever value may emerge from them is not rooted in the divine, so that even where they coincide with the values of faith they do so by coincidence and remain, for all their machinery, without spirit — dead corpses. 'With men this is impossible, but with God all things are possible,' as Jesus says in the Gospel. Translated into the present context, that principle might be stated as follows: when justice is conceived in isolation from spiritual principles, personal idealism is acutely vulnerable to outward contingencies and all too likely to disintegrate under the pressure of difficulty and disappointment; in a sacred conception of justice, by contrast, divine grace upholds the incorruptible ideal of justice high above the frailties and vicissitudes to which both the human soul and human society are subject. To the extent that one's ideals are rooted in spiritual knowledge, one's susceptibility to disappointment and despair in the face of difficulty is diminished.

<!-- page 118 -->
The importance of this ideal of justice is clearly visible in the Imam's own experience as caliph: a continuous succession of bloody conflicts against foes who professed Islam prevented him outwardly from enacting many of the principles and policies that flowed from his conception of truth and justice — yet none of this produced dejection, despair, or cynicism, nor did it diminish in the slightest his efforts to achieve justice wherever and whenever possible, as is amply demonstrated by the quality of his own administration and by the steady stream of letters and counsels to his officers and governors, including the letter now under consideration. Such despair is almost inconceivable for one whose inner life is shaped by the conviction that 'No act is negligible if it is accompanied by piety.' For such a person, despair is excluded by piety in the deepest sense of the word — that is, the permanent awareness of divine Reality; for this degree of piety is the makhraj, the 'way out', from the darkness of all earthly injustice. As the Imam says, 'He who has pious awe towards God, for him God establishes a way out of tribulation, and a light out of darkness (man yattiqi'Llah yaj'al lahu makhrajan min al-fitan wa nuran min al-zulam).'


<!-- page 116 -->
We now come to what is arguably the lynchpin of the entire epistle: the most important means by which the sacred substance of all the virtues is assimilated by the ruler — namely, prayer. We saw at the opening of the letter how the Imam began with a counsel to observe the obligatory prayers. At this point, after stressing the need to perform all administrative tasks at their proper times, the Imam instructs Malik to set apart the 'most excellent' portion of his available time for 'what is between you and God' — that is, for acts of worship and devotion. Malik is urged to pay special attention to the obligatory prayers as the means by which he 'purifies' his religion for God, or renders sincere his devotion to God. This instruction is not to be taken merely as an exhortation to perform the formal prayers; it must also, and above all, be understood in the light of the deeper meaning of 'purifying one's religion for God' — that is, purifying the very substance of the relationship between the soul and God, for it is the depth of that relationship which determines the quality of all human virtue. As the Imam put it in the saying noted above: 'Know that your every act is dependent upon your prayer' — in other words, your every action depends upon the way you gaze upon God, who is the source of all good. What matters is not only the formal, physical, and verbal performance of the prayer, but the inward quality that accompanies it and enlivens it from within. The sincerity, intensity, and frequency of prayer are essential to its efficacy. The Imam continues: 'Give unto God of your vital energy in your nights and your days, and perform fully that by which you draw near to God, doing so perfectly, without becoming dull or deficient, taking your body to its limits (balighan min badanika mā balagha).'

All the emphasis here should fall on the word taqarrub, 'drawing near'. One attains divine nearness most directly through deep devotion, offered up alone, in the dead of night, with all one's heart. Once that nearness is realized, all one's other actions in the world are imbued with something of the spiritual quality gained through divine proximity. It is in this manner that all action can be assimilated to 'devotion' in the wider sense — all outward actions being truly governed by the sincere intention to give oneself to God. If one's inner life is dominated by prayer, prayer comes to penetrate one's outer life as well. Hence, as the Imam said, immediately after telling Malik to set apart the best of his time for God: 'all times [and the actions performed therein] are for God if the intention underlying them is good and if your subjects derive security as a consequence.' In other words, all the actions of a ruler will partake of the sacred to the degree that his devotion truly reaches into the source of the sacred. One can then envisage an overflow, as it were, from the fountain of obligatory and voluntary prayer into the outward domain of action. This subtle truth is beautifully expressed in the famous hadith qudsi of the Prophet, often referred to as the hadith of taqarrub:

My slave draws near to Me through nothing I love more than that which I have made obligatory for him. My slave never ceases to draw near to Me through supererogatory acts until I love him. And when I love him, I am his hearing by which he hears, his sight by which he sees, his hand by which he grasps, and his foot by which he walks.

To 'see' and act through God can be understood, at one level at least, as seeing things as they really are and then acting upon that vision — in other words, it implies perfect justice, placing each thing in its proper order. This is why the Imam repeatedly draws the reader of his sermons back to the 'vision' of God, setting that vision as the true foundation of a life of virtue: in prayer we efface the ego through prostration, and this submission to God through self-effacement is itself what puts all things in their right place, including — and especially — the ego. It is important to note in the hadith qudsi above the distinction between the obligatory and the supererogatory acts of devotion. Both are means of 'drawing near', but it is primarily through the latter that the slave comes to perceive and act through the divine. It is equally important to note the Imam's phrase, 'taking your body to its limits'. Strenuous effort is called for; it is far from sufficient to perform the prayers and devotions in a mechanical or perfunctory manner. 'He who prays without making an effort,' says the Imam, 'is like one who shoots arrows without a bow.' As noted earlier, the sincerity (ikhlas) demanded by the Imam's conception of the oneness of God calls for the integration of the soul, and it is in prayer that the deepest dimensions of one's soul are expressed, cultivated, and integrated. The prayer must therefore be accomplished not merely with one's tongue or one's body, but with all that one is.

Elsewhere, Ali (ع) refers to the key supererogatory practice by which the spiritual station of 'nearness' is attained. He does so in a commentary on the following words of a Qur'anic verse: '… men whom neither commerce nor trade diverts from the remembrance of God' (24: 36).

Truly God has made the remembrance (al-dhikr) a polish for the hearts, by which they hear after suffering from deafness, and see after being blind … There have always been slaves of God … with whom He held intimate discourse through their thoughts and spoke with them through the essence of their intellects. They diffused illumination through the awakened light in their hearing and their seeing and their hearts, calling unto the remembrance of the days of God.

The importance of the dhikr — both as a principle of awareness of ultimate reality and as a concrete means of attaining that awareness — is clearly paramount in the Imam's perspective. It must be stressed that by the word dhikr the Imam implies both a methodic practice of invoking a divine Name and a principle of permanent awareness of God embracing all modes of worship, meditation, and reflection. It is to be understood not only as being aware of God or remembering God, but also as an intense mode of prolonged, prayerful meditation on ultimate reality, represented both symbolically and sacramentally by one or more of the divine Names. For this remembrance is not the learning of new things from scratch; it is the recollection of ultimate reality — of the immutable, unchanging principles and divine attributes that are inherent in the very substance of our intellects, by which everything can be understood, and which are ultimately rooted in the divine nature of our Creator. (This aspect of remembrance will be addressed more fully in the following chapter.) The relationship between the remembrance of God and the cultivation of justice is made clear in this sermon, for the Imam describes those illuminated by the dhikr as follows:

Truly there are people who belong to the dhikr; they have adopted it in place of the world, such that 'neither commerce nor trade' distracts them from it; they spend the days of their life in it … they instruct people about justice and themselves are steadfast therein.

It is those souls who truly 'belong to the dhikr' who are not only 'steadfast' in justice but also able to 'instruct people about justice'; putting first things first, they are able to show how to place all things in their proper order. Fervent, lived spirituality is therefore not opposed to dedicated activity in the world — it infuses that activity with an ethical depth that springs from direct contact with the divine, a contact articulated by prayer in general and by the dhikr in particular. Just as it was seen earlier that the sincerity of a good intention is proven by the effort to realize it, so it is clear now that the sincerity of prayer is likewise proven — and rendered efficacious — through the sustained effort to prolong and intensify it, 'taking your body to its limits'. What this has to do with justice should be clear: if justice is grasped as a quality rooted in the Real (al-Haqq), then nearness to the Real cannot but draw one nearer to justice and to all positive qualities, spiritual, intellectual, and moral. The relationship between worship and justice must therefore be firmly established if the conception of justice is to be qualified as 'sacred'. From this perspective, justice is both cause and consequence of worship. On one hand, justice requires us to give everything its due, and God must accordingly be given His due — which entails, sacramentally, worship and prayer. On the other hand, justice emerges ethically as a consequence of that worship, as do all the virtues, in proportion to the 'nearness' granted by God to His worshipper and in accordance with the radiant repercussions of that nearness.

Gazing on the Good

We have made occasional references above to certain similarities between principles expressed in the Imam's letter and those discussed in Plato's Republic. This chapter can be brought to a close by proposing that what the Imam offers in his stress on prayer — together with its essence, the remembrance of God — is a devotional principle that illuminates and, in a sense, sublimates Plato's philosophical position, and might be seen as rendering explicit in spiritual terms what remains implicit in Plato's pedagogy. After discussing the question of justice at great length, Socrates comes, in Chapter 23 of the Republic, to address the crux of the matter: the nature of the Sovereign Good, which is of paramount concern to the ideal ruler, the 'guardian', whose moral and intellectual formation lies at the heart of the dialogue's practical dimension:

'… the highest object of knowledge is the essential nature of the Good, from which everything that is good and right derives its value for us … I need not tell you that, without that knowledge, to know everything else, however well, would be of no value to us …'

Yet Socrates declines to respond to Glaucon's entreaties to describe the nature of this Good, offering instead to speak of its 'offspring'. This leads him to draw an analogy between the Good and the Sun: what the Sun is to visibility, the Good is to intelligibility and being. He proceeds with the famous allegory of the cave and then describes the educational programme that would turn the attention of his prospective rulers from the world of shadows to the world of real objects — a programme comprising mathematics, arithmetic, geometry, solid geometry, astronomy, harmonics, and dialectic. Having successfully passed through all stages of this education, 'when they are fifty, those who have come safely through and proved the best at all points in action and in study must be brought at last to the goal. They must lift up the eye of the soul to gaze on that which sheds light on all things; and when they have seen the Good itself, take it as a pattern for the right ordering of the state and of the individual, themselves included.' Yet Plato does not anywhere state what this 'gazing' actually means, nor how it is to be achieved — by what means, intellective, contemplative, or methodic.

At an important point in the Republic, Glaucon presses Socrates on the definition of the Sovereign Good: 'But, Socrates, what is your account of the Good? Is it knowledge, or pleasure, or something else?' Socrates refuses to give a direct answer, saying after some further discussion: 'I am afraid it is beyond my powers; with the best will in the world I should only disgrace myself and be laughed at.' It is interesting to note in this connection that Dhi'lib al-Yamanī, very much in the manner of Glaucon, provocatively asked Ali (ع) whether he had seen the object of his devotion. To this the Imam replied: 'I would not be worshipping a lord whom I have not seen.' Dhi'lib then asked, 'O Commander of the Faithful! How did you see Him?' The Imam replied with the important sentence that serves as one of the leitmotifs of our book: 'O Dhi'lib! Eyes see Him not through sight's observation, but hearts see Him through the verities of faith.'

We are reminded here of the 'polishing' of the heart effected by remembrance — by ⟪ar:ذکر⟫ — and this very word evokes the essence of Plato's approach to knowledge as 'recollection', anamnesis. For remembrance in this sense is not the learning of something new from scratch, but the recollection of ultimate reality — of the immutable divine principles and attributes inherent in the very substance of our intellects, by which everything else can be understood. Yet, as noted above, Plato does not in any of his dialogues make a direct reference to the devotional, methodic, or contemplative counterpart of his educational, ethical, and intellective endeavour. There is no explicitly postulated link between 'remembrance' of the 'forms/ideas' that engender true knowledge and the 'gazing upon the Good' — the contemplation of 'that which sheds light on all things'; in other words, not merely knowing and remembering the 'forms', but coming to know 'the Bestower of Forms', which in Islam is referred to by the divine Name al-Musawwir. There is speculation as to whether there was any spiritual method in Plato's teaching, and if there was, why he did not divulge it. One answer may be gathered from a statement in Plato's famous Seventh Letter, where he denies that any exposition can be given of the true reality of philosophy, and points instead to the 'disciplined way of life' and rigorous 'daily routine' that the subject demands if philosophy in the deepest sense is to be realized. 'No treatise by me concerning it exists or ever will exist. It is not something that can be put into words like other branches of learning; only after long partnership in a common life devoted to this very thing does truth flash upon the soul, like a flame kindled by a leaping spark, and once it is born there, it nourishes itself thereafter.' This brings us back to the importance of active spirituality for the practice of authentic virtue. If the Real — indefinable in its essence — is the foundation of all good, the means of assimilating the Real existentially, and not merely comprehending it mentally, cannot be set aside. Plato's philosophical approach to the Good can be augmented and deepened by an appreciation of Ali (ع)'s means of 'drawing near' to the Real, for in the Imam's perspective intellectual comprehension and spiritual devotion are inseparable and complementary. Integral, deep-rooted justice emerges as one of the fruits of this 'drawing near', just as, for Plato, gazing on 'that which sheds light on all things … the Good itself' results in the capacity to take the Good 'as a pattern for the right ordering of the state and of the individual'.

In this chapter we have attempted to sketch the outlines of a spiritual approach to the theme of justice, as this presents itself principally in the Imam's letter to Malik al-Ashtar. We are fully aware that we have only skimmed the surface of this important subject, but hope nonetheless to have indicated that the Imam's illumination of the principle of justice — addressed in the first instance to a prospective governor of Egypt in the first century of the Islamic era — is by no means outmoded. Whether one is operating in an avowedly Islamic or an overtly secular environment, whether the rule be autocratic or democratic, whether political institutions be strong or weak: wherever individuals are vested with power and authority — and by that very token exposed to the temptations that power offers — the approach to justice articulated in the Imam's ethos will not be irrelevant. For this ethos does not presuppose any particular form of government; it engages the universal theme of moral responsibility, but does so in terms of that which transcends the level of morality, offering insights derived from a direct vision of ultimate spiritual realities. The principles contemplated through this vision surpass the domain of politics, and for that very reason are capable of penetrating deeply and refashioning, from above, the moral substance of political consciousness. So, although the Imam's perspective is particularly pertinent to the interface between spirituality, morality, and governance within Islamic discourse and praxis, its relevance extends to all contexts in which the sense of the sacred plays a part in the articulation of moral sensibility and the cultivation of political responsibility.


We also hope to have demonstrated here that the Imam's letters and discourses are a vast and largely untapped source of inspired ideas, capable of illuminating the role of spiritual principles in formulating ethical values and shaping the quality of political practice. In particular, it should be clear that any integral 'Islamic' approach to politics and society must take into account the requirements and fruits of 'worship' — understanding that word in its widest sense. Sincere worship transforms the notion of the Absolute into a concrete reality, imparting to the soul an existential impetus and a spiritual resource that buttresses, stabilises and deepens one's moral life. What is derived from our consciousness of the divine Reality is not so much a philosophy of ethics as a 'spiritual morality' — an existential orientation that is at once the consequence of a lived spirituality and the cause of its continuous, dynamic and ever-deepening realisation of spiritual truth, the Real as such, al-Haqq. This means that the haqq, or right due to each person or group, will be given in accordance with the principle of justice — that of 'putting each thing in its proper place': ⟪ar:وَضَعَ الشَّئُی حَقَّ مَحَلِّہِ⟫. Virtue, from such a perspective, is a form of 'ethical realism', where 'realism' denotes an assimilation of spiritual reality; and morally correct behaviour translates that realism into action across every plane — individual, social and political.

If it be objected that this perspective is hopelessly utopian, the harsh exigencies of politics and the sublime ideals of spirituality being poles apart, the reply is this: if no effort is made to comprehend the deeper dimensions of Islamic piety and spirituality, then any ostensibly 'Islamic' discourse — whether in politics, sociology, ethics or any other field — will be reduced to an outer shell, stripped of the inner life that gives it all its meaning and, more importantly, its transformative power. For Islamic concepts to be grasped aright, they must be assimilated not merely by the mind but by the heart — failing which they remain mere abstractions. As the Imam insists, it is the heart — the inmost point of consciousness — that 'sees' the true nature of things. This is why the vision of God 'as if one could see Him' lays bare the crucial relationship between worship and virtue, between spirituality and ethics, and equally between devotion to the Creator and kindness towards His creation: one cannot have the one without the other, for to conduct oneself as though one could see God is to live in the consciousness of standing constantly in the divine presence, since even if we cannot see Him, He sees us. Without this vision there is no ihsan in the ethico-spiritual sense discussed earlier, and without ihsan the very integrity of the Islamic way of life is fatally undermined. It is precisely the denial and subversion of this tradition that bears ultimate responsibility for the atrocities committed by a tiny minority of Muslims in our times — people who betray the highest ideals of the Islamic ethical tradition by resorting to unbridled violence in its name, as though one might hurt creation in order to please the Creator. Without ihsan, religion is left acutely vulnerable; it is transformed into an ideology, driven by individual passion and subject to political manipulation. It is no surprise that those most prone to terrorism in the name of Islam have emerged from the currents most hostile to the ihsanī or mystical tradition.

This brings us back to the devotional practices that are all too often regarded as the preserve of a minority — viewed as either mystical or misguided, depending on one's standpoint — rather than being understood as a God-given means of narrowing the gap between what one aspires to and what one is; of moving from the actual towards the ideal; of making concrete what would otherwise remain abstract; of living what is only thought about; and of rendering existentially galvanising what might otherwise be merely theoretically compelling. While the principles established here are universal in their import and applicability, it would be wrong to attempt to 'de-contextualise' the Imam's perspective by dismissing — as so much unnecessary 'superstructure' — his repeated, insistent and uncompromising emphasis on the imperative of prayer. The principle of justice requires that all the elements of one's life — devotion, worship, ethics, action — be bound together in a harmonious, indissoluble and interconnected whole, in accordance with the ideal of tawhid; and tawhid means precisely this integration, the making of many into one, so that when a person's theory and practice have themselves become one, that person has become a true bearer of that oneness. There can be no question of detaching the domain of the virtues from that of spirituality, for in the Imam's perspective the only true virtue is one that is rooted in, sustained by and oriented towards the divine Reality. A virtue that is not so rooted — even one that happens by coincidence to mirror an Islamic value — is baseless and without spirit; cut off from its source, it becomes situational and self-serving, open to compromise the moment circumstances warrant it, and ceases to be a virtue at all, dwindling into a mere series of pragmatic adaptations to shifting conditions. One must therefore conclude that justice, together with all the other virtues, can only be brought to fruition in a soul — and in a society — permeated and penetrated by spirituality, the essence of which is the principle and practice of the remembrance of God. And this remembrance, this dhikr, is not the learning of something entirely new; it is a recollection of the ultimate Reality, a recollection of the immutable principles and unchanging qualities inherent in the very substance of our intellects — principles by which everything else can be understood, and which are ultimately rooted in the divine nature of our Creator. Remembrance of this kind is nothing less than the cognitive and philosophical foundation of ethical thought in Islam. In the last analysis, it is the remembrance of God that determines the quality of human virtue. It is to the spiritual realisation intended and cultivated by this remembrance that the following chapter is addressed.

1. Alasdair Macintyre, After Virtue: A Study in Moral Theory (London, 1981), p. 204.

For a concise overview of the tradition as a whole, see Azim Nanji, 'The Ethical Tradition in Islam,' in A. Nanji, ed., The Muslim Almanac (New York, 1996).

## Realization through Remembrance: Ali (ع) and the Mystical Tradition of Islam

Realization through Remembrance: Ali (ع) and the Mystical Tradition of Islam

This chapter addresses the meaning of dhikru'Llah — 'the remembrance of God' — one of the most fundamental principles in Islamic spirituality. It does so by focusing on the Imam's sayings, viewed essentially as commentaries on the Qur'anic notion of dhikru'Llah, and by engaging also with relevant doctrinal formulations that have emerged from within the mystical traditions of Islam. At the outset of this book we stated that to speak of Ali (ع) is to speak of the quintessential spirituality of the Islamic faith. His influence is felt wherever spirituality has flourished in Islam, whether in the more clearly defined spiritual disciplines of Sufism and Shi'i 'Irfan, or in the realms of futuwwa (spiritual chivalry), the craft guilds, music, poetry and the arts. His presence, as the 'gate' to the city of prophetic wisdom, leaves its mark on all the noblest manifestations of the spirit of Islam.

As regards the Sufi tradition in particular, one discerns a uniquely organic connection with the Imam. For whether they be Shi'i or Sunni, Arab or Persian, Turk or Malay, Mauritanian or Bosnian, the Sufi orders are united in the figure of Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), who stands at the summit of all the chains of affiliation (salasila, sing. silsila) by which the Sufi masters trace their spiritual lineage back to the Prophet. Ali (ع)'s unifying role is equally apparent with respect to the most significant internal division within Islam — that between Sunnism and Shi'ism. Beyond the vast common ground the two traditions share in essential beliefs and practices, these two branches of Islam are brought together in a particularly direct and existential way by their shared orientation towards sanctity (walaya) and gnosis (ma'rifa) as the goal of the religious life. In this connection, Seyyed Hossein Nasr writes: 'Islamic esotericism or gnosis crystallized into the form of Sufism in the Sunni world, while it poured into the whole structure of Shi'ism, especially during its early period.' Thus the esoteric core of Islam expresses itself in Sunni Sufism and in Shi'ite 'Irfan with different 'accents', taking on different forms and expressions, without this spiritual attunement to the essence of the Islamic message of tawhid being fundamentally altered. Indeed, according to one of the great figures in whom Shi'ism and Sufism converge, Sayyid Haydar Amuli (d. 787/1385), the true Shi'i cannot but be a 'Sufi' if he plumbs the mystical depths of the Imams' teachings; conversely, the Sufi is a Shi'i if he comes to recognise that the most important roots of Sufism lie in the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt, the first poles (aqtab, sing. qutb) of the spiritual tradition through whom the mystical blessings of the Prophet were transmitted in an initiatic context. The differences of outward form between the two traditions are, for Amuli, dissolved in the fire of walaya — sanctity — the true substance and goal of both Sufism and Shi'ism alike. And for both traditions, Ali (ع) is the wali Allah, the 'friend/saint of God', par excellence.

The following reference to Ali (ع) by Ali Hujwiri (d. 464/1071), author of the first manual of Sufism written in Persian, Kashf al-mahjub, can be taken as altogether representative of the way Ali (ع) is regarded within the Sufi tradition: 'Among them [i.e. the Sufis] is the son of Muhammad's uncle, he who drowned in the ocean of trying affliction, and was consumed in the fire of loving passion (ghariq-i bahr-i bala' wa hariq-i nar-i wala), the leader of the saints and the pure ones (muqtada-yi awliya' wa'l-asfiya'). In this Path he holds a place of tremendous honour and elevated degree. He had complete mastery over the precise expression of the principles of the supernal realities, such that Junayd said of him, "Ali is our master as regards principles and affliction (shaykhuna fi'l-usul wa'l-bala)." ... That is, Ali (ع) is the Imam of this Path in respect of both theoretical knowledge and practical application (andar 'ilm wa mu'amalat imam-i in tariqat), for the adepts of Sufism call the knowledge of this Path its "principles", while its practice is summed up in the endurance of affliction.'

The mention here of the 'endurance of affliction' recalls the Imam's reference to the sages as those who 'befriend that by which the ignorant are estranged', noted in Chapter 1 above. One possible reading of that elliptical phrase, as suggested earlier, is that among the things the sages befriend and the ignorant flee is prayer — and more specifically the act of pure concentration on the Absolute, excluding all other thoughts; for this is a kind of death, the ego and all its preoccupations being as it were 'sacrificed' before the divine Reality on which concentration is exclusively fixed. The remembrance of God, the focus of this chapter, can be understood both as the consummation of that concentration and as the path leading to the summit of spiritual realisation: self-effacement before the sole reality of God.

The corpus of Ali (ع)'s teachings elucidates in a remarkable manner the notion, the principle and the practice of the dhikru'Llah — a theme of central importance both in the Qur'an and in the Sunna of the Prophet. The Imam's sayings constitute a valuable commentary on those Qur'anic verses that speak of dhikr; and, given the centrality of the dhikr within Sufi practice and doctrine, these sayings also help explain why the Sufis regard the Imam as the first spiritual 'Pole' (qutb) of Sufism after the Prophet himself. The close relationship between remembrance and spiritual realisation will be explored further in the light of both the Imam's sayings and the expositions of representatives of the Sufi tradition, particularly those belonging to the school of Ibn al-'Arabi — known in the tradition as al-Shaykh al-akbar, 'the greatest Master', on account of his towering influence on subsequent expressions of Sufi thought. These expositions can be seen as doctrinal reverberations of the dense, often elliptical pronouncements of the Imam. Taken together, the Imam's sayings and the Sufis' elaborations on the meaning and implications of 'the remembrance of God' bring key aspects of Islamic spirituality into sharp focus.

To begin, one must consider what is meant by the term dhikr and why it is accorded such prominence in Islamic devotional practice. The term is extremely rich, and no single English word can do justice to its range of meanings. It encompasses: remembrance, recollection, invocation, repetition, incantation, recitation; and also memory, mention, telling, relating (as of a story), praise, glorification, celebration, fame, eminence, honour, exhortation and warning — as well as denoting the Qur'an itself, or any book of revelation or religion. The definition that brings us closest to the spiritual principle expressed by the term, however, is that given by al-Raghib al-Isfahani in his Qur'anic dictionary. He writes that dhikr is that by means of which 'man preserves that knowledge (ma'rifa) which he has attained, so it is akin to memory (hifz), except that memory is spoken of in respect of its function of acquisition (ihraz), whereas dhikr is spoken of in respect of its function of rendering present (istihdar). Dhikr is also used to refer to the presence of a thing in one's consciousness (lit. "heart") or in one's speech. Thus, it is said that there are two types of dhikr: dhikr of the heart and dhikr of the tongue, and each comprises two further kinds: a dhikr which follows forgetfulness, and a dhikr which does not follow forgetfulness but which, on the contrary, expresses a continuous remembering.'

As will become clear, much in this definition resonates with the Imam's perspective. What deserves emphasis for now is that when the thing remembered — or 'rendered present' to consciousness — is God, the importance of dhikru'Llah as a principle becomes self-evident. Dhikru'Llah should be understood, first and foremost, as a principle of awareness, an in-depth assimilation of the divine presence; but it must also be understood as an action, a practice or series of practices, and thus as a spiritual discipline. In other words, dhikru'Llah can be grasped both as the end and as a means to that end — both as spiritual awareness and as the methodic practice aimed at cultivating that awareness. It is within the Sufi tradition that this relationship between the practice of the dhikr and the principle of consciousness it intends is most fully elaborated. There the dhikr is presented both as a contemplative discipline centring on the methodic invocation or repetition of a Name of God, and as the cognitive consequence and mystical fruit of that methodic discipline.

The dhikr is thus the spiritual method par excellence of the Sufis — a discipline that contributes to the realisation or in-depth assimilation, within the heart, of that which is present to the mind, thereby transforming an abstract, notional grasp of certain ideas into a concrete perception of spiritual realities. Insofar as dhikru'Llah is conceived as the permanent awareness of the divine Reality, it can be presented as the very quintessence of religion as such, being that which 'once again binds' (re-ligare) the human being to God — and thus as the raison d'être of all religious rites and practices. If prayer constitutes the core of religious practice, the dhikru'Llah is, as the Qur'an states simply, akbar, that is, 'greater' or 'greatest': 'Truly, prayer keepeth [one] away from lewdness and iniquity, and the remembrance of God is greater' (29: 45).


Numerous sayings of the Prophet attest to the pre-eminence of the remembrance of God. For example, it is related that the Prophet asked his companions: 'Shall I not tell you about the best and purest of your works for your Lord, and the most exalted of them in your ranks, and the work that is better for you than giving silver and gold, and better for you than encountering your enemy, with you striking their necks and them striking your necks?' Those he addressed replied: 'What is that, O emissary of God?' He said, 'The perpetual invocation of God — exalted and glorious (dhikru'Llah 'azza wa jalla da'iman).'

Likewise, Ali (ع) affirms, 'Perpetuate the dhikr, for truly it illumines the heart, and it is the most excellent form of worship (huwa afdal al-'ibada).' The performance of dhikr receives much emphasis in the Shi'i tradition, which records numerous sayings of the early Imams on the virtues of invoking the Names of God and various sacred phrases. Indeed, Imam Ja'far al-Sadiq (d.148/765) described his followers specifically in terms of dhikr: 'Our Shi'a are those who, when alone (idha khalau), invoke and remember God much.' The khalwa — the spiritual retreat in which one seeks to be 'alone' with God — is described as follows by 'Allama Tabataba'ī, one of the most important masters of Shi'i gnosis in the twentieth century: 'As to the particular spiritual retreat (khalwat-i khass) it is distancing oneself from people ... it is especially required during certain types of verbal invocations (adhkar-i kalamiyya) ... and is considered essential by the masters of the path.'

As regards the Sufi tradition, the following statement by Shaykh Ahmad al-'Alawi (d.1934) on the dhikr may be taken as entirely representative:

Remembrance is the mightiest rule of the religion ... The law was not enjoined upon us, neither were the rites of worship ordained but for the sake of establishing the remembrance of God ... In a word, our performance of the rites of worship is considered strong or weak according to the degree of our remembrance while performing them.

Al-Ghazalī raises the question of why the dhikr should be the most meritorious practice when it is so simple to perform compared to all the other acts of worship, which demand so much effort. His answer is that a full understanding of this superiority can only be reached through the 'science of unveiling' ('ilm al-mukashafa). What can be said, however, from the standpoint of the practical religious sciences ('ilm al-mu'amala), is that the most efficacious dhikr is that which is performed continuously with the presence of the heart (hudur al-qalb). This is the aim of the dhikr, and it is this which 'takes precedence over all acts of worship; nay, it is through this that the other acts of worship are dignified; this is the ultimate fruit of the practical acts of worship.' One is therefore justified in asserting that the principle of dhikru'Llah relates to the highest levels of religious practice, contemplative discipline and spiritual realization — and thus to the very heart of Islamic spirituality. Despite all their variety across time and place, their divergent perspectives and emphases, the Sufis are unanimous on the centrality of dhikru'Llah to their way. And as seen above, despite all their differences of name, orientation, school of thought or mode of practice, the Sufi orders are united in the pivotal figure of Ali (ع) as the one who connects all the later masters of the spiritual path to the Prophet himself. This convergence of the Imam and the dhikru'Llah in the Sufi tradition is far from coincidental. The Imam's elucidation of the meaning of the dhikr, together with his actual initiation of disciples into its methodic practice, constitutes a key aspect of his role as spiritual guide — the wali Allah, or the Pole (qutb) of his age — and is therefore central to his esoteric function within the spiritual tradition of Islam, in both its Sunni and Shi'i manifestations.

As regards the esoteric function of the Imam, it must be acknowledged that we have very little historically firm information about his initiatic role in this domain — that is, his actual initiation of disciples into the practice of invocation (known in the tradition as talqin) — just as we have precious little about his own initiation into the esoteric practice by the Prophet. This is hardly surprising, given that the practice is, precisely, esoteric; it is not enjoined upon all as an obligatory rite. Rather, within both Sufism and Shi'i 'Irfan, the invocation is bestowed upon those with a particular vocation — those who have received a 'calling' to practise something beyond the obligatory rites that are binding upon all. Nonetheless, there are historical accounts that provide prima facie evidence of the Imam's role in transmitting the dhikr. The individuals described in the earliest sources as being devoted to the dhikr as a particular practice were known as close associates, if not disciples, of the Imam. His role in this initiatic domain is stressed in the orally transmitted sources of the Sufi way, and it is also expressed in several written sources. These refer to reports of the transmission of the dhikr from the Prophet to the Imam and from him to several other disciples who went on to initiate others into what later came to be called the way of taşawwuf.

Despite the scarcity of historically sound material on the period of the earliest usage and transmission of dhikru'Llah, we do possess the extremely important literary data provided by the sayings recorded in the Nahj al-balagha and the Ghurar al-hikam, as well as in the supplications attributed to the Imam. Taken together, these sayings constitute a homogeneous corpus — a distinct body of doctrine — that furnishes the researcher with a rich source of philosophical reflection, spiritual meditation and metaphysical insight into the nature and meaning of this practice, which occupies so central a place in the mystical tradition of Islam.

The sayings we shall be examining shed light not only on the meaning of the practice of dhikr but also on the philosophy and epistemology underlying it; and they offer precious allusions to the fruits of its practice — that is, its consummation in spiritual realization. Particular attention will be given to the way in which the Imam's sayings, on one hand, constitute an important source of commentary on the Qur'anic verses relating to this theme; and, on the other, present a clear foreshadowing of the more elaborate doctrines pertaining to the dhikr found in the later Sufi and 'Irfanī traditions. The Imam's sayings in this domain thus serve as a bridge connecting what came before him — prophetic revelation — with what came after him, in the form of mystical elaboration. The texts and manuals of the mystical tradition seem to make explicit what was more often than not implicit in the Imam's sayings. In other words, the mystics elaborated on esoteric truths that the Imam had conveyed in sayings that were concise, often paradoxical and elliptical, but always intellectually challenging and spiritually fruitful. Reflection on these sayings — read in the context of the Qur'an and the Sunna they illuminate, and in light of the later Sufi doctrine they anticipate — can bring one closer to a proper understanding of the role of dhikru'Llah in the practical, operative and visionary dimensions of Islamic spirituality.

### Dhikru'Llah As A Polish For Hearts

The most fitting place to begin this discussion is the Imam's comment on the dhikr in sermon 213 of the Nahj al-balagha. The Imam first quotes words from verse 37 of Sura 24 of the Qur'an, entitled al-Nur ('The Light'), in which the invocation of God's Name is mentioned, and then proceeds to comment on them. The words in question are: '... men whom neither trade nor merchandise diverts from the remembrance of God'. Given that this fragment of the verse is organically connected with the preceding 'verse of light' — the ayat al-nur, so rich in mystical symbolism — the Imam's comment takes on considerably deeper resonance. The Imam clearly knew that his recitation of the words from verse 37 would call to mind in his listeners the verses that preceded them; the fact that he did not quote the verse of light in full only heightens the rhetorical effect of the ellipsis, such hints, implications, evocations and allusions being characteristic of Arabic balagha. To understand the context of the Imam's words, therefore, it is worth citing the verse of light in full:

God is the light of the heavens and the earth. A similitude of His light is a niche wherein is a lamp; the lamp is enclosed in a glass; the glass is as it were a shining star. [The lamp] is lit [by the oil of] a blessed olive tree, neither of the East nor of the West. The oil well-nigh shineth forth, though fire touch it not. Light upon light! God guideth to His light whom He will; and God striketh similitudes for mankind; and God knoweth all things (24: 35).

After this verse come the following two:

In houses which God has allowed to be elevated, and that His Name be remembered therein, He is glorified in the mornings and in the evenings by men whom neither trade nor merchandise diverteth from the remembrance of God, and from establishing the prayer, and from the bestowing of alms ... (24: 36-7).

After reciting the words 'men whom neither trade nor merchandise diverteth from the remembrance of God', the Imam begins his sermon:

Truly, God has made the remembrance (al-dhikr) a polish for the hearts, by which they hear after being deaf, and see after being blind and yield after being resistant ... There have always been slaves of God ... with whom He held intimate discourse through their thoughts and spoke with them through the essence of their intellects. They diffused illumination through the awakened light in their hearing and their seeing and their hearts, calling unto the remembrance of the days of God ... Indeed, there is a special group (ahl) who belong to the dhikr; they have adopted it in place of the world, such that 'neither trade nor merchandise' distracts them from it. They spend the days of their life in it ... It is as though they had left this world for the Hereafter, and they are there, witnessing what is beyond this world ...

In this passage it is important to note the Imam's description of the dhikr as a 'polish for hearts' (jila 'an li'l-qulub), alongside the reference to the 'awakened light' in the hearing, sight and hearts of those who have applied this polish to their hearts. The connection between the illumination of the heart and the verse of light is clear. The latent light of the heart is disclosed by the remembrance of God; the light within shines together with the light from on high: 'light upon light'. Once the heart is illumined, all the other faculties share in the luminosity generated by the invocation — and so there is, again, further 'light upon light'. The very word given for 'polish', jila', can itself be read as an implicit commentary on the light verse, for it derives from the verb jala, whose primary meaning is 'to become clear', exposed to view, disclosed, brought to light. The Qur'an refers to 'the night when it enshroudeth in darkness, and the day when it shineth forth in brightness (idha tajalla)' (92: 1–2). Likewise: 'By the day, as it bringeth to light [the sun] (idha jallahā)' (91: 3). The consciousness lying hidden within the heart is 'brought to light' through the effect of the polishing wrought by the remembrance of God.

Here the concern is with clarifying the organ of consciousness — the spiritual 'heart' — both enabling it to function as a mirror that reflects truths from above, and revealing its own hidden depths, thereby disclosing truths from within: those concealed contents of consciousness the Imam calls the 'buried treasures of the intellect' (dafa'in al-uqul). The dhikr is presented as a means of purifying this spiritual mode of perception — the 'heart' — which cannot be simply identified with the rational faculty, and still less with the organs of sensory perception, as discussed in Chapter 1.

The Qur'an points to the deeper significance of the heart and its distinctive mode of 'vision': 'Have they not travelled in the land, that they might have hearts with which to comprehend, and ears with which to hear? Indeed, it is not the eyes that are blind, but blind are the hearts that are in the breasts' (22: 46). The outward sight of the eye reaches only the surface of things, whereas the vision proper to the heart is an insight that penetrates beneath that surface to the reality concealed there. And again: 'They have hearts with which they understand not, eyes with which they see not, ears with which they hear not. They are like cattle — nay, even further astray. They are the heedless (al-ghafilun)' (7: 179). The reference at the end of verse 179 to the 'heedless', the ghafilun, brings us back to the dhikr, for the ghafil is the very opposite of the dhakir, the 'rememberer'; and the verse that immediately follows constitutes one of the most important 'methodic' foundations of the mystical practice of dhikr: 'Unto God belong the most beautiful Names, so call Him by them. And leave behind those who desecrate His Names; they will be requited for what they do' (7: 180). The relationship between remembrance and the heart is affirmed plainly in these verses: '...
