Those who believe and whose hearts are at peace in the remembrance of God — is it not in the remembrance of God that hearts are at peace?' (13: 28); 'Truly, therein is a reminder (dhikra) for him who hath a heart' (50: 37); 'Those are true believers whose hearts quake with awe when God is invoked' (8: 2); 'Is he whose breast God hath opened up to submission (islam), such that he followeth a light from his Lord [like he who remains closed to submission and in darkness]? So woe be to those whose hearts are hardened against the remembrance of God, they are in plain error' (39: 22).

By contrast, the verse that follows refers to hearts that 'soften' to dhikru'Llah: 'God hath revealed the most beautiful saying, a Book [containing] similitudes and paired [threats and promises] at which the skins of those who fear their Lord quiver; then their skins and their hearts soften to the remembrance of God' (39: 23).

The Imam's comment on verse 37 of the 'Light' chapter, cited above, should therefore be understood as shedding light on all those Qur'anic verses that refer to the 'understanding' or spiritual vision to which the heart has access in principle, and that allude to dhikru'Llah as the means of translating this potentiality into actuality. The heart needs to be 'polished' by the invocation of God. The Imam's comment echoes and reinforces a saying of the Prophet frequently quoted in Sufi circles: 'For everything there is a polish (siqala), and the polish of the hearts is the dhikru'Llah.'

What is clearly being referred to here is an actual discipline — a practice, an action, or a regular series of actions — without this in any way excluding the meaning of dhikr as awareness or as a state of recollectedness in the divine presence, a disposition of soul shaped by the consciousness that 'if you cannot see Him, He sees you'. The 'polishing' in question can be understood both as the work of purifying thought through reflection and meditation, and as a regular methodic practice, whether this takes the form of glorification (tasbih), magnification (tamjid), praise (tahmid), sanctification (taqdis), the declaration of divine unity (tahlil), or the dhikr in the strict sense — the invocation of God's Names — by means of which the 'rust' of forgetfulness is removed. Ali (ع) also refers in his comment to a particular group who 'belong' to the dhikr, calling them an ahl. It is worth noting that the Prophet likewise spoke of the ahl al-dhikr: 'God has angels who rove the pathways, seeking out the ahl al-dhikr, and when they find a group of people (qawm) invoking God, they call out to one another, "Come to that which you desire!"'

Moving forward in time to one of the greatest members of the qawm — one of the names by which the Sufis refer to themselves — Rumi offers a poetic expression of the image of the heart polished by the dhikr. It is the custom of kings, he tells us in the Mathnawi, to place the Sufis right before them: for they are a mirror for the soul, and better than a mirror — breasts polished by invocation and meditation, such that the mirror of the heart receives the pure, virginal image.


<!-- page 220 -->
Another aspect of the heart's polishing deserves reflection — this time centred on the nature of the 'vision' experienced by the heart that is purified by the dhikr and vivified by faith. In the Imam's perspective, this vision is divinely inspired; it is nothing less than the spiritual vision of God to which we have already referred: 'Eyes see Him not through sight's observation, but hearts see Him through the verities of faith.'

In another saying, the Imam refers to 'pure hearts' as the 'places' from which God can be seen, 'so whoever purifies his heart sees God'. The connection between the purification of the heart and the remembrance of God is confirmed in this saying: 'Where are they whose actions are accomplished purely for God, and who purify their hearts [so that they become] places for the remembrance of God?'

Taken together, these sayings of the Imam on the impact of the dhikr upon the heart can be read as a commentary on the important hadith qudsi cited in Chapter 2: 'My slave never ceases to draw near to Me through supererogatory acts (nawafil) until I love him. And when I love him, I am his hearing by which he hears, his sight by which he sees, his hand by which he grasps, and his foot by which he walks.' The Sufis regard this 'divine utterance' as defining a foundational principle of their metaphysical doctrine while simultaneously pointing to the goal of their mystical practice.

One observes here a mutual confirmation between the Imam's saying and the hadith qudsi. Through the dhikr — the supererogatory act par excellence — the individual's perceptions and actions are mysteriously assimilated to the divine Reality, and the result is that the heart attains spiritual vision. But then the heart, as the centre of consciousness, can no longer be regarded in its exclusively relative, human dimension. Its inmost essence, or secret (sirr), pertains in truth to the divine, and it is this alone that can see as God sees and, ultimately, 'see' God Himself. As the Sufi master Abu Talib al-Makki wrote, 'None sees Him to whom nothing is similar but Him to whom nothing is similar.' If it is true that God enters into the human acts of perception proper to the outward senses, then a fortiori the supreme act of spiritual awareness — the vision of God — is in reality not a vision of God by man but a vision of God by God, effected through rather than by the individual's 'heart'. The Imam tells us, 'Know God through God (i'rafu'Llah bi'Llah).' In the final analysis, then, it is God who both 'sees' and is 'seen'. According to a typical Sufi reading of the Qur'anic verse 'by a witnesser and that which is witnessed' (85:3), it is God Himself who is both the 'witnesser' (shahid) and the 'witnessed' (mashhud). Al-Kashani, for example, writes in his esoteric exegesis (until quite recently wrongly attributed to Ibn al-'Arabi) that the human witness is effaced in his witnessing, so that it is God who witnesses Himself through the individual whose separative consciousness is extinguished, such that 'He is the identity of the witness, there being no difference between the two except in terms of notional expression.'

<!-- page 221 -->
This discussion anticipates our final section, where the ultimate fruits of invocation will be explored in terms of spiritual realization through self-effacement. For now, the focus remains on a more relative aspect of the heart's vision of God — that whereby the reality of God is rendered clear and present to the heart by means of the 'verities of faith'. The agent here is the inner dimension of human consciousness, and the object 'perceived' is the divine Reality. To put it another way, profound faith reveals the objective presence of God — a presence that is ubiquitous and inalienable, constituting the ontological reality of all existing things. As the Imam's famous Du'a' Kumayl expresses it, noted earlier: 'By Thy tremendousness ('azmatika) which has filled all things ... by Thy Names which have filled the foundations of all things, by Thy knowledge which encompasses all things, by the light of Thy face, through which all things are illumined ... ' (emphasis added).

Nothing can exist without the 'foundation' — the ontological infrastructure, as it were — that the Name, or combination of Names, of God furnishes for it. What constitutes the true being and the positive qualities of each manifested thing is therefore, ultimately, a divine Name or combination of Names. To see 'things' correctly means seeing the divine attributes manifested by and through them, and thus perceiving them as existential reflections of principial realities — as so many tajalliyyat, or theophanies, self-disclosures of the divine Reality. The 'heart' purified by invocation comes to see God everywhere.

This vision of the divine in all things derives from two principles affirmed in the Qur'an: the first concerns the divine 'object' — that is, the objective, metaphysical nature of reality — and the second concerns the human subject and the mystical assimilation of that reality. On the one hand, nothing that exists, even the most outward aspects of material existence, can be excluded from the divine Reality, for 'He is the First and the Last, and the Outward and the Inward' (57:3), and 'Wherever ye turn there is the Face of God' (2:115); and on the other hand, 'We shall show them Our signs on the horizons and in their own souls, until it be clear to them that He is the Real' (41:53). Everything around us thus provides a sign pointing back to its Creator.

The heart polished and purified by the dhikr can decipher these signs and thereby 'see' God everywhere and in everything; yet the possibility of such a vision in no way implies a reduction of the divine Reality to the level of sensible phenomena. As noted earlier, the first sermon of the Nahj al-balagha proclaims that 'God is with every thing, but not through association; and other than every thing, but not through separation.' This 'withness' (ma'iyya) of God does not imply any relativization of God, any association or conjoining (muqarana) of the divine Reality with created things, or any diminution of divine transcendence. The interplay of ideas here can be taken as a commentary both on the Qur'anic verses that proclaim divine immanence — the presence of God to and within all things — and on those that stress divine transcendence, surpassing and incomparable to all things. God may be 'absolutely' close to us, yet we are infinitely far from Him. On the one hand, 'We are closer to man than the neck-artery' (50:16) — He is as near to us as our own jugular vein — and on the other, '... there is none comparable unto Him' (112:4).

<!-- page 222 -->
Nothing that exists can be altogether separate from the all-encompassing reality of God, and yet that reality has no common measure with anything that exists. His oneness both includes and excludes all things; hence the affirmation of God's immanence within the world — His being 'with every thing' — does not imply any diminution of His transcendence, and conversely, the affirmation of God's transcendence above the world — His being 'other than every thing' — does not imply His absence from the world. The oneness of God implies all of this, just as it negates any merely numerical oneness, as was briefly discussed in the first chapter: in the Imam's perspective, God is One not in the sense of a countable 'one' — a unit or entity among other units — but in the sense that His oneness admits no 'other'. In his words, 'That which has no second does not enter into the category of numbers.'

This can be taken as an esoteric commentary on the Qur'anic verse 'Say: He, God is One' (112:1). The opposite of this divine oneness — the multiplicity of gods implied in the error of shirk — is refined by this conception and transformed into a statement about being, not merely theology. That is to say, the act of shirk, of associating 'partners' with God, is carried to a higher register and shown to imply not simply the affirmation of false deities but the attribution to God of a merely numerical oneness — for this is to reduce His oneness to the wholly relative category of number, a oneness confined to the level of determinate entities. Each such entity is a unit, and from this standpoint God would be no more than one among other similar 'beings'. But the Imam insists that God 'has no second' — there is no other 'being' in being but Him. To call Him 'one' in the ordinary sense is itself imprecise, for it implies there could be a second in sequence; He is better described as unique. No entity or unit that apparently exists can, in reality, be compared with His being, which is one in an infinitely all-encompassing sense and unique in an absolutely exclusive sense: a oneness that is inclusive of all reality and exclusive of all alterity. This radical notion of a oneness that absolutely excludes the possibility of any 'otherness' in Being implies that created entities are so many false gods, to the precise extent that their apparent 'oneness' in existence is compared with God's true oneness — or rather, with God's 'one-and-onliness'.

In another sermon, Ali (ع) addresses the aspect of divine immanence, focusing this time on the kind of awareness the human intellect can have of the divine Reality — which brings us back to the question of what vision of God is possible for the human being:

Praise be to God who is present in the hidden inwardness of things, and whose being is indicated by the signs of manifest things. The eye of the onlooker cannot behold Him, so the eye of one who does not see Him cannot deny His reality, nor can the heart of one who affirms His reality see Him. He is utmost in elevation, so no thing is more elevated than He; but He is also close in His nearness, so no thing is closer than He ... God has not made the intellects capable of defining His qualities, but He has also not veiled the intellects from essential knowledge of Him.

<!-- page 223 -->
This passage tells us, on the one hand, that human intellects cannot define and a fortiori cannot know the divine attributes; and on the other, that 'essential knowledge' of Him is attainable. How is this apparent contradiction to be resolved? Wrestling with the paradox brings us closer to another key dimension of dhikr — that of recollection, remembrance, recognition. In other words, one is reconstituting ('re-collecting') fragments of a knowledge that already exists but has been forgotten. One recognizes what one has always known, and it is precisely for this reason that one is able to know it 'once again'. This does not mean that the knowledge in question is composed of parts to be reassembled — that would be empirical knowledge; rather, the remembrance here is more like an awakening, an illumination, that dawns as the clouds of congenital forgetfulness are dispelled.

This approach to knowledge through remembrance recurs throughout the Imam's sayings. In Sermon 1 of the Nahj, for instance, we find the phrase: 'Foremost in religion is knowledge of Him (awwal al-din ma'rifatuhu).' The word awwal literally means 'the first' and can be understood as the starting-point, the foundation, and hence the foremost element of religion. As for the word ma'rifa, it means not merely knowledge but also carries the sense of 'recognition'; the implication is clearly that the basis of all religious orientation is man's pre-existing knowledge of the divine Reality. One 'recognizes' — that is, 're-cognizes' — what one has always known at the deepest level of consciousness; one 'ac-knowledges' what one already 'knows' but which had apparently been forgotten.

Further on in the same sermon, as noted earlier, we are told that God sent to mankind 'His messengers, dispatching prophets in succession, in order to claim from them [His creatures] the fulfilment of the covenant of His creation; to remind them of His forgotten graces; to remonstrate with them through communication [of His revelation]; to unearth for them the buried treasures of the intellects ... ' (dafa'in al-'uqul) (emphases added).

The 'buried treasures' can be identified with the knowledge of divine realities ingrained in man's deepest nature — those realities to which humanity was made to bear witness at the dawn of creation: 'And when thy Lord brought forth from the children of Adam, from their loins, their seed, and made them testify of themselves, "Am I not your Lord?" They said, "Yea, we testify." [This was] lest ye say on the Day of Resurrection, "Truly, of this we were unaware"' (7:172).

<!-- page 224 -->
These divine realities can also be understood in reference to the divine spirit breathed into man at the creation of the human soul: 'Then He fashioned [man] and breathed into him of His Spirit' (32:9); to the 'names' taught by God to Adam: 'And He taught Adam the names, all of them ... ' (2:31); and to the fitra, the primordial nature of man, patterned upon the divine nature: ' ... the nature of God (fitrat Allah), that according to which He created man' (30:30). The primordial nature of the human soul can thus be seen to comprise the very element — knowledge of God — to which all of humanity is made to bear witness in the pact (mithaq) mentioned in the Qur'anic verse above. This identification between the fitra and the knowledge of God is made explicit by Imam Muhammad al-Baqir, in a saying quoted by al-Khu'i as a commentary on that sentence of the first sermon. Al-Baqir was asked about the meaning of the words in the Qur'an, 'the nature of God, that according to which He created man'. He answered, 'He created them [such that their primordial nature was] in conformity with tawhid, at the covenant regarding knowledge of Him, [when they testified] that He is indeed their Lord.'

Knowledge of God, then, essentially defines human nature; it is for this reason that the human intellect cannot be veiled, as the Imam said, from 'essential knowledge of God.' Yet even though this knowledge is ingrained in human nature, forgetfulness (ghafla) has become man's second nature. In Qur'anic terms, the very passage of time smothers and thereby 'hardens' the heart: 'Hath not the moment come for the believers, that their hearts be humbled to the remembrance of God, and to what hath been revealed of the truth; and that they not become as those who were given the Book aforetime. Then the term was prolonged for them, so their hearts were hardened; and many of them are evil-doers' (57:16, emphasis added).

The verse speaks both of this hardening of the heart over time — which can be identified with the ghafla of second nature — and of its opposite, the heart's being present, here and now, to the dhikru'Llah. To remedy forgetfulness, more is needed than a simple factual reminder — some item of information, some element of formal, conceptual, discursive knowledge. No amount of empirical data, coming exclusively from without, can bring man to knowledge of God; rather, it is integral remembrance, spiritual awareness welling up from within and crystallizing upon contact with revelation from without, that renders the innate knowledge of God present once again to heart and mind — that knowledge which is 'buried' deep in the intellect. There is no common measure between this consciousness of God and ordinary empirical knowledge of phenomena. The incommensurability between the two kinds of knowledge is expressed by the Imam in sayings such as, 'Praise be to God who is well-known (ma'ruf) without being seen.' The paradox is sharpened by the following formulation of the same principle: 'By things their Creator manifests Himself to the intellects; and by things He is guarded from the sight of the eyes.' This brings us to a complex theme in the Imam's perspective — the nature of the 'veil' constituted by created phenomena, and how it is to be understood in the light of the infinite and inalienable reality of God. On the one hand, all created phenomena are a veil, in that they prevent the divine Essence from being seen by outward vision; but on the other hand, all phenomena cannot but manifest the divine, bearing witness to its creativity and revealing something of its reality — this being clear to the intellect in its normative state and dazzlingly evident to the heart illumined and purified by the remembrance of God.

<!-- page 225 -->
The following aphorisms of one of the great masters of Sufi gnosis or ma'rifa (⟪ar:معرفۃ⟫), Ibn 'Ata'illah, are altogether pertinent here. They read as a series of reflections on the paradox expressed so succinctly by the Imam. Ibn 'Ata'illah writes: 'That which shows you the existence of His omnipotence is that He veiled you from Himself by that which has no existence alongside of Him.' In one respect, God is 'guarded from the eyes', in the Imam's words, and thus veiled by 'things'; but insofar as God alone truly 'is', these 'things' do not really exist — so God is veiled by 'nothing'. Through their very 'appearance' — both in the sense of their coming into existence and in the sense of their 'apparent' existence — it is 'by things' that 'their Creator manifests Himself'. The mystery of this self-manifestation of God by and through all things is pressed upon the reader with full force by the following rhetorical questions of Ibn 'Ata'illah:


How can it be conceived that something veils Him, since He is the one who manifests everything?

How can it be conceived that something veils Him, since He is the one who is manifest through everything?

How can it be conceived that something veils Him, since He is the one who is manifest in everything?

How can it be conceived that something veils Him, since He is the Manifest to everything?

How can it be conceived that something veils Him, since He was the Manifest before the existence of everything?

How can it be conceived that something veils Him, since He is more manifest than anything?

How can it be conceived that something veils Him, since He is the One alongside of whom there is nothing?

How can it be conceived that something veils Him, since He is nearer to you than anything else?

How can it be conceived that something veils Him, since were it not for Him the existence of everything would not be manifest?

It is a marvel how Being has been manifested in non-being, and how the contingent has been established alongside of Him who possesses the attribute of Eternity!

<!-- page 289 -->

The import of all these sayings can be grasped within a paradox tersely expressed by the Imam: 'The veils do not veil Him (la tahjubuhu al-hujub).' The veils cannot help revealing what they are supposed to conceal; they are not merely transparent to the reality they cannot hide — in and of themselves, they reveal rather than conceal it. This calls to mind the Imam's famous saying, noted earlier, so often quoted by the Sufis: 'Were the veil to be removed, I would not increase in certitude' (⟪ar:لو کشف الغطاء ما ازدت یقینا⟫). The veils of creation ultimately reveal what they initially conceal, yet this revelation is perceived only by the eye of the heart purified through the dhikr — the heart that now 'sees after being blind'. The Imam continues, deepening the paradox still further:

The veil between Him and His creatures is His very creation of them. [This veil is] for the sake of His prevention of that which is possible in their essences, and for the sake of making possible that which is prevented in regard to Him; and to distinguish the Creator from the created, and the limit from the limited, and the Lord from the vassal.

All of creation — every 'veil' it constitutes — is thus nothing other than the divine dimension of al-Zahir: the Apparent, the Clear, the Evident, the Manifest. Yet creation is al-Zahir not on its own account, but by virtue of the divine reality of which it is a projection, a form of tajallī. God, as al-Zahir, projects something of His 'hidden treasure' within and as the creation. The world manifests the 'apparent' aspect of God — that which 'appears' from Him, that which He can manifest or bring into being — even while concealing the mystery of God: that which remains 'hidden', al-Batin, the supra-manifest source of being, the uncreated essence. The veil of creation is therefore nothing other than the self-revelation of God. It is important to stress, however, that it is so only by virtue of what pierces the veil of its own apparent otherness — that divine creativity which the creation cannot in fact conceal, even though in principle it has the character of a veil.

<!-- page 290 -->

The veil constituted by creation prevents what the essences of creatures make possible. What do the essences make possible? The realization of inmost identity with the divine Reality — tawhid at its highest level — a possibility that derives from the fact that this same divine Reality is the ontological foundation of 'their essences'. As noted above, it is the divine Names, qualities, and attributes that 'fill the foundations of all things'. At the foundations — or within the 'essences' — there is but the one and only divine Reality. If the realization of this truth of identity is thus 'possible', what then does the veil 'prevent'? It prevents the affirmation of this identity at the wrong level of being — that of empirical existence — rather than at the deepest level, where the 'essences' of things are nothing other than the divine qualities and thus the divine Reality itself. To equate created phenomena with God is tantamount to pure shirk, the cardinal sin of 'association', and it is against this that a vision of divine oneness must guard. One must always 'distinguish the Creator from the created, and the limit from the limited, and the Lord from the vassal'.

The veil of creation, in other words, acts as a barrier against pantheism — the reduction of the Creator's infinite transcendence to the finite forms of creatures. Yet insofar as the veil has no existence apart from the self-manifestation of the Real, it paradoxically also makes possible 'that which is prevented in regard to Him': rendered transparent to the divine Reality of which it is one expression, the veil cannot prevent the immanent reality of God from shining through and within all things. On one side stands essential identity, without any diminution of divine transcendence; on the other stands existential distinction, without any attenuation of divine immanence. We return to the foundational sentence of the first sermon of the Nahj: 'He is with every thing, but not through association; and other than every thing, but not through separation.' We are also reminded of a central theme in Sufi metaphysics: the dynamic relationship between tanzih — the declaration of divine incomparability and transcendence — and tashbih — the affirmation of similarity and, at the deepest level, of immanence. In the words of Ibn al-'Arabi:

He is not declared incomparable in any manner that will remove Him from similarity, nor is He declared similar in any manner that would remove Him from incomparability. So do not declare Him nondelimited and thus delimited by being distinguished from delimitation! For if He is distinguished then He is delimited by His nondelimitation. And if He is delimited by His nondelimitation, then He is not He.

<!-- page 291 -->

To return to the Imam's powerfully evocative statement: 'The veils do not veil Him.' Both tanzih and tashbih can become veils unless each is seen in the light of the other; both transcendence and immanence can become veils unless the two perspectives are held together in spiritual vision; both the creation — as divine manifestation — and the uncreated Essence — above and beyond all manifestation — can become veils unless they are integrated in the spiritual vision proper to tawhid, and thus rendered transparent, each 'revealing' the other. Equally, a one-sided emphasis on God as 'the Apparent' becomes a veil by blinding us to God as 'the Hidden', and conversely: for even the hidden aspect of God, considered in isolation, will — despite its non-manifest nature — constitute a conceptual veil that prevents us from seeing God's 'evidence', His 'evident-ness', in the very heart of the created world. In other words, 'veils' are not only created phenomena but also the limitations inherent in every human conception of God, including the divine qualities and attributes — hence the need, as we shall see shortly, for a radical negation, on the plane of human conception, of those divine attributes. Only on the basis of such an apophasis can consciousness begin to fathom the true nature of divine transcendence and see through the veils that 'do not veil Him'.

It is clear that these perspectives on the nature of the 'veil' are better understood as the evocation of a spiritual vision than as the logical expression of empirical observation; they are the fruits of contemplative synthesis, not the conclusions of rational analysis. It is a vision that resolves apparent contradiction through intuitive awareness of the unitive simplicity of reality — not the product of some tortuous mental effort to reconcile the complexities of existence. It is at once a vision reflected by the heart burnished by the remembrance of God and also itself a form of that very remembrance. What the rational faculty will always perceive as irreducible contradiction, the spirit of the intellect can apprehend as a fruitful paradox. This aspect of the Imam's teaching finds vivid expression in the words of the famous Sufi Abu Sa'id al-Kharraz, who, when asked how he came to know God, replied: 'through the fact that He brings opposites together (jam'uhu al-diddayn).' He then recited the Qur'anic verse, 'He is the First and the Last, and the Outward and the Inward'.

<!-- page 292 -->

It is spiritual intuition that imparts the capacity to see as one — in the unifying vision of tawhid — that which appears on the surface as many. In sharpening the paradox on the conceptual plane, Ali (ع) is, to use modern parlance, 'deconstructing' the artifices of mental conception, and in doing so paves the way for receptivity to intuitive apprehension and spiritual contemplation. The very work of the intellect — عقل, a word whose root means to bind or restrain — is one of binding together: the realities the heart discloses through intuition and inspiration are drawn by it into the organic unity of tawhid through an exercise in logic, a movement of negation and affirmation, of constriction and expansion. It is only at these deeper levels of consciousness that the complementary process of 'reconstruction' can take place — a 're-cognition' of the Real, al-Haqq, arising from the heart's vision of God through 'the verities of faith' (haqa'iq al-iman). Only these haqa'iq are truly commensurate with al-Haqq; only these realities hidden within the depths of faith, and brought to light in a heart polished by the remembrance of God, can do justice to the reality of God.

### Remembrance As The Quintessence Of Worship

To focus on the actual practice of the dhikru'Llah, we should turn first to certain Qur'anic verses that allude to the dhikr as a devotional practice:

And invoke the Name of thy Lord morning and evening (76: 25).

And invoke the Name of thy Lord, devoting thyself to Him [or: it] with utter devotion (73: 8).

O ye who believe! Invoke God with much invocation (33: 41).

Call upon thy Lord in humility and in secret (7: 55).

And invoke thy Lord within thyself, in humility and awe, and beneath thy breath, in the morning and in the night (7: 205).

All of these verses point to an actual practice, one to be maintained according to different modes ('within thyself', 'in humility', 'in awe', 'in secret', 'beneath thy breath', and so on). But perhaps most importantly, the invocation is to be performed abundantly — dhikran kathiran. As noted earlier, the intensity and duration of the devotions of the Prophet and the first Muslims is referred to in the Qur'anic chapter al-Muzzammil ('The Enwrapped'), which mentions the long night vigils these early Muslims maintained: 'Truly, thy Lord knoweth that thou standest in prayer for almost two-thirds of the night, and a half of it, and a third of it — as doth a group from those with thee' (73: 20). It is worth noting that the opening of this Sura contains the verse quoted above, instructing the Prophet to 'remember/invoke the Name of thy Lord, and devote thyself to Him with utter devotion' (73: 8). The pronoun in the word ilayhi, 'to Him', can refer either to Him as 'thy Lord' or to 'it' as the 'Name of thy Lord', so that the operative meaning of the verse becomes: 'Invoke the Name of God and devote yourself to this Name with utter devotion.' This recalls the explicit injunction in the verse, 'Glorify the Name of thy Lord, the Exalted' (87: 1). On the plane of devotional practice, then, devotion to the Name of the Absolute is tantamount to devotion to the Absolute itself. The Absolute in itself is above and beyond all forms — including all verbal forms — yet It is nonetheless made accessible through the divine Name: a Name not defined by any human linguistic invention but bestowed through divine self-disclosure, God revealing the sacrament of His own Name. Distraction from the remembrance or invocation of the Name is, equally, distraction from God Himself, as the following verses make clear: 'The devil hath overcome them, causing them to forget the remembrance of God' (58: 19), and 'O ye who believe, let not your wealth nor your children distract you from the remembrance of God' (63: 9).

<!-- page 293 -->

This identification between devoting oneself to God and devoting oneself to His Name flows naturally from a principle enunciated by Ali (ع) in the following simple affirmation: 'He who loves a thing dedicates himself fervently to its invocation (man ahabba shay'an lahija bi-dhikrihi).' The lover of God should therefore devote himself utterly to the invocation of the Name of God — giving himself to it in abundance, kathiran. The pairing of lahija — meaning both 'to be devoted to' and 'to mention repeatedly' — with dhikr, which clearly indicates the constant verbal repetition of the invocation, also appears in the Imam's Du'a' Kumayl, there with an added explicit reference to the tongue: '... my tongue has devoted itself to thy invocation (lahija bihu lisanī min dhikrika).' He implores God, 'Make my times in the night and the day inhabited by Thy remembrance', and 'make my tongue invoke Thee with utter dedication (ij'al lisanī bi-dhikrika lahijan)'. Finally, the invocation as a practice is clearly confirmed in his words: 'O He whose Name is a remedy and whose invocation is the cure (ya man ismuhu dawa' wa dhikruhu shafa').'

Here the Name of God is far more than a name among other names, a word or sound among other symbols: it is the remedy for man's congenital forgetfulness, the spiritual amnesia — ghafla — that has become his 'second nature', as noted above. But to be effective, the Name must be actively invoked rather than merely contemplated, just as a medicine must be taken and not merely thought about if it is to exercise its healing power. As the Imam puts it in one of his sermons, describing the greatness of the Qur'an: 'It has made the dhikr incumbent upon your tongues.' And, as we have seen, the invocation is to be performed kathiran — much, in abundance. The following hadith of the Prophet should be noted in this context, for it makes absolutely clear that dhikr has an operative, practical dimension and cannot be reduced to a purely theoretical interpretation of its meanings:

'Abd Allah b. Busr reported that a man said, 'O Messenger of Allah, the laws of Islam are too many for me, so tell me something that I may cling to.' He replied, may God bless him and give him peace, 'Let your tongue never cease to be moist from invoking Allah.'

It is also worth quoting the verses that follow the Qur'anic exhortation to invoke much: 'O ye who believe, invoke God with much invocation, and glorify Him early and late. He it is who blesseth you, as do His angels, so that He may bring you forth from darkness into light; and He is ever merciful to the believers' (33: 41-43).

<!-- page 294 -->

The reciprocity here between the dhikr of the believers and the salat — the prayer or blessing of God — upon the believers deserves attention. The relationship between the human act of invocation and the divine act of blessing is more complex than it first appears. Exploring this relationship and delving into the ontological dimensions of the invocation brings us close to the mystery expressed by the verse, 'Remember Me, I will remember you' (2: 152).

We shall return to the possible meanings of this reciprocity below. For now, remaining with the verses cited above (33: 41-43), the relationship to note is that between invocation and illumination — being brought from darkness into light. The Imam speaks of the way in which human invocation results in divine illumination, and his words may be read as a commentary on this dimension of those verses: 'Whoever invokes God, glorified be He, God enlivens his heart and illuminates his inner substance (lubb).' The word lubb (pl. albab) here evokes the Qur'anic verse describing the ulu'l-albab, the 'possessors of substance', in which the dhikr once again figures prominently: 'Truly in the creation of the heavens and the earth, and in the alternation between night and day, there are signs for those of substance, those who invoke God standing, sitting and reclining on their sides, and reflect upon the creation of the heavens and the earth ...' (3: 190-191).

Beyond reinforcing the practical and methodic dimension of remembrance, this passage also clearly indicates the relationship between dhikr and fikr — between invocation and reflection or meditation. Returning to the observations made earlier: the practice of invocation is understood to polish the inmost organ of awareness, thereby deepening the capacity for meditation upon the 'signs of God' in creation and within oneself. When the element of love is placed between invocation and meditation — for 'he who loves a thing dedicates himself fervently to its invocation' — one can begin to see how these three elements enter a virtuous circle in which each strengthens and deepens the other two. The more seriously one ponders the 'signs' of God and appreciates their beauty, the more one comes to love God; the more one loves God, the more one invokes His Name; the more one invokes, the more keenly one perceives the sacred message of those 'signs' in the universe. Thus, fikr intensifies dhikr and dhikr deepens fikr.


These verses (3:190–1) also matter because they highlight the supra-formal dimension of invocation. The dhikr is presented here as the quintessence of all religious activity—the spiritual act par excellence. Its modes are universal: standing, sitting, reclining, as this verse states, and "within thyself," "in humility," "in awe," "in secret," "beneath thy breath," as the verses cited above make clear. These modes transcend the formal rules governing the fixed canonical prayers, which prescribe specific words, movements, and conditions. The dhikr, by contrast, is to be performed at all times, in all places, and in all postures; it is something to be woven into the texture of everyday life rather than imposed upon life as an external, formalistic practice. It is worth noting here that, according to Imam Ja'far al-Sadiq, invocation is the one practice that should be carried out in all circumstances and at all times, with no limit placed upon it: "Everything has a limit, at which it terminates, except the dhikr Allah. God has made obligatory the religious duties (al-fara'id), so whoever fulfils them, that fulfilment constitutes their limit. For the month of Ramadan, whoever fasts therein, that constitutes its limit …" He goes on to mention other rites, then cites the Qur'anic verse: "O ye who believe, invoke God with much invocation," and adds that his father, Imam Muhammad al-Baqir, never ceased invoking—even while eating and walking.

<!-- page 229 -->

In this light it becomes easier to understand those whom the Qur'an describes as "men whom neither trade nor merchandise diverts from the remembrance of God." The verse also illuminates why believers are described as those who are "perpetually at prayer ('ala salatihim da'imun)" (70:23). Such people are not merely regular in performing the canonical prayer at its appointed times; for the men of substance, there is no moment when they are not "at prayer"—if one understands "prayer" as the remembrance of God. The Qur'an instructs mankind to "establish the prayer for the sake of My remembrance" (20:14). The very purpose and goal of prayer, its spiritual value and substance, is thus the remembrance of God.

A saying of Ali (ع) is directly relevant here: "Continuous invocation is the food of the spirit and the key to prayer (miftah al-salat)." This sheds further light on why the Qur'an tells us—as was noted at the outset of this essay—that "prayer keepeth [one] away from lewdness and iniquity, and the remembrance of God is greater" (29:45). The formal, canonical prayer is described here in negative or constraining terms: it is a preventative. Its formality is a ritual necessity, but it is also, unavoidably, an existential limitation—performed and operative at one time and not another, expressed in certain forms and not others. The remembrance of God is greater in that it is the positive, enlivening substance of prayer; it is what liberates consciousness from the verbal, mental, and existential limits by which all formal prayer is defined. Since formal prayer is inescapably bounded by such limits, its baraka—its blessedness—can all too easily become confined, subjectively, within those same limits. One feels close to God only during formal prayer, and not in everyday life outside the prayer-times. It is dhikr, in the sense of recollectedness and constant awareness of God, that allows prayer—or the consciousness shaped by prayer—to open outward into its intended essence: an awareness of the unconditional presence of God. Dhikr thus transforms a preventative measure against moral transgression into a positive apprehension of spiritual reality. One might say that the dhikru'Llah is "greater" than the ritual or canonical prayer in the measure that spiritual awareness transcends moral conscience, or in the measure that essence transcends form. The "lesser" is not negated by the "greater" but is rather comprised within it, enriched by it, and brought to a fullness and scope it could never attain on its own. In this light one can better understand at least some of the reasons why the Imam, following the Prophet and the Qur'an, presents dhikru'Llah as the quintessence of worship and devotion.

<!-- page 230 -->

In this final section, we turn to the spiritual fruits of the remembrance of God—and in particular to "realization," tahqiq: the process by which al-Haqq, the Real, is "made real" within oneself. This is closely related to the process the Imam earlier described as tawahhud—self-integration, the spiritual assimilation of oneness within oneself—as distinct from the simple theological affirmation of oneness, tawhid, in its conventional sense. In keeping with the Imam's characteristic use of paradox, the fruit of remembrance can be approached first by examining its very opposite: ghafla, heedlessness, or sahw, forgetfulness and distraction. Pursuing this line of inquiry, we encounter another striking example of how the Imam's sayings function implicitly as a commentary on Qur'anic verses, while opening onto a rich terrain of mystical speculation and spiritual contemplation concerning the ultimate consequences of the invocation and remembrance of God. The Qur'an is severe in its condemnation of those who pray ostentatiously and hypocritically—those who "give the lie to religion": "Woe to those who pray, those who are heedless of their prayer, those who make a show, and who withhold simple assistance" (107:4–7). The Imam likewise warns against remembering God heedlessly or distractedly, and in describing its opposite—perfect or complete dhikr—he articulates a paradoxical return to a kind of "forgetting" that goes to the very heart of the Sufi principle of "extinction in remembrance" (al-fana' fi'l-dhikr):

Do not remember God absent-mindedly (sahiyan), nor forget Him in distraction; rather, remember Him with perfect remembrance (dhikran kamilan), a remembrance in which your heart and tongue are in harmony, and what you conceal conforms with what you reveal. But you will not remember Him according to the true reality of the remembrance (haqiqat al-dhikr) until you forget your own soul in your remembrance.

This is an extraordinarily important saying, and it offers a summary of the entire mystical path. What is implied here is the whole sequence of spiritual "stations" (maqamat, sing. maqam) through which total sincerity is attained. Sufism is itself often described simply as "sincerity"—that is, sidq or ikhlas. To be totally sincere in this sense is to orient oneself and one's desires purely toward God, without any taint of impurity—where impurity means the presence, in one's devotion, action, or intention, of any desire for something other than God. On one hand, the dhikr itself helps to realize sincerity by "polishing" the heart; on the other, it is sincerity that confers "perfection" or "completion" upon the spiritual efficacy of the dhikr. What matters at this stage of "perfect" dhikr is that the outward verbal form of the invocation must harmonize with heartfelt intention. There must be no contradiction between what the tongue articulates and what motivates the heart. Bringing heart and tongue into harmony means that the divine Reality invoked by the tongue reverberates in the heart—the very source of the soul's fundamental dispositions, its virtues.

<!-- page 231 -->

The divine qualities contained within the Name of the Absolute must, to some degree at least, be reflected in the heart and soul of the invoker, in the form of virtues—virtues that are summed up in the sincerity or "purity" of soul to which ikhlas refers. When the soul is completely pure, sincerely and utterly at one with the highest ideals proclaimed by the tongue and the intellect, it becomes transparent to the spiritual qualities embedded within its own substance: the fitra, or primordial nature. These are the qualities "brought to light"—and brought to life—by the dhikr; they are the qualities that shine forth from the heart once it has been polished by the dhikr. In their absence, however, the purely technical act of invocation is "heedless" and devoid of all efficacy. One who makes no effort to attain sincerity—who does not engage with the "greater" jihad discussed in Chapter 1—is heedless of God, neglecting the moral concomitants of authentic remembrance and ignoring the moral preconditions for its efficacy.

Yet what is most remarkable in this saying of the Imam is his presentation of the ultimate stage of dhikr in terms of its apparent opposite. The "reality" of dhikr, its haqiqa, opens outward into the supreme haqiqa—divine Reality as such. This degree of dhikr requires the "forgetting" of the individual: the veil of the self must be cast aside if the spiritual vision of divine Reality is to be attained. There can be no ma'rifa without self-effacement at the outset, and "extinction" (fana') in the end. The reality of "remembering" calls for the most radical "forgetting."

In other words, there is a clear distinction—indeed a radical disjuncture—between "perfect" dhikr and the "reality" of dhikr, such that the movement from "perfection" to "reality"—from human perfection to divine Reality, one might say—requires an ontological leap of consciousness. At the first level, where the aim is the elimination of hypocrisy and the attainment of sincerity, integrity, and totality—in short, perfection on the plane of the human soul—the individual as agent of the dhikr retains his identity. The harmonious reverberation between hidden intent and outward act is at this stage called "perfect" dhikr. But it remains a dhikr that pertains to the individual as agent. At the higher level, by contrast, the dhikr can no longer be defined in relation to the individual, since the individual has been "forgotten"—by himself. This can only mean that the centre of consciousness has shifted from the invoker, al-dhakir, to the Invoked, al-Madhkur. The haqiqa of the dhikr is thus one with the object of invocation, the Real itself, al-Haqq. Esoterically, then, one may say that the true agent of the invocation is God Himself, the apparent invocation by man being no more than an outward appearance—an illusion extinguished by the very act of invocation itself.

<!-- page 232 -->

This brings the discussion back to the question of agency raised earlier. The hadith qudsi quoted previously—"My slave never ceases to draw near to Me through supererogatory acts (nawafil) until I love him …"—should be recalled here. The Imam's commentary on the Qur'anic verses concerning those who are not distracted by the world from the remembrance of God made clear that the nawafil in question are centred on the practice of invocation. What must be stressed at this point is that invocation can be understood as disclosing the reality of God's ontological agency within all acts: God is the hearing and the seeing of the individual who is loved by God as a result of his devotions. It must be emphasized, however, that this account of the Divine penetrating the faculties of the soul and body of the individual describes a universal reality—the true state of affairs—and not any change of state, either for God or for the individual; for God does not "change" in order to enter into the acts and perceptions of one person rather than another.

His "becoming" one with the acts of perception is to be understood as a reality "becoming clear" to the soul that has been unveiled, so that it sees through the illusory appearance of its own agency. By virtue of the invocation, the soul is enabled to see the deeper meaning of the Qur'anic verses, "God hath created you and what ye do" (37:96), and "thou didst not throw when thou threwest [the stones], but God threw" (8:17).

Returning to the kind of "vision" made possible by the dhikr: the very title of one of Ibn al-'Arabi's treatises, Kitab al-fana' fi'l-mushahada (The Book of Extinction in Contemplation), appears to point directly to the Imam's paradox of forgetting in the very heart of remembrance—for being extinguished in contemplation is akin to the state of being forgotten in invocation. At the opening of this extremely important treatise, Ibn al-'Arabi writes that the divine Reality is too exalted to be witnessed by the creaturely eye. The vision of God is impossible for as long as any "trace of the condition of the creature in the eye of the witness" subsists. It is only toward the treatise's conclusion that this apparently insuperable barrier before the vision of God is removed. Ibn al-'Arabi resolves the problem by means of the Prophet's definition of ihsan, discussed above: "It is to worship God as though you see Him, and if you see Him not, yet truly He sees you." The Arabic wording is such that, by placing a stop in the middle of the phrase "if you see Him not (in lam takun: tarahu)," the meaning is entirely transformed into: "if you are not, you see Him."

<!-- page 233 -->

This vision of God cannot, then, belong to the individual, who is "forgotten" in the Imam's words, or non-existent according to Ibn al-'Arabi. The vision of God can only be by God, not by man. We will return to this possibility of "ontological transference" and its metaphysical underpinnings in relation to the divine Names shortly. But it is useful here to reinforce the message of "extinction in contemplation" by referring to the discourse—as narrated by Ibn al-'Arabi—between him and Moses in the sixth heaven. Ibn al-'Arabi presents this within his account of his own visionary ascent, his spiritual mi'raj through the heavens to the divine presence within his own soul.

He addresses Moses: "You requested the vision (of God), while the Messenger of God (Muhammad) said that 'not one of you will see his Lord until he dies.'"

Moses replies: "And it was just like that: when I asked Him for the vision, He answered me, so that I 'fell down stunned' (7:143). Then I saw Him in my (state of) being stunned."

Ibn al-'Arabi asks: "While (you were) dead?"

Moses: "While (I was) dead … I did not see God until I had died."

Here we have a supra-personal vision entirely congruent with Ali (ع)'s unselfconscious remembrance. What is implicit in both forms of spiritual assimilation is what was termed above an "ontological transference" of agency. The true vision of God cannot take place for as long as anything creaturely subsists in the "eye" of the beholder. It is illuminating to see how the spiritual principle—and even some of the language—of this dialogue is rendered in condensed form in a supplication attributed to the Imam. He pleads with God to make him "one of those whom You have called and he has replied to You, upon whom You have glanced and who falls unconscious before Your majesty." The word sa'iqa ("to fall unconscious," "be stunned") used in this supplication is the same as that used in the Qur'anic verse (7:143) in relation to Moses' state when God revealed Himself to the mountain. A single "glance" from the Real is sufficient to precipitate this denouement of self-awareness—the shadow cast, upon the plane of individual consciousness, by the fulgurating light of the self-disclosure, the tajalli, of God.


Also, to speak of this divine 'glance' (lāhiza) takes us back to Ibn al-'Arabī's evocative phrase 'extinction in contemplation'. In his Tarjuman al-ashwaq, he refers to divine wisdom as a peacock that 'kills with her glances', and then adds in his own commentary that this refers to 'the station' of al-fana' fi'l-mushahada (extinction in contemplation). Earlier in the same commentary, he refers to the 'murderous glances' of this divine wisdom 'which accrues to a man in his hours of solitude, and which assaults him with such violence that he is unable to behold his personality, and which exercises dominion over him.' The commentary on the following lines is also instructive in underlining the relationship between dhikr and fana': 'Wild is she, none can make her his friend; she has gotten in her solitary chamber (bayt khalwatiha), a mausoleum for remembrance (dhikr).' The commentary adds: '... because contemplation of the Essence is a passing away (fana') ... Her solitude is her looking on herself, for God says, "Neither My earth nor My heaven contains Me, but I am contained by the heart of My believing servant," and since the heart which contains this essential wisdom of the race of Jesus is bare and empty of all attributes, it is like a desert and she is like a wild animal.' Realization through remembrance, then, strictly implies the extinction of individual consciousness — the perishing of egoistic self-awareness before the Absolute. This principle is far from being purely speculative or theoretical. Quite apart from the wealth of Sufi literature on the states of consciousness (ahwal, sing. hal) through which the mystic passes, we have the following evidence of the Imam's own immersion in such states. Abu'l-Darda', an eminent companion of the Prophet, reported that he once found Ali (ع) lying in one of the palm orchards of Medina, his body seemingly lifeless, as stiff as wood. He went to condole with Ali (ع)'s family, but was told by his wife Fatima, 'My cousin [that is, Ali (ع), her "cousin" by virtue of being the cousin of the Prophet] has not died. Rather, in fear of God he has fainted. This condition overcomes him often.' The 'fear' in question here is evidently to be understood as 'awe', and relates to the heavenly aspect of khawf — heavenly because Paradise is said to be the consequence of realising this kind of fear to perfection — as the Qur'an indicates: 'But whoso feareth (khafa) the station of his Lord and restraineth his soul from caprice, verily, the Garden will be his abode' (79: 40-41). 'Fear of God', in the spiritual rather than the moralistic sense, does not so much terrify as beatify; it produces not simply quaking awe before the transcendent Absolute, but also intimate absorption within the immanent divine presence. As is often quoted in Sufism, when one fears creatures one flees from them, but when one fears God one flees to Him. 'There is no refuge from God except [fleeing] to Him' (9: 118), as the Qur'an puts it.

This report also shows that the following saying of the Imam is not mere extravagant rhetoric; rather, it appears to allude to an authentic state of spiritual intoxication, a total re-absorption of consciousness within its divine source — a description of the conditions leading up to the experience of the state that 'overcomes him often', as Fatima put it:

Truly, God has a drink for His friends (awliya'ihi). When they drink it, they are intoxicated (sakara); and when they are intoxicated, they are enraptured ('araba); and when they are enraptured, they are blessed (taba); and when they are blessed, they dissolve (dhaba); and when they dissolve, they are free (khalasa); and when they are free, they devote themselves purely (akhlasa); and when they devote themselves purely, they seek (talaba); and when they seek, they find (wajada); and when they find, they arrive (wasala); and when they arrive, they are at one (ittasala); there is no difference between them and their Beloved.

This saying of the Imam echoes that of the Prophet, who claimed that he had states or 'moments' (waqt) that no 'angel brought close' nor 'prophet sent forth' could encompass — the implication being that neither angel nor prophet can subsist as such in the ineffable 'moments' of absolute tawhid, which is not 'union', it must be stressed: it is, rather, the realisation of oneness. For union implies two entities becoming one, whereas God is, as the Imam put it, 'that which has no second' — and indeed, even to call Him 'one' falls short, since that word implies that a second might stand in sequence to Him; He is more truly named unique, a reality that admits no otherness. This ultimate realisation of tawhid can be seen as the fruit of the invocation — the most potent of all intoxicating 'drinks', if prayer may be described as drink — and leads one to consider carefully the true agent of the invocation, the true 'drinker'. For what appears to be man's invocation of God is transformed into the divine invocation of man; whereas at the outset it seems that man 'drinks' God through His Name, the ultimate result of the invocation — unitive realisation — reveals that it is in fact God who 'drinks into Himself' the essence of human consciousness, an essence from which the impurity of self-consciousness has been extracted. The true nature of the dhikr, masked by the outward form of man's invocation of God, is alluded to in another saying of the Imam: 'The invocation is not a formality of speech nor a way of thinking; rather, it comes forth firstly from the Invoked, and secondly from the invoker (awwal min al-madhkur wa thanin min al-dhakir).'

It is thus God Himself who performs the invocation, first and foremost; man's invocation is but a shadow, a reflection, a consequence of this divine invocation. The polyvalence of the Imam's brief sentence opens onto a number of fruitful meditations on the meaning, the origin, the end, and the very substance of the dhikr. Among other things, one recalls here the Qur'anic verse cited above: 'Remember Me, I will remember you.'

Before addressing one of the metaphysical implications of God being Himself the Invoker, it is important to appreciate how this perception of the reality of the invocation reveals its spiritual efficacy more clearly. For the reality denoted by the term dhikr is no longer confined within the boundaries of the individual, still less definable in terms of his verbal or mental activity. Instead, what dhikr means in its primary, metaphysical significance opens out into the realm of engendering, implying the function of existentiation, the effusion of Being. The creative Word uttered by God — 'Be! (kun)' — which brings the possibilities of being into existence, can be seen in this light as a form of dhikr: God utters the 'name' of the possible thing, 'calling' it from nonexistence into existence. This passage from nothingness to being is what renders the possible thing 'remembered', madhkur. Here again, the Imam's sentence can be read both as a commentary on a Qur'anic verse and as an anticipation of the highly elaborate cosmogony associated with Ibn al-'Arabi.

The Qur'an poses the following question: 'Hath there come upon man any moment in time when he was not a thing remembered?' (76: 1). Man's being a 'thing remembered' is here tantamount to his coming into being, and his nothingness is equivalent to his being 'not remembered' — that is, not yet called forth from the abyss of pre-existential hiddenness into the light of manifest existence. In this pre-existential state, the human being is, according to Ibn al-'Arabi, 'known', ma'lum, but only by God. He refers to the above verse in the following lines of al-Futuhat al-makkiyya (The Meccan Illuminations):

God said of Himself that He is al-Badi', the Originator, that is, He created that which had no like or exemplar in all the degrees of being, for He knows, by His all-encompassing nature, every single thing that enters into the degrees of being. Therefore He says, as regards the creation of man, that he 'was not a thing remembered', for His is the remembrance ... so a thing has being in the remembrance of the one who remembers it.

God's 'remembrance' of man thus precedes man's remembrance of God — ontologically, not merely temporally — and this constitutes another interpretation of the verse cited earlier: '... and the remembrance of God is greater', infinitely greater, that is, than man's remembrance of Him. The true being of man resides in the 'remembrance' God has of him. While man's devotion to God is articulated through the utterance of the divine Name, God's creation of man is articulated through His utterance of the creative Word. In Ibn al-'Arabi's cosmogony, the utterance of this Word is not simply an event in time; rather, that Word never ceases to reverberate, furnishing each human soul with its divine substrate, support, or foundation — its mustanad ilahi. This Word 'breathes' existence upon the immutable entity, al-'ayn al-thabita, which, though inexistent in itself, determines the contours, form, and configuration of being that will be manifested through it — just as a prism refracts the light passing through it, or as water takes on the colour of its cup, an image used by Junayd and frequently quoted by Ibn al-'Arabi.

It is also worth noting that the hadith al-haqiqa, attributed to Ali (ع), contains the essence of the Akbari doctrine of the tajalli of the unique Light of God upon the differentiated 'receptacles' — the loci of manifestation for that Light. The Imam is questioned by his disciple Kumayl on the meaning of al-haqiqa. The response includes the following affirmations:

Al-haqiqa is the unveiling of the splendours of Majesty, without any allusion ... the effacement of that which is erroneously imagined (al-mawham), together with the clarity of that which is truly known (al-ma'lum) ... the attraction of absolute unicity to the quality of tawhid ... a light dawning from the morn of eternity, its traces shimmering on the temples of tawhid ...

Al-Kashani comments on this last image: 'That is, the manifestation of the Light of the one Essence ... upon the loci of manifestation of the qualities of the Real, the Essence, these being the loci of the entities of existents (a'yan al-mawjudat), which the Imam calls "temples of tawhid", that is, the forms of the divine Names in the station of tawhid.' This mustanad ilahi, or divine support of each individual soul, is therefore its particular Lord, the divine Name that engenders and sustains it. The soul is but the locus of manifestation, the mazhar, where God discloses Himself in a particular, unique mode.

The very first words of the khutba with which Ibn al-'Arabi opens the Futuhat al-makkiyya are these: 'Praise be to Him who engendered things from nothing, then negated it; and established their being through the orientation towards them of His Words.' This orientation (tawajjuh) of the divine Word towards the possible thing results in the manifestation of the Word's properties in accordance with the receptivity of that possible thing; the possibility is then 'breathed' into existence and becomes akin to a 'body', the 'heart' of which is the divine Name existentiating it: '... every slave has a Name which is its Lord; so it becomes the "body" of that Name, which is its "heart".'

This analogy recalls the Imam's image of the dhikr as a 'polish' for hearts. The Name revealed from 'above' is mirrored by a Name that resounds from 'within'. The reciprocal reflection appears when the rust obscuring the surface of the heart is removed by the polish of the dhikr. A mode of union between two divine realities is evoked here, calling to mind once again the phrase 'light upon light'.

Earlier we saw that the Imam referred to the Names of God 'which have filled the foundations of all things'; this clearly foreshadows Ibn al-'Arabi's conception of the divine Names as the supports or substrata of all things. But a question arises here, the answers to which may help bring us a step closer to comprehending the powerful and mysterious efficacy of the dhikr. What is the difference between the divine Names conceived as the metaphysical roots of all things, and the particular Names of God as revealed in the Qur'an? On one hand, the whole of creation is the 'Book of God', all phenomena being so many 'words' of God; on the other, the revealed text contains specific words and Names, and it is these that are to be recited and invoked. Why? One clear answer is given in the following verse: 'Call upon Allah or call upon al-Rahman. By whatever Name you call upon Him, His are the most beautiful Names' (17: 110). The Qur'an itself enjoins believers to invoke these specific, revealed Names, and not others. These Names are therefore utterly unlike the names of the idolaters' gods: 'They are but names that ye have named, ye and your fathers, for which God hath revealed no authority' (53: 23).

The names of idols resemble conventional names of phenomena in that they are merely arbitrary labels or signs with no intrinsic relationship to the phenomena themselves, let alone to the principles underlying those phenomena. By contrast, the Names of God, revealed by God, are penetrated by the very substance of the reality they denote; these Names therefore possess 'authority' (sultan), by virtue of which they have the power to transform and not merely to inform. They are, in other words, impregnated with a theurgic power — the capacity to render present to mind and heart the immanent reality of God. Though God's presence is inalienable, since nothing existent can possibly lie 'outside' His all-encompassing presence, that presence can nonetheless become veiled, at least subjectively. The heart can be veiled from God by the 'rust' of forgetfulness covering it, such that the divine Reality comes to be 'veiled' by the very phenomena that manifest it. As noted earlier, the Imam challenges us to consider how it is 'by things' that the Creator manifests Himself, and yet it is also 'by things' that He is hidden from sight.

This being the case, what is needed to open oneself to the divine presence within and beyond all things is both a polishing of one's own 'within' through the dhikr, and an unveiling or revelation from that 'beyond'. This latter form of disclosure can be understood as a direct revelation of God within that indirect revelation of Him which consists in the entirety of the created cosmos — a cosmos that simultaneously reveals and veils the divine Reality. Even if, from a certain point of view, the two 'revelations' — cosmic and scriptural — are both defined as manifestations of God, from another point of view the scriptural revelation is charged with a spiritual power that renders it incomparably more 'revealing' than the created cosmos. There is no common measure, in terms of spiritual method, between the cosmos as manifested divine 'Word' and Allah as a revealed Name of God. On one hand there is differentiated manifestation; on the other, a unique revelation. One might also say that the divine Name is the quintessential manifestation, at once summing up the entire realm of manifestation and transcending it. The difference between cosmic manifestation and scriptural revelation might be seen in one respect as analogous to that between shadow and light. Revelation is like a projection of the pure Light of God, and is therefore of one substance with the source from which it emanates, whereas manifestation is like the shadow cast by that light. Both are projections of one and the same reality, but this does not diminish the incommensurability between light and darkness. In the words of Ibn al-'Arabi, 'The very Being of the Real is a verified Light, the very existence of creation is a shadow following after.'


<!-- page 246 -->
There is a remarkable supplication attributed to Ali (ع), the Du'a' al-Mashlal, 'supplication of the lame man'. It consists of 122 verses containing a multitude of divine Names, attributes, and titles. Towards the end of the supplication one reads a verse that can be taken as the key to the entire text: 'I ask Thee by every Name with which Thou hast named Thyself.' It is the fact that God 'names Himself' that imparts to His Names their theurgic power, their sacramental substance, their mystical efficacy. It is the revealed substance of the Names that accounts for these distinctive properties and renders them incomparable to any other names. This is why, as Sufi texts so commonly assert, the Name is the Named (al-ism huwa'l-musamma). These reflections on the ontological relationship between the Names of God and the nature of being, while they help explain certain aspects of the dhikr as a mystical practice, nonetheless fall short of accounting for that ontological leap of consciousness — that extinction of self-awareness — to which the Imam refers in the words, 'you will not remember Him according to the true reality of the remembrance until you forget your own soul in your remembrance'. One is led to ask: why and how does the dhikr produce this extinction of consciousness, this effacement of individual being, especially given that the function of dhikr appears to be precisely the opposite — namely, to actualize or precipitate a heightened mode of consciousness and to enhance or deepen one's quality of being?

The first point to be made by way of response is this: the individual cannot on his own make this leap from remembrance to forgetfulness. It is impossible for a conscious person, concentrating on the invocatory act, to render himself unconscious of himself in the course of that invocation. One should note carefully that the Imam says the soul is to be forgotten 'in' the remembrance. Any effort to will oneself into a state of unconsciousness emanates from a conscious will and thus entrenches, rather than effaces, self-consciousness. This is therefore not a matter of forcing oneself to forget oneself while invoking God; it is rather a matter of total concentration on the Name — an attempt to be so focused on the Name that no room remains in one's consciousness, no 'space' mentally or existentially, for anything but the Named, the reality being invoked. A certain initial degree of self-effacement can thus be seen as a natural concomitant of total concentration. The ultimate consummation of self-effacement in fana', however, comes about as a divine grace, as something that surpasses all individual effort, even if the act of invocation is understood to attract this grace — or to remove from the heart the 'rust' that obscures the vision of one's own nothingness before the sole reality of God, 'that which has no second'. This part of the response can be summed up by an esoteric reading of the Qur'anic words, '... thou wilt not forget, unless God willeth it so' (87: 6-7). It is also worth noting that al-Kalabadhi, author of one of the most important early manuals of Sufism, opens his chapter on dhikr with words that echo those of the Imam: 'The haqiqa of the dhikr is this: that you forget everything other than the madhkur (the Invoked) in the dhikr. For He says: "So remember thy Lord when thou hast forgotten" (18: 24). That is, when you have forgotten what is not God, then you have remembered God.'

<!-- page 247 -->
The second response to the question can be framed in terms of the principle of inverse analogy. The human invocation is analogous to the divine invocation — being, as noted earlier, a kind of reflection of it — and, like any reflected image, the human act of invocation both positively participates in what it reflects and inverts it. In the first respect, the human act of invocation leads to a greater plenitude of consciousness and a fuller participation in being for the invoker; in the second respect, the human invocation finds its consummation in what appears to be the very opposite: the effacement of the individual in the object invoked. Yet this is only apparently opposed to the enhancement of consciousness and being. In reality it is the fulfilment of that same process, though its final stage involves a quantum leap — the negation of the limits that constitute individuality as such. Insofar as the individual is limited, and insofar as limitation is itself a negation, the negation of this negation amounts to pure affirmation: the affirmation of unconditional, supra-individual being. This negation of negation, on the plane of the self, can be seen as the reflection of that negation of nothingness referred to by Ibn al-'Arabi in the line quoted above: 'Praise be to Him who engendered things from nothing, then negated it.' In this connection it is worth recalling the enigmatic saying of the Imam cited earlier: 'I am the death of the dead.' This recalls the words Rumi places in the Imam's mouth in the following celebrated lines of his Mathnawi:

He said, 'I am wielding the sword for God's sake, I am the servant of God, I am not under the command of the body.

I am the Lion of God, I am not the lion of my passion: my deed bears witness to my religion.

In war I am (manifesting the truth of) thou didst not throw when thou threwest: I am (but) as the sword, and the wielder is the (divine) Sun.

I have removed the baggage of self out of the way, I have deemed (what is) other than God to be non-existence.

I am a shadow, the Sun is my lord; I am the chamberlain, I am not the curtain (which prevents approach) to Him.

I am filled with the pearls of union, like a (jewelled) sword: in battle I make (men) living, not slain.

In other words, whereas the cosmic unfolding of creation — the movement from God to man — requires the negation of that relative nothingness which is pregnant with the possibilities of being, the realization of God by man requires the negation of that same 'nothingness' that was breathed into being by God. Returning to Ibn al-'Arabi's treatise Extinction in Contemplation, what is at stake here is 'the extinction of that which never was ... and the subsistence of that which never ceased to be.' The following Qur'anic verses are clearly implied: 'Everything thereon perisheth (fan), and there subsisteth (yabqa) only the Face of thy Lord, possessor of glory and bounty' (55: 26-27).

<!-- page 248 -->
Yet all things, insofar as they exist, have two aspects or 'faces', as noted above: the divine aspect, which is the true being of the thing, and the created aspect, which is 'nothing'. The 'Face' of the Lord that alone subsists is therefore not simply the divine Reality above and beyond all things, but also that same reality within all things. This is the import of al-Ghazali's esoteric commentary on the Qur'anic verse, 'Everything perisheth except His Face' (28: 88). Al-Ghazali comments:

[it is] not that it perisheth at some particular moment, but rather it is forever perishing, since it cannot be conceived except as perishing. For each thing other than God is, when considered in and by itself, pure nothingness; and if considered from the face (wajh) to which existence flows to it from the Prime Reality, it is viewed as existing, but not in itself, solely from the face which accompanies Him Who gives it existence. Therefore, the divine face is the sole thing in reality.

He then connects the mystical realization of this oneness to the invocation of God and to the forgetting of the self in that invocation, and in doing so provides a valuable commentary on the meaning of this total dissolution of self-consciousness. The Sufis who verify this oneness through mystical 'taste' (dhawq), he writes, are 'drowned in oneness', such that they become 'dumbfounded':

No capacity remained within them save to recall Allah; yea, not so much as the capacity to recall their own selves ... when this state prevails, it is called in relation to him who experiences it Extinction, nay Extinction of Extinction, for the soul has become extinct to itself, extinct to its own extinction; for it becomes unconscious of itself and unconscious of its own unconsciousness, since were it conscious of its own unconsciousness, it would be conscious of itself.

Also pertinent here is the following statement attributed by al-Qushayrī to Abu 'Uthman, which sheds light on another dimension of the 'unconsciousness' realized through invocation: 'Whoever has not tasted the loneliness of unconsciousness will not find the food of the intimacy of remembrance.' Here, losing consciousness of oneself is equated with 'loneliness', while becoming fully conscious of God through invocation results in 'intimacy'. One cannot find true intimacy with the divine Reality except by depriving the self of its false 'intimacy' — that self-satisfied, habitual self-centredness which is the shadow cast by self-awareness. It is this awareness of self that, to return to the Imam's statement, must be forgotten. This higher forgetfulness cannot arise except on the basis of the remembrance of God, for it is the source of Being, unconditional Consciousness, and eternal Life that alone is capable of extinguishing, eclipsing, or effacing the relative existence, partial consciousness, and apparent life of the individual. This, the haqīqa of the dhikr, is expressed with perfect economy in the Imam's own words: '... forget your own soul in your remembrance.'

<!-- page 249 -->
The state of fanā' — the ultimate degree of this 'forgetting' of oneself — is therefore a grace; it cannot be attained by the individual, however great the effort he exerts, as was seen earlier. But this does not imply any abandonment of effort on the part of the invoker. On the contrary, as the Sufi tradition abundantly attests, fanā' is closely linked with the practice of dhikr, being its very climax. Subsequent to this state, or ḥāl, of extinction comes the station, or maqām, of subsistence, baqā' — a return of consciousness to the self, a self now transfigured by the knowledge of reality impressed upon the soul in the state of fanā'. For it is only in the state of fanā' that the ultimate 'vision' of absolute oneness is attained, a vision in which there is no room for the ontological duality that is the inescapable concomitant of individuality. To posit the 'individual' is to posit its correlate, the Lord, and thus to affirm duality.

Al-Kāshānī makes this point in his commentary on the Qur'anic verse, 'Remember Him as He hath guided you' (2:198), where he traces the guided ascent through the degrees of the dhikr.
