The first is the dhikr of the tongue, which corresponds to the soul (al-nafs); then that of the heart (al-qalb), which corresponds to the divine acts, those which manifest His graces; then that of the 'secret' (al-sirr), corresponding to the vision of the divine acts together with 'the unveiling of the sciences of the manifestation of the divine qualities'; then that of the spirit (al-rūḥ), corresponding to 'the contemplation of the lights of the manifestations of the divine qualities, with an apprehension of the light of the Essence'; then comes the dhikr of 'the hidden' (al-khafī), which is 'the contemplation of the beauty of the Essence along with the subsistence of duality'; and finally the dhikr of the Essence, 'which is essential vision, attained through the removal of duality.'

The same point emerges from the following counsel of Ibn 'Aṭā'illāh, which illuminates the way in which the dhikr generates receptivity to the divine presence and to the grace by which alone the individual can truly 'forget' himself — in the sense of becoming 'unconscious of his own unconsciousness', as al-Ghazālī put it:

Do not abandon the invocation because you do not feel the Presence of God therein. For your forgetfulness of the invocation of Him is worse than your forgetfulness in the invocation of Him. Perhaps He will take you from an invocation with forgetfulness (ghafla) to one with vigilance (yaqaẓa), and from one with vigilance to one with the Presence of God (ḥuḍūr), and from one with the Presence of God to one wherein everything but the Invoked (al-Madhkūr) is absent. 'And that is not difficult for God.'

<!-- page 250 -->
In his commentary on the Ḥikam, Ibn 'Ajība adds: 'So, in the measure that one is extinguished in the Name, one is extinguished in the Essence.' This follows from what was said above: the Name is the Named. In another treatise — a commentary on a poem by Shushtarī on the divine Name Allāh — he articulates this cardinal tenet of the invocatory discipline in Sufism more explicitly. The first line of the poem reads, referring to the letters of which Allāh is composed: 'The alif before two lāms, then a hā': coolness of the eyes.' Ibn 'Ajība comments: 'The "coolness of the eyes" — this is joy, for tears of joy, as one knows by experience, are cool. The author means this: this Name is the joy of my heart, its happiness, its beatitude.' He then cites some lines from al-Ḥallāj on the letters of the Name Allāh, adds a verse of his own, and follows with this comment: 'The Name is not other than the Named, for joy cannot come from anything but the essence of a thing, not from the name as such.'

This encapsulates the doctrine of identity between the Name and the Named — the rationale for the Sufi practice of dhikru'Llāh. Yet here a certain problem arises. However congruent the Imam's pronouncements on the dhikr may be with these later Sufi expressions of the underlying principles, this particular stress on the identity between the Name and the Named appears, on the surface, to stand in tension with numerous sayings of the Shi'i Imams which deny that the Name is the Named. Imam Ja'far al-Ṣādiq, for instance, is reported as saying:

The Name of Allāh is other than Him. For everything to which the name of a thing is applicable is a creation, hence other than God. For whatever the tongue expresses, or is worked on by the hand, is a creation ... everything described is created, and the Creator of things cannot be described within the limits of a named object.

Allāh has ninety-nine Names. If the Name were the Named, then every one of the Names would be a god. But Allāh is the one meaning — or spiritual reality, ma'nā — indicated by the Names, and all of them are other than Him.

<!-- page 251 -->
This apparent contradiction can be resolved in two ways. First, one must attend carefully to what is meant by the word 'Name' in the two respective contexts: that of doctrinal distinction, in which the Absolute is rigorously distinguished from the relative and the Essence from all forms; and that of methodic concentration, in which the Absolute is understood to be the reality of the very form through which it reveals itself. One can then turn to one of the most esoteric aspects of the Shi'i Imams' conception of themselves — their references to their own selves as the 'most beautiful Names' (al-asmā' al-ḥusnā).

As regards the meaning of 'Name', Ibn al-'Arabī offers a precise definition: '[T]he Names have two connotations; the first connotation is God Himself who is what is named, the second that by which one Name is distinguished from another ... As being essentially the other, the Name is the Reality, while as being not the other, it is the imagined Reality.' Each Name is, on the one hand, identified with every other Name by virtue of its essential identity with the Named, the one Essence that is the source of all the Names; on the other hand, each Name has its own specific meaning and quality — the relationship by which the one Reality engages with the created cosmos. In this latter respect, each Name is distinguished from the others, and distinction implies limitation, hence relativity and, ultimately, a kind of illusion before the one and only Reality. It is in this sense that the Name assumes the character of 'imagined Reality'. In Ibn al-'Arabī's perspective, the Names have no separate ontological entities of their own; they all revert to the one and only Reality, that of the divine Essence. This dissolves the problem, raised in the saying of Imam Ja'far, of different Names implying different 'gods'.

In the lexicon of Sufi terms, Laṭā'if al-i'lām fī ishārāt ahl al-ilhām (Subtle Disclosures in the Allusions of the Folk of Inspiration), attributed to al-Kāshānī, one finds a definition of the divine Names that also helps to distinguish between these two connotations. Under the term 'Names of the Divinity', we read as follows:


These are, in the terminology of the Sufis, expressive of the manifest aspect of being, in respect of its determination within a specific meaning. For every divine Name is the manifest aspect of being, which, in turn, is identical to the Essence — but not as the Essence is in itself; rather, in respect of its specification and determination within a particular meaning, or what one might call a 'quality'. For example, the Name al-Ḥayy ('The Living') is a Name of manifest, determined being, but in respect of its specification and determination as a particular meaning, it is Life. So in regard to being itself, al-Ḥayy is identical to the Essence, whilst in regard to its determination as a particular meaning, it is other than the Essence. If you have understood what we have said, you will know what is meant by the saying: the Name is not identical to the Essence, the Named, neither is it other than It.

There is a nominal and cognitive distinction between the Name and the Named, in terms of which the Name is not the Named; but at the same time, there is a spiritual assimilation between the Name and the Named, such that the Name is the Named. Beyond this, a further distinction must be taken into account: the verbal form of the Name is to be distinguished from the Name as an attribute of the Essence. On the one hand, the Name in this latter sense is nothing but the divine attribute it designates, and this attribute is in turn identical to the Essence; on the other hand, there is the 'name' of this Name, which is merely a verbal form. This distinction is brought to light by Qayṣarī, another key commentator of Ibn al-'Arabī. In the introduction to his commentary on the Fuṣūṣ al-ḥikam — a major work in its own right, a masterly and succinct exposition of the essential elements of the Akbarī perspective as a whole — he writes:

The Essence, considered together with a determined quality and in relation to one of Its theophanies, is named by a Name; so 'The Compassionate' has an essence, which is compassion; 'The All-Conquering' has an essence, which is all-conquering power. These spoken Names are Names of Names; from this it can be understood what is meant by the saying: The Name is identical to the Named.

In his own monumental commentary on Qayṣarī's introduction, Jalāl al-Dīn Āshtiyanī, a contemporary authority on Shi'i 'Irfan, makes an important observation after citing the passage above — one that brings the discussion back to the principle of extinction through the invocation of the Name, and also to the second point raised earlier, namely that the Imams themselves constitute the 'most beautiful Names'. Āshtiyanī writes that the saints frequently speak of the Names as being one with the Named because they are implicitly referring to the referent (misdaq), which is the sifa, the divine attribute or quality: 'The sifat are in one respect the specifications of the Essence, while in the station of unicity they are annihilated in the Essence, and are [thus] identical to the Essence.'

This metaphysical conception of the relationship between the Name, the sifa, and the Essence can be seen as mirroring, objectively and universally, the path traced subjectively and mystically by the soul extinguished in and by the invocation of the Name — this extinction being what is meant by fana ⟪ar:فنیٰ⟫, the perishing of egoistic consciousness before the Absolute. Just as the ṣifat are 'annihilated' in the Essence — with which they are thus absolutely and unconditionally one — so the soul extinguished by the Name attains at one and the same time its total nullification and its ultimate realization, though no longer as an individual soul. It is only by virtue of the divine theophany, or the particular divine Name — the 'heart' that constitutes the soul's true being, its mustanad ilahi — that this 'extinction' on the plane of the individual soul amounts to 'realization' on the plane of transcendence. Here the deepest reality of tawhid is revealed: the individual no longer affirms the notion that 'there is no god but God', for the individual has been extinguished by the reality that 'there is no reality but the one and only Reality'. God is 'that which has no second'. Just as 'No one has truly said Allah but Allah',130 so too no one realizes God but God. It is God alone who comes to 'know' or 'be' Himself — this constituting one aspect of the purpose of creation, as indicated in the hadith qudsi cited earlier: 'I was a hidden treasure and I loved to be known.'

In this light the saints (al-awliya') can be seen as perfectly self-realized theophanies,131 totally effaced in that of which they are theophanic manifestations, their very effacement in the divine Reality being the ultimate source of their knowledge of it. Needless to say, this applies a fortiori to the Prophet himself, the prototype of all the saints and the 'perfect man', al-insan al-kamil. In this connection, Sabziwarī cites the saying of Ali (ع), 'We are the most beautiful Names', in his commentary on the divine Names: 'There is no doubt that they [the Imams] are His most exalted indicators and His greatest signs ('ala imihi'l-'uzma wa ayatihi'l-kubra), as the Prophet said, God bless him and his family, "Whoso sees me has seen the Truth (al-Haqq)."'132 He proceeds to equate the spiritual station, or inner reality, of the Imams with those of the divine Names and attributes, stressing that the divine Essence transcends all Names and attributes and is forever ineffable, known only to itself. The Prophet, the Imam, the saint, the 'perfect man', thus manifest those aspects of the divine nature that can be perceived on the human plane. The more the divine Essence is exalted in transcendence above all Names and attributes, the more brilliantly those Names and attributes are reflected in the mirror constituted by the 'perfect man'. The unknowable transcendence of the hidden 'treasure' is, to some degree, compensated by the faithful reflection of its communicable aspects in the 'friend of God'.

It is worth dwelling, in conclusion, on the implications of identifying the Imam with the divine Name or attribute; for in one respect this identification would appear to be shirk — the 'association' of a creature, however exalted, with the transcendent Reality. The same applies to the ecstatic utterances (shathiyyat) of many of the most celebrated Sufis, such as the ana'l-Haqq ('I am the Real') of al-Hallaj and the subhanī ('Glory to me') of Bayazīd.133 However, when one adopts a viewpoint shaped by the Imam's own conception of the divine attributes, a very different picture emerges — one that, far from being tainted by shirk, in fact reaches the most profound application of tawhid. The first sermon of the Nahj al-balagha contains, as we have seen, the following remarkable and highly important statements regarding the attributes of God:

The perfection of purification (ikhlas) is to divest Him of all attributes (sifat) because of the testimony of every attribute that it is other than the object of attribution, and because of the testimony of every such object that it is other than the attribute. So whoever ascribes an attribute to God — glorified be He! — has conjoined Him [with something else]; and whoever so conjoins Him has made Him twofold; and whoever makes Him twofold has fragmented Him; and whoever thus fragments Him is ignorant of Him.134

When the Imam says that pure devotion to God requires divesting God of all attributes, the negation here is not of the divine attributes as such; rather, it is a negation of them as separate, autonomous, or super-added realities conceived of as substantially 'other than' or ontologically distinct from the Essence of God. For the reality of God's attributes is clearly affirmed in an earlier phrase in the same sermon: 'He whose attribute has no binding limitation (alladhi laysa li-sifatihi hadd mahdud).' What is denied is the possibility of confining the reality of any divine attribute within the boundaries of human conception and language. Since no divine attribute can be distinct from the divine nature, and since this nature is infinite, every attribute is likewise of infinite scope and thus beyond all the limitations presupposed by formal conception and linguistic definition.

In one respect, then, the attribute or Name is to be affirmed, and in another respect it is to be negated: affirmed as not other than the Essence, negated insofar as it is conceived in itself, on its own account. This metaphysical perception of the relationship between the attribute or Name and the Essence can also be applied, mystically, to the relationship between the individual soul and the divine Reality. On its own account the individual is nothing, while in respect of the divine substrate — the divine Name, the mustanad ilahi that constitutes its essential reality — it is not other than the unique Real. According to Ibn al-'Arabī, 'The final end and ultimate return of the gnostics ... is that the Real is identical with them, while they do not exist.' This is a perfect expression of the dual aspect of the soul of the realized sage: insofar as he is aware that 'he does not exist', he knows that the Real is his true identity. However paradoxical it may appear, the mystic's realization of absolute Reality goes hand in hand with his realization of his own non-existence. These two aspects of the one and same realization are elliptically expressed by Ibn al-'Arabī. He describes the climax of his spiritual ascent, his mi'raj, when he recounts that God removed from him his 'contingent dimension (imkan)', resulting in the attainment of 'the inner realities of all the Names'. He continues:

Thus I attained in this nocturnal journey the inner realities (ma'ani) of all the Names, and I saw them returning to One Subject (musamma) and One Entity ('ayn): that Subject was what I witnessed and that Entity was my Being. For my voyage was only in myself and pointed to myself, and through this I came to know that I was a pure 'servant' without a trace of lordship in me at all.136

This passage helps explain so many of the ecstatic utterances of the Sufis and the apparently self-deifying statements of the Imams. Their personal servitude to God is not denied — on the contrary, it is fully realized through the knowledge of their true identity, for that identity as the Named is attained only upon the effacement of their individuality. Like the divine attributes, they are mustahlak, annihilated in the divine Essence, so that there can be no question of any individualistic appropriation of the glory of God. When the Imam appears to speak of his own greatness, it is to be understood that this is nothing but the greatness of God being expressed through him as a mazhar, a locus for the theophanic manifestation of the divine — and this, precisely in the measure of his own effacement in that greatness.

The same point is reinforced by what 'Attar recounts in his Tadhkiratu'l-awliya' about Imam Ja'far al-Sadiq. A person accused him of being proud, and the Imam replied: 'I am not proud, for pride pertains to the divine greatness. Since I have gone beyond the pride of self, His pride has come and taken the place of my own pride. With pride of self present, there can be no divine greatness, but through the divine greatness, pride will be present.' The divine qualities shine with greater, not lesser, brilliance through the prism of a soul rendered transparent to the divine Reality — a transparency that results from the dissolution of all the clouds of egoism and passion.

Let us recall the holy utterance cited earlier: 'My slave draws near to Me through nothing I love more than that which I have made obligatory for him. My slave never ceases to draw near to Me through supererogatory acts (nawafil) until I love him. And when I love him, I am his hearing by which he hears, his sight by which he sees, his hand by which he grasps, and his foot by which he walks.'

In the light of the foregoing, the paradox contained in this divine utterance can now be resolved. On the one hand, the most beloved thing to God appears to be the obligatory rites; on the other, it is the supererogatory rites that lead the slave to draw near to God until He loves him, this love being apparently greater insofar as it results in the divine penetration of the human faculties — the unitive realization proper to tawhid. The paradox resolves, however, once one understands that God's 'love' for the obligatory rites is a love directed toward something apart from Himself, whereas when His love is for the 'slave' — the pure slave effaced in his Lord — this love is infinitely greater, insofar as it partakes of God's love for His own Self-manifestation, and thus for Himself. In the beginning, God 'loved to be known', and so He created the world. In 'knowing' Himself through the effaced slave, the apparent 'other', His love to be known is realized through the saint whose greatest glory is, precisely, his own effacement, poverty, and emptiness of self.

It is in this sense that the following statement of the Imam is to be understood: 'Down from me torrents do pour; up to me no bird can soar.' Torrents flow down from him because — and in the measure that — he is extinguished in the source of the torrent. This image recalls the Qur'anic description of the 'slaves of God' who, unlike the 'righteous', drink directly from the celestial spring of Kafur, identified ultimately with the divine Essence: 'Truly, the righteous shall drink from a cup flavoured with Kafür, a spring wherefrom the slaves of God drink, making it gush forth abundantly' (76: 5-6). These slaves cause the spring's flow to increase because, according to al-Kāshānī, 'they are [themselves] the sources of this spring; there is no duality or otherness ... were it otherwise, it would not be the fountain of Kafur, because of the darkness of the veil of egoity (ana iyya) and duality.'

We thus return to the final degree of the dhikr as al-Kāshānī defined it above: the dhikr of the Essence, 'which is essential vision, attained through the removal of duality'. The dhikr 'polishes' away not only the impurities of the heart and mind but, ultimately, through the grace of the madhkur, all taint of duality — including, pre-eminently, that fundamental postulate of duality, the individual as such. It removes all subtle or hidden shirk — the 'partners' in being that are non-existent before the One 'that has no second' — and establishes the spiritual principle of tawhid, such that the heart transfigured by the knowledge of the sole Reality comes to perceive the light of that Reality reflected in all the mirrors of creation. To realize — to 'make real' in the fullest sense — this one and only Reality is, by the same token, to realize the unreality, or merely apparent reality, of everything else.

At this level of realization there is an inescapable degree of perplexity accompanying the revelation of the divine Reality on every side. The elevation of the divine Essence beyond all comprehension makes it all the more inescapable that creaturely consciousness must be utterly annihilated if that Essence is to be 'realized', if gnosis of it is to be attained. On the one hand, one 'knows' God by knowing that His Essence is unknowable, utterly other, utterly transcendent — the essence of the Creator lying beyond the veil constituted by creation itself; on the other hand, the very distinction between creature and Creator is effaced when the creature has 'forgotten' itself — not merely as a notion but existentially, in a state of authentic contemplative extinction. Then it is no longer a question of one form of self-consciousness replacing another, but of the 'self' being taken out of the equation altogether, leaving only pure consciousness as such, which cannot 'belong' to or pertain to any entity without being reduced to the relativity proper to that entity. Thus, consciousness of the Absolute — that is, 'being conscious of the Absolute' — is transmuted into absolute consciousness; the Real is 'realized' not by another, but through another, whence the designation al-'arif bi'Llah, literally 'the knower through God' rather than simply 'the knower of God.' As we noted earlier, the Imam tells us, 'Know God through God.' Knowledge of God at this deepest level is the Real knowing itself through the creature; and to say 'through' implies an unimpeded passage, the creature being qualified by perfect faqr — poverty, self-effacement, and, ultimately, extinction.


Realization through remembrance, however, implies not only 'forgetting' everything in existence except God, but also discovering that, in reality, there is nothing in being but God. To return to the Imam's image of the 'polish' effected by remembrance: the heart, once blind and deaf, is rendered capable of 'seeing' and 'hearing'. It sees the Face of God wherever it looks, and hears the cosmic hymn of remembrance and praise sung by the whole of creation — for every creature in existence proclaims the infinite creativity of its Creator. As the Qur'an says: 'Wherever ye turn, there is the Face of God' (2:115), and 'Do you not see that God is the one who is glorified by everything in the heavens and the earth, and the birds with wings outstretched; each one indeed knows its prayer and its glorification, and God is All-Aware of what they do' (24:41). But you understand not their glorification — so that whosoever is in the Heavens and the earth praises God, and the birds in flight with outstretched wings, each knoweth its prayer and glorification.

We conclude this chapter with the following reflection on a passage from the Imam's famous and much-disputed Khu;bat al-bayan, 'The Sermon of Clarifying Expression':

I am the Sign of the All-Powerful. I am the gnosis of the mysteries. I am the Threshold of Thresholds. I am the companion of the radiance of the divine Majesty. I am the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden. I am the Face of God. I am the mirror of God, the supreme Pen, the Tabula secreta. I am he who in the Gospel is called Elijah. I am he who is in possession of the secret of God's Messenger.

This extraordinary affirmation of the Imam's inmost reality may appear shocking to the exoteric mentality, and is all too easily misread as a blasphemous self-deification in the manner of Hallaj. One key to unlocking the correct understanding of these declarations of 'his' true nature is the statement: 'I am the gnosis of the mysteries.' Once this is grasped, every other statement falls naturally into place. For here the Imam is not identifying himself with God, but with the knowledge of God — and not with his own knowledge of God, since that would still be a relative knowledge belonging to himself as a relative being. Only knowledge itself — the knowledge God has of Himself, not the knowledge a human being has of God — can be fully identified with its object. The distinctions between knowledge, knower, and known dissolve only at this transcendent level, just as the distinction between knowledge and being ceases to operate there. Thus the knower is the known when the knowledge in question is that consciousness which the divine Reality has of itself; and individual consciousness gains access to this knowledge only on condition of the utter extinction of every trace of individual, creaturely selfhood. To 'remember' God, one must have 'forgotten' oneself. To 'be' the knowledge of God is, by way of metaphysical reverberation, to 'be' everything that God is, for the consciousness that is absolutely at one with the Absolute is nothing other than 'the radiance of the divine Majesty'. Insofar as this consciousness is one with 'the gnosis of the mysteries', and these mysteries (asrar) are all rooted in the ultimate mystery (al-sirr) — the one and only Essence of God — it is of necessity one with all the divine attributes. It is in this sense that the Imam can proclaim: 'I am the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden.'

Analogous remarks apply to the claim to be 'the Face of God' (wajh Allah). This is intelligible only once one first understands that the Imam describes himself as the 'mirror' of God. A mirror loses its identity as mirror when attention is fixed exclusively on the object reflected in it — that is, when the mirror's own surface becomes, as it were, invisible and only the object is seen. Conversely, when the mirror itself is focused upon, the face reflected within it can no longer be seen. The mirror is thus identified with the object it reflects — which, in a certain sense, is 'in' the mirror — without the mirror ceasing to be a mirror, and without the Face ceasing to be absolutely independent of it. The 'veil' here is the mirror itself: directing attention to it as such prevents one from seeing the object it reflects, but once attention is wholly fixed on the image the mirror 'contains', the mirror ceases to be a veil and instead reveals the object itself. Within this symbolism, forgetting the mirror corresponds to the effacement of the self, and focusing on the image within it corresponds to the remembrance of God.

Yet this leads to another paradox: the deepest effacement of individuality goes hand in hand with the most perfect expression of humanity. The 'friends of God', who know themselves to be nothing before the Absolute, are precisely the ones who most faithfully reflect the divine Names and qualities — which belong not to them but to God. This is why the great saints and sages are also exemplars of virtue, whose true foundations are the 'most beautiful Names' and attributes of God. The reflections of those divine attributes shine through them most clearly because the mirrors of their hearts have been polished by the remembrance of God and the most persistent stains — those imprinted by egotism — have been removed. The creativity of the saint is raised to its highest pitch precisely because the divine qualities reflected by the heart are no longer impeded, subjectively, by the individualistic coagulations generated by egotism, nor constrained, objectively, by the cosmic coagulations of this lower world. For such saints are those whose 'hearts are in the Gardens [of Paradise] while their bodies are at work'. Their hearts, bound and attached to that divine realm of the abodes and stations of paradise reaching all the way to the divine presence, are already 'within' that transcendent realm; they already live more in that spiritual world than in this earthly one. And so, though they labour in this world with their bodies, their outward actions veil their inward transcendence of higher realities: they act, but they are not bound by the fruits of their action.

Such souls have not merely understood this principle; they have been seized by it, existentially, at the very root of their being. It is the principle voiced in the Qur'anic verse: 'And the life of this world is naught but sport and play; and truly, the abode of the Hereafter — that is true life' (29:64). Their instincts are therefore always attuned to that higher reality. When Ali (ع) was struck by the poisoned sword of Ibn Muljam, the cry that broke from him was not one of pain but of triumph: 'I have triumphed, by the Lord of the Ka'ba!' At the very moment of death there was in him nothing but the remembrance of God, and the Imam passed away, it is said, with his tongue still repeating the words la ilaha illa'Llah. His death was thus consummated exactly as his life had been lived, in accordance with the wisdom of the Prophet, who, when asked which of all acts is the most meritorious, replied: 'That you die while your tongue is moistened with the invocation of God.'

All the rites and practices associated with the chivalric orders of Islam were traced, without exception, back to the Prophet's son-in-law, Ali (ع) ibn Abi Talib, who was thereby established as the supreme source of the virtues of futuwwa — as Muhammad Ja'far Mahjub observes in his 'Chivalry and Early Persian Sufism' (in Lewisohn, ed., Heritage, vol. 1, p. 552). S. H. Nasr writes in a similar vein that Ali (ع), having received the 'truth and power' of futuwwa from the Prophet, 'became the supreme source of futuwwa in Islam for both Sunnis and Shi'ites... The personality of Ali, at once sage and knight, contemplative and protector of laborers and craftsmen, has continued to dominate through the centuries over the horizon of futuwwa as it has over much of Sufism' ('Spiritual Chivalry', in Nasr, ed., Islamic Spirituality, vol. 2, pp. 305–306). Nasr also draws attention to the organic relationship between futuwwa and the guilds — the asnaf, singular sinf — of craftsmen and artists established throughout the length and breadth of the Muslim world.

It is often said that the Naqshabandi tariqa is the one exception to this rule, tracing its line of descent from the Prophet through the first caliph, Abu Bakr, rather than through Ali (ع). But this affiliation with Abu Bakr pertains to only one of its three principal silsilas. In his al-Hada'iq al-wardiyya fi haqa'iq ajilla al-Naqshabandiyya (Damascus, 1306/1889, p. 6), 'Abd al-Majid b. Muhammad al-Khani — himself a Naqshabandi-Khalidi shaykh — calls the first chain (al-silsilat al-ula) the 'golden chain', which begins with Ali (ع) and proceeds through the Shi'i Imams down to Ali (ع) b. Musa, then to Ma'ruf al-Karkhi and the other masters. The second chain (al-silsilatu'l-thaniya) likewise begins with Ali (ع), proceeding through Hasan al-Basri. Only the third chain, called al-silsilat al-siddiqiyya, proceeds from the Prophet to Abu Bakr (al-siddiq) — a reference owed to Professor Mahmut Kilic.

## The First Sermon of Nahj al-Balagha

This, the opening sermon of ⟪ar:نھج البلاغہ⟫, Maulana Ali (AS) delivered as a ⟪ar:خطبۃ⟫ proclaiming the oneness of God — a oneness, he teaches, that cannot fall into the category of numbers. It is built upon paradox, for the loftier the truth he means to reveal, the deeper the paradox he forms to reveal it: by negating the reach of human words and concepts he affirms the Reality that no word and no concept can bound. This is the manner of his sermons — the eye cannot see Him, yet the mind cannot deny Him; He is farther than the flights of imagination can reach, yet nearer to us than our own jugular vein — and nowhere is this plainer than in these opening lines. Praise be to God — الحَمْدُ للهِ الَّذَي لاَ يَبْلُغُ مِدْحَتَهُ القَائِلُونَ — whose laudation those who speak cannot deliver; here is the very paradox of speech, whose office is to convey a concept, turned to demonstrate that God is so lofty and sublime that words can deliver nothing of Him. Whose graces those who count cannot number — وَلاِ يُحْصِي نَعْمَاءَهُ العَادُّونَ — for counting is suited to enumeration, but not to His bounties; whose rightful due those who strive cannot render — ولاَ يُؤَدِّي حَقَّهُ الُمجْتَهِدُونَ — for however hard they strive, they can never render Him the praise and gratitude He is due. He who cannot be reached by far-reaching aspirations — الَّذِي لاَ يُدْركُهُ بُعْدُ الهِمَمِ — nor fathomed by profound intuitions — وَلاَ يَنَالُهُ غَوْصُ الفِطَنِ; He whose attribute has no binding limitation, no existing description, no time appointed, no term extended — الَّذِي لَيْسَ لِصِفَتِهِ حَدٌّ مَحْدُودٌ، وَلاَ نَعْتٌ مَوْجُودٌ، وَلا وَقْتٌ مَعْدُودٌ — so that His attribute cannot be confined to a definition and yet is affirmed as real, described by the very words of revelation even though no description can capture Him. Through His power He originated all created things, and through His mercy He diffused the comforting winds; and He entrenched with rocky pillars His swaying earth.

Foremost in religion is knowledge of Him; the perfection of this knowledge is believing in Him; the perfection of this belief is affirming His oneness; and the perfection of this affirmation is to purify one's devotion to Him — the perfection of sincerity, أخلاص, which is to render one's devotion and one's very conception of God pure. The perfection of this purification is to divest Him of all attributes (نفی الصفۃ), because every attribute bears witness that it is other than the object of attribution, and every such object bears witness that it is other than the attribute. So whoever ascribes an attribute to God — glorified be He! — has conjoined Him with something else; whoever conjoins Him has made Him twofold; whoever makes Him twofold has fragmented Him; and whoever fragments Him is ignorant of Him. Whoever points to Him confines Him; whoever confines Him counts Him; whoever asks 'in what?' encloses Him; and whoever asks 'upon what?' isolates Him. For the very attributes by which He is named in revelation — that He is the All-Compassionate (رحمن), the All-Merciful (رحیم), the Almighty (عزیز) — are affirmed as real and yet cannot be defined, bounded, or described: He is merciful, but not as any mercy we can picture; powerful, but not as any storm or flood or force we have known. Reason itself 'de-fines,' drawing a boundary, a ⟪ar:حد⟫, around whatever it grasps, and by breaking that boundary the sermon opens an awareness beyond the reach of speculative thought, so that in negating the limits of human words it affirms the Absolute that has no limit. This is the very movement of the testimony of faith: لا الہ, 'no deity,' which is the negation (نفی), and الا اللہ, 'except God,' which is the affirmation (اثبات); each attribute is negated only insofar as the limited mind conceives it, and is thereby affirmed as nothing but God Himself — La Rahmana illa'Llah, there is no All-Compassionate but God; La Raheema illa'Llah, there is no All-Merciful but God. Thus is God glorified above all that the mind would attribute to Him: ‏سُبْحَنَ ٱللَّهِ عَمَّا يَصِفُونَ — 'Glorified is God above all that they attribute to Him!' (Surat al-Mu'minun, 23:91).

Being, but not by way of any becoming; existing, but not from having been non-existent; with every thing, but not through association; and other than every thing, but not through separation; acting, but not through movements and instruments; seeing, even when nothing of His creation was to be seen; solitary, even when there was none whose intimacy might be sought or whose absence might be missed.

He brought forth creation, originating it from the very beginning, without pondering over any deliberation or benefiting from any previous experience; without initiating any movement or engaging in any unsettling exertion. He assigned to all things their span of time and harmonized their differences; He instilled within them their natural dispositions, impressing upon them their respective forms; knowing them before their genesis, encompassing their boundaries and their finalities; aware of their configurations and their contours.

Then He — glorified be He! — created a fissure in the atmospheres, cleaving apart directions and the uppermost levels of air; then He caused water to flow therein, a current of surging waves swelling and swirling in overflowing abundance. Then He mounted it upon a tempestuous wind, a thunderous gale; then He made the wind prevent the return of the water, enabling it to dominate the violence of its flow and aligning it with the boundaries of the water; the air beneath the wind was spread wide open, while the water above it flowed copiously.

Then He raised up a wind, making it sterile, fixing its course, intensifying its current, and distancing it from its source. Then He ordered it to agitate the swelling waters and to stir up oceanic waves. The wind churned up the waters — as one churns milk in a milkbag — imparting to the water its own violence in the atmosphere, turning its foremost back upon its hindmost and its stillness into motion, so that its torrents surged and its swelling waves threw up foam. Then He elevated that foam within a cleft air and a vast open atmosphere and formed from it the seven heavens, making the lowest of them a suspended wave and the highest a protected shelter and an elevated ceiling, without any pillars to support them or any clinch to transfix them. Then He adorned the heavens with the ornaments of the stars and the effulgence of the firmament, and made a radiant lamp — the sun — and an illuminating moon to follow an orbit in a revolving sphere, beneath a roof in motion and a firmament in flux. Then He split up the highest heavens and filled them with angels of different dispositions: some prostrating and not bowing; others bowing and not standing upright; some arrayed in ranks, not separating from one another; others glorifying, never wearying — not overcome by sleepiness in the eyes, distraction in the mind, fatigue of the body, or the negligence of forgetfulness. Among them are custodians of His Revelation, and conveyors of His Words to His Messengers, and those who come and go with His Decree and His Command. Among them are the guardians of His slaves, and the wardens of the gates of His Gardens. And among them are those whose feet stand firmly on the lowest worlds, and whose necks surpass the highest heaven, and whose sides exceed all bounds, and whose shoulders are proportioned to the pillars of the Throne — their eyes cast down before it, enwrapping themselves beneath it in their wings. Between them and all others there is a barrier: veils of Glory and screens of Might. They do not imagine their Lord through formal representation, nor ascribe to Him created attributes; they do not confine Him within places, nor do they allude to Him through similitudes.


<!-- page 206 -->
Then He—glorified be He!—gathered up a quantity of dust, from some hard earth and some soft, some sweet and some salty, and poured water onto it until it was pure, then kneaded into it moisture until it held together. From this He fashioned a form with curves and joints, limbs and parts, compacting it until it held fast, solidifying it until it became as sounding clay, and assigning to it a measured duration and a known end. Then He breathed into it of His spirit, and it rose up as a human being, endowed with intellectual powers for reflection, thoughts by which to conduct himself, limbs to put to use, instruments at his disposal, and knowledge with which to discern between true and false, between different tastes and fragrances, and between different colours and kinds—a constitution compounded of different hues, unified forms, contrasting oppositions, and distinct admixtures of heat and cold, moisture and dryness.

<!-- page 207 -->
Then God—glorified be He!—demanded of the angels the fulfilment of what He had charged them with, and their compliance with His injunction, by prostrating in submission before Adam and humbling themselves before him. So He said, "Prostrate to Adam," and they prostrated—all except Iblis. Passionate scorn deranged him and wretchedness overwhelmed him; he took pride in his constitution of fire and despised the creature made of clay. Then God granted him a respite, so that His wrath would be fully warranted, the affliction consummated, and the promise fulfilled. So He said, "You are among those given respite until a day whose time is appointed."

Then He—glorified be He!—settled Adam in an abode where He made his life comfortable and his domain secure, and He warned him of Iblis and his enmity. But his enemy beguiled him, driven by envious desire for Adam's immortal dwelling and his intimacy with the righteous. So Adam traded certainty for doubt and resolution for weakness, and replaced joy with dread and self-esteem with remorse. Then God accepted his repentance, enveloped him in a word of His mercy, and promised him a return to His Garden—before sending him down to the abode of tribulations and the begetting of progeny.

<!-- page 208 -->
From Adam's offspring God chose prophets, taking from them a pledge concerning the Revelation and entrusting them with the conveyance of His message—at a time when most of His creatures had strayed from their covenant with God, ignoring His due, setting up rivals alongside Him, while the demons distracted them from knowledge of Him and severed them from worship of Him. So He sent among them His messengers and dispatched prophets in succession, to demand of His creatures the fulfilment of their covenant, to remind them of His forgotten graces, to remonstrate with them through the communication of His Revelation, to unearth for them the buried treasures of the intellect, and to show them signs of His omnipotence: the outspread earth laid low beneath their feet, provisions that give them life, endings that mete out death, ailments hastening their decline, and a succession of events that flows over them.

God never left His creation without a prophet sent forth, a revealed scripture, a compelling proof, or a clear path. These messengers were not weakened by the scarcity of their numbers or by the multitude of those who denied them. Those who came earlier were told the names of those who would follow, and those who came later were informed of those who had gone before. In this way generations were born and ages elapsed, fathers passing away and sons succeeding them, until God sent forth Muhammad, Messenger of God—in fulfilment of His promise and in completion of the cycle of prophecy—he concerning whom a pledge had been taken from the prophets, known through his signs, of noble birth.

<!-- page 209 -->
At that time the people of the earth were divided into different religious communities; caprice was rampant and pathways were in disorder—among them the path of those who likened God to His creation, distorted His name, or pointed to other than Him. So God guided them through His Prophet out of their error and, through his presence, delivered them from their ignorance.

Then God chose for Muhammad the meeting with Him, and desired for him what was with Himself. He removed him with dignity from the abode of this lower world and drew him away from the realm of trial, taking him to Himself in honour. The Prophet left behind among you what the prophets leave for their communities, so that they would not be abandoned to wander without a clear path or a visible beacon: the scripture of your Lord—elucidating what is lawful and unlawful, what is obligatory and what is recommended, the abrogating verses and the abrogated, the conditionally permissible and the unconditionally compulsory, the specific and the general, His admonitions and His analogies, the undelimited and the delimited, the unambiguous and the equivocal—making plain what is condensed and clarifying what is obscure.

<!-- page 210 -->
In it are matters whose knowledge is binding upon God's servants, and others whose ignorance is overlooked. There are injunctions affirmed as obligatory in the scripture yet known to have been abrogated by the Sunna, and actions made obligatory by the Sunna from which the scripture has nonetheless permitted abstention. There are duties incumbent upon a person at their prescribed time that cease to be incumbent once that time has passed. The scripture also draws a distinction between forbidden acts: the major sins, with which He has threatened punishment by His fire, and the minor ones, for which He has prepared His forgiveness. And it describes acts that are acceptable when performed to a modest degree and equally acceptable when performed to a higher degree, without either being obligatory.

<!-- page 211 -->
## The Letter of Ali (ع) to Malik al-Ashtar

This letter was written when the Imam appointed Malik as governor of Egypt and its regions, at a time when the rule of the previous governor, Muhammad b. Abi Bakr, had become unstable. It is the longest letter the Imam wrote and the most comprehensive in beauty of form and excellence of meaning.

In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful.

This is what the servant of God, Ali (ع), Commander of the Faithful, commands Malik b. al-Harith al-Ashtar in his mandate ('ahd) of appointment, upon making him governor of Egypt: to collect its revenues, to fight its enemies, to seek the welfare of its people, and to bring prosperity to its lands.

He commands him to fear God, to give obedience to God precedence over all else, and to abide by what He has commanded in His Book—both obligatory and recommended acts—for no one attains virtue except by following them, and no one becomes wretched except by rejecting and neglecting them. He commands him to assist God with his heart, his hand, and his tongue, for God—majestic is His name—has undertaken to grant victory to whoever assists Him and to exalt whoever glorifies Him. And he commands him to curb the passions of his soul and to restrain it when whim and caprice run high, for the soul incites to evil unless God shows mercy.

<!-- page 212 -->
Know, Malik, that I am sending you to a land that has seen governments before yours—some just and some unjust—and the people will now weigh your conduct just as you once weighed the conduct of the rulers before you. They will speak of you just as you spoke of them; and the righteous are known only through what God causes to flow about them on the tongues of His servants. So let the treasure dearest to you be the treasure of good deeds. Master your desires and hold yourself back from what is not lawful to you, for such restraint is fairness by the soul towards itself, keeping it balanced between what it likes and what it dislikes.

Habituate your heart to mercy, love, and kindness towards your subjects. Do not stand over them like a ravenous beast that considers them fair prey, for they are of two kinds: either your brother in religion or your equal in creation. Mistakes slip from them and faults befall them, whether deliberately or by accident. So extend to them your forgiveness and your pardon, just as you would wish God to extend to you His forgiveness and pardon—for you are above them, and the one in authority over you is above you, and God is above the one who appointed you. He has asked you to manage their affairs, and He is testing you through them.

Do not set your soul on a course of war with God, for you have no power before His retribution and cannot do without His forgiveness and mercy. Never regret a pardon, and never rejoice in a punishment. Do not act rashly on any impulse when you can find a way around it. Do not say, "I have been given authority, so I command and am obeyed," for that breeds corruption in the heart, weakens religion, and draws misfortune near. If the authority you hold breeds pride or vanity in you, look at the greatness of God's dominion above you and at His power over you—power the like of which you do not hold even over your own self. This will calm your ambition, restrain your vehemence, and restore the good sense that had strayed from you. Beware of matching yourself with God in greatness or likening yourself to Him in might, for God humbles every tyrant and disgraces every braggart.

<!-- page 213 -->
Be just with God and be just with people, giving them their due from yourself, from your close relatives, and from those of your subjects for whom you feel fondness—for if you do not, you are an oppressor. And whoever oppresses the servants of God has God, rather than His servants, as his adversary; and whomever God opposes, He refutes his plea, and he remains at war with God until he desists and repents. Nothing so surely hastens the withdrawal of God's grace and the coming of His retribution as persistence in oppression, for God hears the cry of the oppressed and lies in wait for the oppressors.

Let the dearest of your affairs be those most balanced in truth, most comprehensive in justice, and most inclusive of your subjects' contentment—for the discontent of the common folk (al-'amma) cancels out the contentment of the elite (al-khassa), while the discontent of the elite is absorbed within the contentment of the common folk. No one is more of a burden to a ruler in times of ease, less helpful in times of hardship, more averse to fair treatment, more insistent in asking for favours, less grateful when given them, slower to accept an excuse when refused, and weaker in patience under the blows of fortune, than the elite. It is the common folk of the community who are the pillar of religion, the mass of the Muslims, and the defence against enemies—so let your inclination and your leaning be towards them.

<!-- page 214 -->
Let the ones among your subjects who most eagerly hunt out the faults of others be the furthest from you and the most despised in your eyes, for people do have faults, and the ruler is the one most bound to conceal them. Do not expose what is hidden from you; your duty is only to correct what has become plain, and God will judge whatever remains hidden. Veil people's failings as much as you can, and God will veil from your subjects the failings in yourself that you would wish kept concealed. Loosen from the people every knot of resentment, and cut away from your own heart the cord of every enmity. Turn a blind eye to whatever is unclear to you, and do not be quick to believe a slanderer, for a slanderer is a deceiver, however sincerely he may appear to advise.

Do not admit into your counsel a miser, for he will turn you away from generosity and frighten you with poverty; nor a coward, for he will make you feel too weak for your affairs; nor a greedy man, for he will dress up avidity as though it were justice. Miserliness, cowardice, and greed are distinct traits united by a single bad opinion of God. The worst of your ministers is the one who served evil rulers before you and shared in their sins—do not let such men into your inner circle, for they are helpers of sinners and brothers of tyrants. You can find good substitutes for them among men of equal intelligence and capability who carry none of that burden of sin and have never helped a tyrant in his tyranny or a sinner in his sin. Such men will cost you the least, support you the best, incline most warmly towards you, and attach themselves least to anyone but you—so choose them as your intimates, in private and in public.

<!-- page 215 -->
Among these, give the highest place to the one who most readily speaks the bitter truth to you and gives you the least encouragement in whatever you do that God dislikes for His friends, however painfully that strikes at your own desires. Attach yourself to people of piety and truthfulness, and accustom them not to flatter you or to gladden you with praise for something you never did, for excessive praise breeds conceit and carries a person towards vanity. Never place the good and the wicked on the same footing before you, for that would make the good think little of their goodness and the wicked settle deeper into their wickedness; hold each of them to what he has bound himself to. Know that nothing so builds a ruler's confidence in his subjects as his kindness towards them, his easing of their burdens, and his refusal to press them beyond what they can bear. So conduct yourself in a way that earns their trust, for that trust will spare you long trouble; and the one most deserving of your good opinion is the one you have treated well, while the one most deserving of your distrust is the one you have treated badly.

Do not break any good tradition established by the leaders of this community, by which harmony was knit together and the subjects set right; and do not introduce any new practice that harms those already in place—for then the reward for the old traditions will go to the one who founded them, while the burden of breaking them will fall on you. Study much with the scholars (al-'ulama) and hold much discourse with the sages (al-hukama), so as to strengthen what keeps your lands sound and to uphold what those before you had already set right.

<!-- page 216 -->
Know that the subjects are made up of several classes, none of which can be sound without the others, and none of which can do without the rest. Among them are the soldiers of God (junud Allah); the scribes (kuttab, sing. katib) who serve the common people and the elite; the judges who administer justice (qudat al-'adl); the officials who uphold fairness and secure the right of redress (rifq); those who pay the poll-tax (jizya, ⟪ar:جزیۃ⟫) and the land-tax (kharaj, ⟪ar:خَرَج⟫) from among the protected people (ahl al-dhimma) and the Muslims; the merchants and artisans; and the lowest class, the needy and the destitute. To each of these God has assigned its share, and He has fixed its bounds and its duties in His Book or in the Sunna (⟪ar:سنۃ⟫) of His Prophet—God bless him and his family—as a binding covenant ('ahd) from Him, kept safe with us.

As for the soldiers, they are—by the grace of God—the fortresses of the subjects, the adornment of the governors, the strength of religion, and the pathways to security (subul al-amn). The subjects cannot maintain themselves except through the soldiers, and the soldiers in their turn cannot be maintained except through the kharaj—the revenues God brings forth for them—by which they gain strength to wage war on their enemies, on which they depend for their own welfare, and by which they meet their needs. Neither of these two classes can be maintained without the third: the judges, the administrators, and the scribes, who uphold every contract, gather and order all interests and benefits, and are entrusted with the particular and the general affairs of state alike. None of these can function without the merchants and artisans, who gather the goods that are needed, establish the markets, and by their own labour supply what the work of others could not procure. Last comes the lowest class, the needy and the destitute, whose relief and support is an obligation owed to them. For every one of these classes there is plenitude with God, and every one has upon the governor a right measured by what its welfare requires. The governor cannot fully discharge the duties God has laid upon him except through earnest striving and seeking God's help (al-ihtimam wa'l-isti'ana), training himself to hold fast to what is right, and bearing patiently whatever comes his way, be it easy or hard.


<!-- page 314 -->
Appoint as commander of your soldiers the man you feel most deeply to be the most sincere toward God, the Prophet, and your Imam—the purest of heart and the most excellent in forbearance (hilm): one who is slow to anger, glad to pardon, kind to the weak, and firm with the strong; neither driven by violence nor held back by weakness. Hold fast to men of noble descent, from pious families of established name and repute, and to those known for their bravery, their courage, their generosity (al-sakha'), and their tolerance (al-samaha)—for they are a group formed by nobility and a party made of honour. Then supervise their affairs as parents would supervise their child. Let no act by which you strengthen them appear too great in your eyes, and do not belittle any kindness, however slight, that you have promised them, for such kindness is a summoner (da'iya) calling them to give you good counsel and to place their trust in you. Do not neglect the close inspection of their affairs in favour of matters that seem weightier, for there are cases in which a small act of kindness benefits them greatly, and cases in which the weightier matters cannot be set aside.

Let the most preferred of your commanders be the one most benevolent to the people and most generous with his bounty, so that they and their dependants have ample means. Let their concern be gathered into a single resolve: to fight the enemy. Your kindness toward them will cause their hearts to incline toward you.

<!-- page 315 -->
The greatest source of joy for governors is the establishment of justice in the land and the flowing of love from their subjects. Such love will not appear unless the subjects' hearts are secure, and their counsel will not be sound unless the governor oversees those charged with their welfare and ensures that they find the burden of rule light and easy to bear—so that they no longer anxiously hope for his rule to end. Raise their hopes, praise them continually, and recount the brave deeds of those who have been brave, for abundant praise of noble acts rouses the courageous and spurs on the lazy, if God wills. Acknowledge the courageous deed of every man; do not ascribe the deed of one to another, and do not fall short in granting anyone his due. Do not let a man's nobility lead you to reckon a small accomplishment great, and do not let a man's lowliness lead you to reckon a great one small. Refer to God and His Messenger any matter that weighs heavily upon you or is unclear to you, for God the Exalted has said to a folk whom He loved to guide: 'O ye who believe, obey God and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. And if ye dispute with one another over anything, then refer it to God and the Messenger' (Qur'an, 4: 59). To 'refer to God' means to follow what is clear and unequivocal in His Book; to 'refer to the Messenger' means to follow that part of his Sunna which unites rather than that which divides.

<!-- page 316 -->
For the settlement of disputes among the people, choose the one you hold to be the most excellent of your subjects: one whom complex matters do not confound and whom disputants do not enrage; who does not persist in error, and does not shrink from returning to the truth once he perceives it; whose soul is not drawn toward greed; who is not content with a shallow understanding but probes to its depths; who is most circumspect in the face of ambiguities, most firm in holding to the proofs, least wearied by litigants returning again and again, most patient in uncovering the true state of affairs, and most resolute once the right judgement is plain; whom praise does not make vain and whom enticement does not sway. Such people are indeed rare.

Then examine carefully and often the carrying-out of the judge's verdicts, and be generous in what you pay him, so that want is removed and his need to seek help from others is lessened. Give him a rank of nearness to yourself that none of your other close companions might hope to attain, so that he is safe in your presence and shielded from the attacks of other influential men. Scrutinize all of this with a piercing scrutiny, for this religion had been held captive in the hands of evil people, abused by them for their own caprice and exploited by them for the sake of this world.

<!-- page 317 -->
Then look into the affairs of your administrators. Employ them only after testing them, and do not appoint anyone out of partiality or favouritism, for these two are a wellspring of every kind of injustice and treachery. Seek out men of proven experience and modesty, from righteous households and families of precedence in Islam, for they are the most noble in character, the most rooted in dignity, the least inclined toward greed, and the most mindful of how all things end. Then provide them with a generous livelihood, for this gives them the strength to keep themselves in good order, frees them from reaching into the funds in their charge, and stands as an argument against them should they disobey your orders or betray your trust. Examine their actions carefully by appointing over them supervisors known for their sincerity and reliability, for your quiet watching of their affairs will move them to keep faith with their trust and to be gentle with the subjects. Keep watch over your assistants as well: if any one of them stretches out his hand toward a treacherous deed, and the reports of your watchers agree upon it so that you hold it sufficient evidence, then lay corporal punishment upon him, take him to account for what his act has brought about, set him in a place of disgrace, brand him with the charge of treachery, and gird him with the shame of the accusation.

<!-- page 318 -->
Look, too, into the matter of the kharaj land-tax in such a way that those subject to it are kept in a proper state of well-being, for it is from their welfare, and from the sound collection of the tax itself, that the good of all others is drawn. There can be no welfare of the rest except through them, since all without exception depend upon the revenues of the kharaj and upon those who pay it. Let your concern for the cultivation of the land outweigh your concern for collecting the tax, for no tax will be gathered where there is no cultivation. Whoever exacts the tax without cultivating the land ruins the land and destroys the people, and his rule will not endure for long. So if the people complain of a heavy burden, or of some blight, or of the cutting off of irrigation, or of a lack of rain, or of a change in the condition of the soil from flooding or drought, then lighten their burden in the measure that you hope will set their situation right. Do not let any relief you grant them weigh heavily upon you, for it is an investment that will return to you in the prosperity of your land, the adornment of your rule, the finest praise you reap from them, and the pride you take in dispensing justice among them. You may then rely upon an increase in their capacity as a result of what you invested in them when you gave them respite, and you may trust them, having accustomed them to your justice and your kindness.

<!-- page 319 -->
It is possible that a situation might arise in which you depend upon them, and they would then bear this responsibility gladly, for a prosperous land can sustain whatever burden you place upon it. The devastation of the land comes about only through the destitution of its inhabitants, and the destitution of its inhabitants comes about only when the desire to amass wealth rules the souls of the governors, when they have doubts about what endures, and when they profit little from exemplary teachings.

Now consider with care the situation of your scribes. Appoint the best of them to manage your affairs. Assign those letters containing the most sensitive strategies and secrets to the scribe in whom you find the most comprehensive moral qualities: one who will not be emboldened by his elevated status to contradict you in public; one who is not negligent in delivering to you the correspondence of your officials and properly despatching your replies; one who takes and gives on your behalf with care. He must not weaken any agreement contracted for you, nor be incapable of repudiating what has been contracted to your detriment. He must not be ignorant of the value of his own capabilities, for one who is unaware of his own worth will be even more ignorant of the worth of others. Do not make your selection solely on the basis of your own instinct, confidence, and trust, for men know how to present themselves to rulers through affectation and good service that conceals neither sincerity nor fidelity. Rather, test them by what they did in the service of good people who came before you, and appoint the one whose virtue left the deepest impression upon the common people and whose reputation for integrity is most widely known. This will be proof of your sincerity toward God and toward the one on whose behalf you have been appointed to this office, for you will be putting the right man in the right place—and in so doing, enacting justice.

<!-- page 320 -->
Appoint for each of your affairs a head officer among your officials, one who will not be daunted by the magnitude of affairs nor scattered by their multiplicity. Any fault of your scribes which you have overlooked will be ascribed to you.

Then attend to the merchants and artisans, treating them well and urging the same on others—those of fixed abode, those who travel with their goods, and those who earn their livelihood with their hands. They are sources of benefit and the means by which conveniences are obtained. Through them, goods are procured from distant and remote places, brought by land and sea, from plains and mountains, and from places where men neither settle nor even dare to venture. They live in a state of reconciliation (silm), so no calamity need be feared from them, and in a state of peace (sulh), so no disturbance need be expected. Examine their affairs attentively, both those close to you and those in every corner of your land. Yet be aware that, despite what has been said, many of them harbour a despicable stinginess, a repugnant greed, a propensity to hoard goods, and a tendency to fix prices arbitrarily. All of this brings loss to the people and is a source of shame for governors. So prohibit hoarding, for the Messenger of God—God bless him and his family—prohibited it. Let trading be carried out with propriety and fairness, at prices that harm neither party, buyer nor seller. If any one of them succumbs to the temptation to hoard after you have prohibited it, inflict upon him an exemplary—but not excessive—punishment.

<!-- page 321 -->
Then—O God, O God!—pay particular attention to the lowest class: those who have no wherewithal, the destitute, the needy, the afflicted, the disabled. Within this class are those who beg and those whose wretchedness calls out to be alleviated but who do not beg. Be mindful of God in regard to their rights, for He has entrusted those rights to your care. Assign to them a portion from your public treasury (bayt al-mal) and a portion of the produce of what is taken as booty by the Muslims in every region, for those who are furthest away have the same rights as those nearest. Upholding the right of each of them is incumbent upon you. Do not let any haughtiness on your part cause you to neglect them, for you will not be pardoned even the slightest shortcoming toward them on account of attending to some seemingly more important matter. Do not turn your concern away from them or assume a contemptuous attitude toward them. Keep a watchful eye over the affairs of those who cannot gain access to you and who are disdained by men of high standing. Appoint from among those you trust a God-fearing and humble person to bring their affairs to your attention. Treat these people in a manner such that God may excuse you on the day you meet Him, for they are more in need of justice from you than any others among your subjects. Offer your excuse to God in respect of fulfilling each one's right. Assume responsibility for the orphans and the elderly who have no resources and yet cannot bring themselves to beg. This is onerous for governors, and the fulfilment of all rights is onerous—but God makes it light for those who aspire to the Hereafter, who restrain their souls in patience, and trust in the truth of what God has promised them.

<!-- page 322 -->
Apportion a part of your time to those who have special needs, making yourself free to attend to them personally, sitting with them in a public assembly in all due humility before God, your Creator. Keep your soldiers, guards, and officers away from them so that they may speak to you without inhibition, for I heard the Messenger of God say—God bless him and his family—on more than one occasion: 'A nation in which the rights of the weak are not wrested in an uninhibited manner from the strong will never be blessed.' Bear patiently any coarseness or inarticulate expression they might show; do not display irritation or disdain toward them—God will thereby extend His compassion to you in its fullest measure and make the reward for your obedience to Him binding. Give whatever you give with beneficence, and withhold, if this be unavoidable, with grace and apology.

There are certain affairs which you must handle personally—among them, replying to your officers when your scribes are incapable of doing so, and issuing responses to the requests of people when they are brought to you in person because your aides find themselves at a loss on their account.

<!-- page 323 -->
Perform each day the task proper to it, for to each day belongs a particular task. Set apart the finest portion of your available time—and the greatest share thereof—for your soul and for what is between you and God, even though all times and all actions within them are for God, provided the intention underlying them is good and your subjects derive security as a consequence. Let your observance of those duties (fara'id, sing. farida) relating exclusively to God be the special means by which you purify your religion for Him. Give unto God of your vital energy in your nights and your days, and perform fully that by which you draw near to God—doing so perfectly, without becoming dull or deficient, taking your body to its limits.

When you lead the people in prayer, do so without repelling them or squandering it, for among them are people with infirmities or special needs. Indeed, I asked the Messenger of God—God bless him and his family—when he sent me as his representative to Yemen, 'How should I lead them in prayer?' He replied: 'Lead them according to the prayer performed by the weakest among them, and be merciful to the believers.'

<!-- page 324 -->
In addition to all of this, do not prolong any period of absence from your subjects, for the isolation of governors from those they govern creates a kind of constriction and breeds deficiency in awareness of their affairs. Such isolation cuts rulers off from acquiring knowledge of things hidden from them, so that what is great comes to appear small and what is small, great; the beautiful appears ugly and the ugly, beautiful; the true becomes mixed with the false. The governor is but a human being: he cannot know what people conceal from him, and truth bears no visible mark by which sincere expressions may be distinguished from false ones.

You can only be one of two kinds of men: either a man who gives himself generously for the sake of the truth—in which case, why seclude yourself, thereby preventing yourself from bestowing a necessary right or performing an honourable deed? Or else you are a man afflicted with an unwillingness to give of himself—in which case, how quickly people will refrain from making requests of you, despairing of your generosity! And this, despite the fact that fulfilling most of the needs people bring to you is not burdensome, whether they be complaints against injustice or demands for fairness in transactions.


<!-- page N -->

The governor has favourites and intimate companions, and among them some are prone to presumptuousness, arrogance, and unfairness in their dealings. Cut this out at the root by eliminating the causes of these vices, and by granting no landed estates to anyone in your entourage or circle of friends. Give them no reason to hope for such a grant; should they receive one, it would harm those living nearby in their access to water and in any shared undertaking, the burden of which would inevitably fall on the neighbours while the profit went to those holding the estate—not to you. What would fall to you is only the blame ('ayb) for it, in this world and the next. Impose what is right upon all to whom it applies, whether they are close to you or distant. Be steadfast and vigilant in this, regardless of how it weighs upon your intimates and favourites. Always seek the end that this principle leads to, however heavily the path may lie upon you, for its outcome is praiseworthy.

If any of your subjects suspect you of an injustice, present your case openly so that the suspicion is cleared away from you by plain explanation. Such conduct is a discipline for yourself, a kindness to your subjects, and a means of pleading your cause that will help you keep them upright in adherence to the truth.

Never turn away from a call to peace with your enemy when God's pleasure lies in it, for in peace there is rest for your soldiers, relief from your cares, and security for your lands. Yet once you have made peace, remain fully on your guard against your enemy, for he may have drawn close only in order to catch you off guard; so take every precaution, and hold even your own favourable opinion of him under suspicion. Yet if you and your enemy enter into a solemn agreement, or he receives from you a covenant of protection (dhimma), then keep your agreement faithfully and honour your obligation of protection with complete trustworthiness. Make your very life a shield for what you have promised, for among all the obligations God has laid down there is none on which people stand more firmly united—despite all the differences of their inclinations and the variety of their opinions—than the honouring of pledges. Even the polytheists observed it among themselves, quite apart from their dealings with the Muslims, so greatly did they fear the consequences of treachery. Do not betray your covenant of protection, do not break your promise, and do not deal treacherously with your enemy, for none dares defy God but the ignorant and wretched (jahil shaqiyy). God has made His pledge and His protection a sanctuary of security, which by His mercy He has spread over His servants—a refuge in whose inviolable strength they find rest, and toward whose sheltering power they hasten. Let there be no corruption within it, no treachery, and no deception. Do not enter into any agreement that leaves room for evasion, and do not resort to twisting words once an agreement has been confirmed and made binding. Do not let any hardship that befalls you drive you to break, without right, an agreement God has made binding upon you; for patience under a difficulty whose relief and good outcome you await is far better than a treachery whose consequence you dread—that a reckoning from God (tilba) should close in upon you from which you will find no release in this world or the next.

Beware of shedding blood unlawfully, for nothing so swiftly invites retribution, weighs more heavily in its consequence, or is more apt to end a blessing and cut short a span of life, than bloodshed without right. On the Day of Resurrection, God—glorified be He—will begin His judgement among His servants with the blood they have shed. Do not seek to strengthen your authority through unlawful bloodshed, for that in truth weakens and undermines it—indeed, it brings it down and takes it away entirely.

You have no excuse before God, nor before me, if you kill anyone deliberately, for that demands capital punishment. And if you are overtaken by an error, and your whip, your sword, or your hand goes too far in inflicting a penalty—for even a single blow, and whatever exceeds it, can bring about death—do not let the pride of your authority hold you back from paying the relatives of the slain what is rightfully theirs.

Beware of self-admiration, of leaning on whatever pleases you in yourself, and of loving to be flattered, for these are among the surest openings Satan finds to efface the good works (ihsan) of the virtuous (al-muhsinin). Beware of making your subjects feel indebted to you for the good you do them, of inflating your own deeds, and of promising what you then fail to fulfil; for laying people under obligation destroys the good itself, self-praise extinguishes the light of truth, and breaking a promise earns you the hatred of God and of men. God the Exalted has said: كَبُرَ مَقْتًا عِندَ ٱللَّهِ أَن تَقُولُوا۟ مَا لَا تَفْعَلُونَ — 'It is indeed hateful to God that you say that which you do not do' (61:3). Beware of rushing into matters before their time has come, and of letting them slip when the moment to act has arrived; of stubbornness when they grow tangled, and of slackness once they have become clear. Put every matter in its proper place, and attend to every task at its proper time. Beware of taking for yourself what belongs equally to all, and of feigning blindness to what is urgent and plain to every eye, for it will be taken from you for the benefit of others. Before long the coverings will be lifted from all affairs, and justice will be sought from you on behalf of the wronged. So master the heat of your pride, the sharpness of your temper, the force of your hand, and the edge of your tongue; guard against all of this by holding back sudden impulse and delaying the blow until your anger has subsided and you have regained your self-command. You will never achieve this mastery over yourself, however, unless you increase your remembrance of your return to your Lord.

It is incumbent upon you to remember the just governance and noble custom that have come before you, the practice of the Prophet—God bless him and his family—and the obligations set forth in the Book of God. Follow faithfully what you have seen us do in these matters, and strive with all your soul to act upon what I have enjoined upon you in this mandate, by which I have made my plea complete against you, so that you will have no excuse when your soul races toward its caprice.

I ask God, by the vastness of His mercy and the greatness of His power to grant every desire, that He bestow upon me and upon you the success that wins His pleasure—that we may present a clear justification before Him and before His creatures, earn the fairest praise among His servants, and leave behind the most beautiful traces in the land, with blessing made complete and honour ever increased; and that He seal my life and yours with felicity (al-sa'ada) and martyrdom (al-shahada). Truly unto Him we are returning (2:156).

And peace be upon the Messenger of God—God bless him and his good and pure progeny.
